Exposition on the Seven Visions of the Book of Revelation

Translated by John Litteral from the Latin text of the Patrologia Latina, vol. 17 (among the works spuriously attributed to Ambrose); released into the public domain. Square brackets give the Latin underlying a rendering. Notes are the translator’s.

Translator’s Introduction

Authorship

The name of the author of this commentary on Revelation is Berengaudus. The commentary is referred to as the Exposition on the Seven Visions of the Book of Revelation, because of the specific way Berengaudus chose to structurally divide and analyze the text of the Book of Revelation, dividing it up into sections that he calls “seven visions”. Many scholars make the argument about the authorship based upon two letters where Lupus of Ferrières mentions a young monk who went by the name Berengaudus and was from the abbey of Saint-Germain at Auxerre. This would mean that the author lived in the Carolingian period in the ninth century. In the commentary itself, the author did not give his name overtly, though he didn’t seem to try to hide his name, because he gives clues by way of a riddle, saying…

Whoever wishes to know the author’s name should attend to the first letters of the explanations in the headings of the seven Visions. The number formed by the four missing vowels, if you supply the Greek ones, is eighty-one.

In the introduction to the commentary in the Patrologia Latina (PL 17, prefatory admonition to the Expositio), it explains the riddle,

The author of this book is called Berengaudus, as can be seen if one carefully observes how he himself teaches that his name may be discovered. For the first letters of the Expositions in the seven chapters of the Visions, which he bids us pay attention to, are these: B, R, N, G, V, D, S. Now the four Greek vowels that are to be joined to these, expressing the number 81, are these: short E, 5; likewise short E, 5; A, 1; and O, 70. Join together therefore the number that each of these vowels contains, and you will without doubt have 81, for this number will not appear to you in any entirely different vowels, unless, as noted above, you take E twice. Compare therefore these four vowels with the seven consonants mentioned above, and form syllables from them in this way: Berengaudos. You have then Berengaudos. But this is a Greek ending; the Latin ending, however, is Berengaudus. For what among the Greeks ends in -os, among the Latins ends in -us, as Petros is Petrus, Paulos is Paulus. But if you prefer, in discovering this name, to keep the order of the aforesaid vowels, and by putting A first arrange the others among the consonants so that you say Barenguedos, the prescribed principle regarding the vowels does not indeed oppose you in this; but from it there is formed a man’s name that is far too barbarous and in every way unusual. It is therefore better and much more correct that, as has been prescribed, he be called Berengaudus, not Barenguedus, because the former is customary and more elegant, while the latter seems an unusual and by no means elegant name for a man.

Yet, the date of this commentary has not been completely settled among scholars. Some scholars argue that this commentary reads like an 11th or 12th century commentary on the Apocalypse. They also argue for a later date because the oldest existing manuscript of this commentary dates only from about 1060. As for the age of the existing manuscripts of the work, it seems like a weak argument considering that it is very common that the earliest existing manuscripts of a work of antiquity come from copies that are a century or two (or even later) from the date of the autograph. For example, Ambrose Autpert lived between 730–784, and the oldest existing manuscripts of his commentary on Revelation come a hundred years after he died.1 Haimo of Auxerre (802–855), whose writings Berengaudus utilized, was a contemporary of Berengaudus and also wrote a commentary on Revelation. The oldest manuscript of that work dates primarily from the 12th century (with some scholarly discussion tracing related fragments or traditions slightly earlier)2.

In some printed editions, this commentary was listed among the spurious works of Ambrose of Milan, though that is easily recognized as being wrong regarding an Ambrosian authorship. The style of writing is far different than that of Ambrose. The author of this commentary was ignorant of Greek, yet Ambrose was not. Also, in chapter seventeen of this commentary the author mentioned the Lombards as if their power had been wiped out, which means it had to have been written after 774AD. Likewise, the author of this commentary speaks of Gregory the Great and Jerome as already counted among the saints of the Church. This author also frequently employs testimonies from the Creed of Athanasius, as though it were a rule of faith already commonly recited throughout the Christian world.

Sources

Commentaries by Carolingian expositors, in many ways, are similar to catenae, which are compilations of comments by Patristic writers that are pieced together to form a running commentary. Greek Catenae were very popular in the Eastern Church, but the West also used the same style later, especially during the Carolingian period and after. Berengaudus used a style in his commentary that is smooth and to some extent original because he presented ideas and explanations very loosely instead of presenting them as a word-for-word patchwork of earlier commentaries like some of his contemporaries. This is one reason why some scholars support a later date than ninth century.

Berengaudus had a great admiration for the Early Church Fathers and their importance to protecting the Church against heretics. He says in comments on Rev. 13:3…

For in the earliest times of the Gospel there were many heretics who tried to undermine the faith of the Church by their errors; but almighty God chose as defenders of the Church the orthodox Fathers from among the children of that same Church, and, filling them with the river of his wisdom, through their teaching he rooted out every heresy from his Church. Such were Ambrose, Hilary, Jerome, Augustine, Gregory, and many others who also published a very great number of books, by which all heresies that existed and that could exist were destroyed.

Berengaudus also refers to them in Rev. 21:20 as being instructors of the faith and light…

Chrysoprase is said to be a stone of green and golden color. Because it is placed in the tenth place, we may understand it to represent certain teachers of the Church, who taught many by their words while present in the body, and instructed, and continue to instruct, many more through their writings, such as Jerome, Augustine, Gregory, and many others. They may rightly be called the lights of the Church, represented by the green and golden colors: they were unwavering in the greenness of faith and radiant with wisdom like gold.

Of all the Fathers of the Church, Gregory the Great is mentioned or quoted over a dozen times by Berengaudus, especially from Gregory’s work on the Homilies on the Gospels. He also mentions and quotes Jerome, especially from his commentaries on Daniel and Matthew, and perhaps Jerome’s Recension of the commentary by Victorinus on Revelation. He also quotes the Athanasian Creed four times throughout the commentary. He frequently inserts passages taken word for word from the Rule of blessed Benedict (written around 530 AD).

The Commentary

The Seven Seals, Trumpets, and Bowls

Berengaudus divides the entire commentary on Revelation into seven visions. First vision (Rev. 1-3), second vision (Rev. 4), third vision (Rev. 5:1 - 8:5), fourth vision (Rev. 8:6 - 15:4), fifth vision (Rev. 15:5 - 20:10), sixth vision (Rev. 20:11 - 21:8), seventh vision (Rev. 21:9 - end). For Berengaudus says in his comments for Rev. 1:4a…

And if you observe carefully, this whole book is divided into seven visions. The first is that which contains the seven letters sent to the seven Churches, (Rev. 1-3) and the other things contained there. The second is that in which he saw a throne set in heaven as a figure of the Church, and above the throne Christ sitting. (Rev. 4) In the third the Lamb opened the book and its seals. (Rev. 5:1 - 8:5) In the fourth, seven angels were seen sounding trumpets. (Rev. 8:6 - 15:4) In the fifth, again seven angels were seen holding bowls. (Rev. 15-16) In the sixth, the resurrection is described. (Rev. 20:11 - 21:8) In the seventh, the glory of the saints is shown in the figure of the city Jerusalem descending from heaven. (Rev. 21:9 - end)

To Berengaudus, the seven seals, seven trumpets, and the seven bowls represent the various periods of the world, from Adam to the end of the world. Each one of these visions from the third through fifth vision recapitulate and overlap, where Berengaudus begins with the seven seals by explaining what each one represents, and then with the seven trumpets and seven bowls he shows how they go into greater detail concerning those periods of time. Concerning the seven seals Berengaudus says…

The opening of the first seal, then, refers to the things that were done before the Flood; the second seal to the patriarchs; the third to those who were under the Law; the fourth to the prophets; and the three openings that remain refer to the New Testament. The fifth opening refers to the martyrs; the sixth to the casting down of the Jews and the calling of the Gentiles; the seventh refers to the coming of Christ.

As for the seven trumpets, Berengaudus understood the seven angels sounding the trumpets to symbolize the teachers of the people of God throughout the different periods of time as listed in the seven seals, though not exactly in the same order. He understood the sounding of the trumpets to signify their preaching. The order and details of the seven bowls are in line with those of the trumpets that came before. For Berengaudus says…

The fifth vision, for the most part agrees with the preceding vision, about whose understanding we have so far said what the grace of the Lord has consented to bestow (in chapter 8). For just as there seven angels were seen, to whom seven trumpets were given, so here likewise it is related that seven bowls were given to seven angels; and just as, at the sounding of the trumpet of the first angel, great hail struck the earth, so also, at the pouring out of the bowl of the first angel, men, who are often signified by “earth”, are said to have been struck with very grievous wounds. And just as, at the sounding of the trumpet of the second angel, a third part of the sea was turned into blood, so likewise, at the pouring out of the bowl of the second angel, the whole sea is described as having been turned into blood. The rest follow the same meaning; but how these agree with the former, we shall say in what follows. Therefore these have the same significance as those: they signify, namely, the preachers who were from the beginning and will be even to the end. But they are set forth twice because, as we have shown above, the things that were seen to have been done under the sounding of the trumpets of the first, second, third, and fourth angels refer to the elect. But the things that are reported to have been done under the sounding of the trumpets of the fifth, sixth, and seventh angels, to which also three woes are joined, wherein the persecutions of the Church are described, which she has suffered from heretics and pagans, and will suffer from Antichrist at the end of the world, refer on one side to the wicked, on the other to the elect; because the ungodly, from the evil which they inflict upon the righteous, acquire everlasting punishment, but the righteous, from the evil which they endure, gain for themselves everlasting glory. Therefore all the things that are described under the pouring out of the bowls of the seven angels refer to the wicked; therefore those first are said to have sounded the trumpets, by which the army of God was called to battle; but the following are reported to have poured out bowls full of the wrath of God upon the ungodly.

Purgatorial Fire

Another interesting aspect of this commentary by Berengaudus is his belief in a variation of Purgatory, which he refers to as “purgatorial fire” [ignem purgatorium], which he talks about in four different places (14:3, 14:5, 20:6, and 21:14) in the commentary. For he says in his comments of 21:14…

And because such ways of life cannot be free from frequent failings, he (Paul on 1 Cor. 3:15) says that such people will be saved through fire, that is, through purgatorial fire. This will happen if they abstain from grave sins and, by giving generously in alms, merit the possibility of being saved after death through purgatorial fire. For just as gold made impure by admixtures is purified by fire, so these people are cleansed by fire of the impurities they contracted through worldly deeds, so that they may become worthy of being joined to the company of the elect.

In his comments on Rev. 20:6 Berengaudus says…

For only those are and will be blessed who, before the end of the world, are deemed worthy to be joined among the company of the saints. For when the end of the world draws near, purgatorial fire will burn many as it purges them; but before the Judgment, once absolved, they will be received into the company of the saints.

And his comments on Rev. 14:3…

We have also seen certain persons, responsible for some sins, preserve bodily virginity until death, whom we believe will be purified by purgatorial fire, so that they may someday deserve to be numbered among the righteous.

As mentioned above, Berengaudus utilized the works of Haimo of Auxerre. Though Haimo did not use the term “purgatorial fire” [ignem purgatorium] in his commentary on Revelation, he did so in other places among his written works. Haimo says in his work called On the Variety of Books3 [De varietate librorum]…

That those who are saved through purgatorial fire will not all endure the torment of the spirit for the same length of time. Just as not all the wicked who are to be plunged into eternal fire are condemned to one and the same degree of punishment, so all who are believed to be saved through purgatorial penalties will not endure the torment of the spirit for the same length of time. Therefore, what is done among the wicked through distinctions in punishment will also be done among those who are to be saved through fire. The punishment of purgatorial fire will be shorter or longer for them in proportion to how little or how much they loved temporal goods in this life.

Haimo also gives more details concerning the “purgatorial fire” [igne purgatorio] in his commentary4 on 1 Corinthians 3:15,

If anyone’s work is burned up in the fire because he has had minor sins, which will be consumed like stubble, he will suffer loss, that is, he will endure punishments while they begin to be tested and consumed. Yet he himself will be saved, as what follows makes clear: “He himself will be saved, but only as through fire,” that is, passing through it, purified and cleansed by the fire. For in this life, too, the elect are tested by adversity and infirmity, like gold and silver in the fire, as were the three young men, the holy martyrs, and blessed Job. Those who accumulate minor sins are cleansed of them by the trials that burn them in this age, or after death in purgatorial fire, or through some other affliction; they are also cleansed by people’s prayers. Yet what they did not possess without unlawful love, they will certainly not lose without burning grief. Those who heap up lead for themselves often begin to be tormented in this age and to suffer in part what they will suffer more fully in the future.

As we can see, Berengaudus held a similar view as Haimo concerning purgatorial fire. Haimo drew heavily upon Julian of Toledo who came two centuries before him, particularly when dealing with eschatology, Biblical exegesis, and the afterlife, and therefore also articulating these things in an eclectic manner from those like Augustine and Gregory the Great.

The Mark of the Beast 666

Another interesting feature about this commentary is that Berengaudus does not try to calculate the number of the name of the beast coming out of the sea, whom he understands to be the Antichrist. He apparently was not impressed by earlier attempts, therefore not even giving any possible calculations himself, nor repeating what others have done. Berengaudus says in his comments on 13:18…

Many have said numerous things about this number, and have discovered several names in whose letters this number is found. Yet they have not been able to foresee whether Antichrist will possess any of these names; and concerning a matter so uncertain I dare to define nothing. For who knows whether the name that will be provided to him by his parents will have this number?

Berengaudus chose not to provide any possible calculations even though Haimo of Auxerre gave names like Antemos, Arnume, Teitan, DICLVX, and Genserikos. Haimo then concludes by saying: “Therefore the number of the beast, computed by understanding, six hundred and sixty-six, rightly encompasses the whole span of the present life and reveals both the wickedness of the Devil’s deception and the final end of the age.”5

The Woman Clothed with the Sun

In chapter twelve of Revelation where the vision of the woman clothed with the sun, Berengaudus understood this woman to primarily be the Church, which is a common interpretation. But he also gives a secondary interpretation, but he waits until he reaches Revelation 12:4b before he offers that analysis. Berengaudus says…

In this place we can also understand the blessed Mary by the woman, because she is the mother of the Church, since she gave birth to him who is the head of the Church; and she is the daughter of the Church, because she is the greatest member of the Church. Therefore the dragon stood before the woman, so that when she had given birth, he might devour her child; because at the very beginning of Christ’s birth he wished to kill him through Herod, his servant (Matt. 2:13). He also stands before the woman, that is, the Church, so that those whom she brings forth for God through baptism he may destroy by evil persuasion.

Perhaps Berengaudus is influenced by Haimo of Auxerre who interprets the woman as the Church, but he also applies Mary to the passage in a similar way; but Haimo is more upfront about it in the way he articulates it, therefore saying…

This is the sign about which Ahaz was urged to ask God for a sign, either in the depths of hell [inferni] or high above. (Isa. 7:11) But when he said, “I will not ask, and I will not put the Lord to the test,” (Isa. 7:12) the prophet turned to the house of David and said: “Therefore the Lord says: Behold, I will give you a sign: Behold, a virgin shall conceive and bear a son.” (Isa. 7:14) It was indeed a great sign when a virgin conceived and gave birth, and remained a virgin after giving birth. And this happened in heaven, that is, in the Church, of which the Mother of the Lord was also a member. A woman clothed with the sun, that is, surrounded by the divinity of the Most High, as the angel said: “The Holy Spirit will come upon you, and the power of the Most High will overshadow you” (Luke 1:35). That is the power of which Paul says that Christ is the power of God and the wisdom of God. (1 Cor. 1:24) The blessed Mother of God herself, in this passage, represents the Church. For not everything narrated here can apply specifically to the Blessed Virgin according to the literal sense; rather, according to the mystical account, it applies generally to the Church of the elect, in which this sign takes place every day, because Christ is conceived and born in her every day.6

The Thousand-Year Reign

Berengaudus rejected the literal hypothesis that the Devil is completely powerless or confined in a future physical pit. Instead, he followed the tradition influenced by Augustine of Hippo and Haimo of Auxerre. Berengaudus says in his comments on Rev. 20:2…

Christ seized the ancient serpent, that is, the Devil, when by his death he destroyed his dominion. By the thousand years, however, we should understand the whole period from Christ’s coming until the end of the world. Christ therefore bound the Devil not with physical chains, but with the chains of his immense power: casting him out of the hearts of his chosen ones, he keeps him, by his divine might, from deceiving them.

He understood this period as the period of the first resurrection, that is “the period from Christ’s Ascension until the end of the world”. Therefore, Berengaudus says in his comments on Revelation 20:4b…

But we must ask how the elect who are to be born as the end of the world approaches are said to reign with Christ for a thousand years, since the thousand-year period refers not to the future, which is without end, but rather to the present, which will be brought to its completion at the end of the world, and during which the faith of the Gospel flourishes throughout the whole world, directing the chosen daily to achieve eternal life. I think this should be resolved as follows. The period from Christ’s Ascension until the end of the world, which is signified by a thousand years, has been given to all the chosen, so that during it they may enjoy heavenly glory with the Lord without their bodies. We know that all the chosen are members of Christ, and that many members form one body; and if one member rejoices, all the members rejoice with it. Therefore the joy of the saints who came before will also belong to those who are to be born at the end of the world, because they will form one body with them and, through the others, possess what they themselves lack.

Haimo of Auxerre has a very similar interpretation, though just very minor differences. Haimo says the thousand years begins at the Passion of the Lord, while Berengaudus says it begins from Christ’s coming (Rev. 20:2) or from the Ascension (Rev. 20:4). Haimo says…

Therefore, because of its completeness, this number (one thousand) signifies the whole present age, from the Passion of the Lord, that is, until the end of the world. Here the whole is used for a part, because a thousand years represent whatever span of the Church’s time extends up to the reign of Antichrist. During this period the Devil is bound in the abyss, that is, in the hearts of unbelievers and wicked people, where he lies hidden and reigns until the coming of Antichrist. Then he will be released from his prison and will proceed to open blasphemies and persecutions.

Final Thoughts

Probably written during the ninth-century Carolingian period, the commentary became widely circulated in medieval England around 1100. It was copied into richly illuminated manuscripts, including the famous Douce Apocalypse. Later readers encountered its metaphors while Christians were developing ideas about Purgatory’s physical geography. As a result, imagery that had been only “proto-purgatorial” was reinterpreted as concrete descriptions of a distinct third place in the afterlife.

The translation of this commentary comes from the Latin text provided in the printed version of the Patrologia Latina, volume 17. This is the version that provides the spurious works attributed to Ambrose of Milan. As discussed above, the commentary had been wrongly attributed to Ambrose of Milan in some printed editions.

On the First Vision

It is certain that the blessed John, apostle and evangelist, published this book, the Apocalypse; although there have been some who said that it was composed not by him but by another. However, the following points clearly show that it was his.

Chapter 1

Revelation 1:1a The Apocalypse of Jesus Christ, which God gave to him.

Apocalypse [Apocalypsis] is interpreted as revelation [revelatio] or manifestation [manifestatio]. This gift of revelation both the Father gave to the Son, since he was man, and the Son himself gave to himself, namely, the divinity to the humanity which He assumed.

Revelation 1:1b To make known to his servants what must happen soon.

Since this book recounts not only future things, but also present and past ones, why did he say here that our Lord Jesus Christ would reveal to his servants only future things? Because present things are easily known by sight, and past things by hearing; but future things can be known only either through the teaching of the divine Scriptures or through revelation.

Revelation 1:1c And he signified it, sending through his angel to his servant John.

In this passage, such a meaning seems to me to be present: the Lord Jesus sent his angel, and through that same angel he made known to his servant John the things that must happen soon.

Revelation 1:2a Who bore witness to the Word of God.

This passage clearly shows that this book was composed not by some other John, but by the one who wrote the Gospel. He also, at the supper, reclined on the breast of the Lord (Jn. 13:25), in which all the treasures of wisdom and knowledge were hidden (Col. 2:3), and from whom he deserved to draw an immense fountain of wisdom. Therefore he bore witness to the Word of God when he said: In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was with God, and the Word was God (Jn. 1:1). But in what follows, a question arises:

Revelation 1:2b And the testimony of Jesus Christ, whatever he saw.

How then did he bear witness to Jesus Christ, whatever he saw, since he himself says in the same Gospel: Now Jesus did many other signs in the presence of his disciples, which are not written in this book (Jn. 20:30)? But he bore witness to Jesus Christ, whatever he saw, namely by preaching to the nations all that he had learned from Christ, and by recounting the miracles he had seen, writing down only those things which he foresaw would be of greater benefit to future generations.

Revelation 1:3a Blessed are those who read, and those who hear the words of this prophecy, and keep the things that are written in it.

He sets out three kinds of blessedness, but two are nothing if the third is missing; for it does no good to those who read or hear the commandments of God, if they do not keep what they read or hear. For there are many who either hear or read the commandments of God, but it helps them not at all, because though they are present in body, they are absent in mind; for their mind is devoted to acquiring possessions, or is driven by lust, or passes judgment on their neighbor, or is proud against their superior, or imagines that it will become powerful, or is stretched out after other most vain things. What the prophet says can apply to such people: “You will hear with your ears and not understand; and seeing you will see and not perceive” (Isa. 6:9). There are also some who read and hear the divine Scriptures, that is, they adapt their mind to understanding them; but they learn the divine Scriptures for this reason, that they may be considered wise and outstanding among men, and may flourish in the prosperities of this world, not that they may correct a depraved life. But they are not blessed, because they learned the divine Scripture not for the love of God, but for the love of the world. Of such the prophet says: “They are wise to do evil, but to do good they have no knowledge” (Jer. 4:22). And the Lord in the Gospel, in the parable of the sower, speaks, saying: “What fell among thorns, these are they who have heard, and as they go, they are choked by the cares and pleasures of life, and they do not bear fruit” (Luke 8:14). Therefore only those are blessed who for this reason strive to learn the commandments of God: that they may hear, that is, store them up in the mind; they store them up in the mind so that they may fulfill them in deed; they fulfill them in deed so that they may obtain eternal blessedness.

Revelation 1:3b For the time is near.

That is, the time of judgment; for if you set the time from the coming of the Lord to the end of the world before your eyes, you will find it long; but in comparison with the former ages, it is short.

Revelation 1:4a John to the seven Churches that are in Asia: grace to you and peace from him who is, who was, and who is to come.

By the seven Churches, one Catholic Church is indicated, on account of the seven gifts of the Holy Spirit, with which, enlightened, it shines forth in the world. Asia is interpreted as “exalted,” because among the exalted of this age Christ found the multitude of the faithful, from whom he built his Church. And notice that this whole book is arranged according to the number seven: as there are seven Churches to which these writings are directed, so there are seven lampstands, seven stars, seven lamps, seven horns of the Lamb, seven eyes, seven seals, seven angels sounding trumpets, seven thunders, likewise seven angels holding bowls. For even the Dragon, who signifies the Devil, and the beast, who signifies Antichrist, both are seen to have had seven heads; (Rev. 13) likewise the beast on which the harlot woman was seen sitting; (Rev. 17) and if you observe carefully, this whole book is divided into seven visions. The first is that which contains the seven letters sent to the seven Churches, (Rev. 2-3) and the other things contained there. The second is that in which he saw a throne set in heaven as a figure of the Church, and above the throne Christ sitting. (Rev. 4) In the third the Lamb opened the book and its seals. (Rev. 6) In the fourth, seven angels were seen sounding trumpets. (Rev. 8-11) In the fifth, again seven angels were seen holding bowls. (Rev. 15-16) In the sixth, the resurrection is described. (Rev. 20) In the seventh, the glory of the saints is shown in the figure of the city Jerusalem descending from heaven. (Rev. 21)

Revelation 1:4b-5a Grace to you and peace from him who was, and who is, and who is to come, and from the seven spirits who are before his throne; and from Jesus Christ, who is the faithful witness.

Here the whole Trinity is shown; for by him who was, and who is, and who is to come, the Father is indicated. For he was before the foundation of the world without any beginning; he is now, after the foundation of the world, ruling and governing all things that he created; he is to come after the end of the world with the Son and the Holy Spirit, so that he may reign with his saints for ever and ever. By the seven spirits, moreover, the Holy Spirit is understood, because he is sevenfold. And from Jesus Christ, who is the faithful witness. He can truly be called the faithful witness, who does not know how to lie; who will without doubt fulfil what he has mercifully promised to his elect; who does not leave without reward even the one who has given a cup of cold water to the needy (Matt. 10:42); whose words remain so firmly fixed that it is easier for heaven and earth to be reduced to nothing than for anything to be changed from all the things that he foretold he would do.

Revelation 1:5b The firstborn of the dead and the ruler of the kings of the earth.

How is Christ called the firstborn of the dead, since he was born at the end of times, and an innumerable multitude of human beings preceded his coming? But “firstborn” is used for “first,” just as we call “the first” among the people those who surpass the others not in age, but in honor and dignity. And the dead themselves are those of whom the Apostle speaks, saying: For you have died, and your life is hidden with Christ in God (Col. 3:3). Therefore Christ is the firstborn of the dead, because he is the first and the head of all his elect. The ruler of the kings of the earth. “King” is so called from “ruling”: by “kings,” therefore, the apostles and the other saints are indicated, who know how to rule both themselves and others; by “earth,” moreover, the Church is indicated.

Revelation 1:5c Who loved us and washed us from our sins in his blood.

Therefore, because he loved, he washed; and what this washing has brought us, the following words make clear.

Revelation 1:6 And he has made us a kingdom and priests to God and the Father.

The kingdom of God is called the Church of God: therefore Christ God made the kingdom of God out of human beings, when he appointed them to be such that he himself, together with the Father and the Holy Spirit, would consent to dwell and reign in them. For those who are signified by “kingdom” are the same as those signified by “priests”; therefore the chosen are called priests of God, because they are members of the supreme Priest, of whom the Psalmist speaks, saying: You are a priest forever according to the order of Melchizedek (Ps. 109:4). The chosen of God can also be called priests of God in another sense, because they daily offer themselves to God through sorrow of heart and prayers and other good works, as the Psalmist says: The sacrifice to God is a repentant spirit (Ps. 50:19). There is also the sacrifice of praise, which holy Church is accustomed to offer to God every day, about which the same prophet speaks, saying: Offer to God a sacrifice of praise (Ps. 49:14). And again: The sacrifice of praise will glorify me (Ps. 49:23).

Revelation 1:7a Behold, he comes with the clouds, and every eye will see him, even those who pierced him.

He will come to judgment with the clouds, just as he himself says in the Gospel: “And then they will see the Son of Man coming on the clouds with great power and majesty” (Matt. 24:30). But how will every eye see him, since Scripture says: “Let the wicked be taken away, so that he may not see the glory of God” (Isa. 26:10)? The wicked will see him, placed on the left in judgment, in that form in which, while he was in the world, he could be seen by human beings; but not in that in which he is God. The righteous, however, will see him in that brightness in which those now set in heaven see him, and in which they will see him forever after the end of the world. And even those who pierced him will be forced to see him, whether they wish to or not: namely the wicked Jews who crucified him; so that they may know whom they mocked, as it is written in the Gospel: “They will see him whom they pierced” (Jn. 19:37).

Revelation 1:7b And all the tribes of the earth will mourn over him.

By “all the tribes of the earth” only the wicked are understood. They will mourn over themselves because they despised the commandments of God, because for brief joy they acquired everlasting sorrow. Otherwise: “Behold, he comes with the clouds”, by the clouds the apostles and the other preachers can be understood. He comes with the clouds when his name is made known to all nations through the holy preachers. “And every eye will see him”; for all who had eyes saw him, namely those eyes of which he himself says to the apostles: “Blessed are the eyes that see what you see” (Luke 10:23). But the wicked Jews had eyes, yet they did not have those eyes by which God is seen. “And those who pierced him”: that is, those who persecuted him and his members were for the most part converted to his faith, of whom the apostle Paul was one. “And all the tribes of the earth will mourn over him.” By “all the tribes of the earth” the whole multitude of the nations can be understood, which has been converted to Christ; which, placed daily in the Church of God, mourns its sins so that it may deserve to obtain pardon from God.

Revelation 1:7c Even so, amen,

This is a confirmation of the statement.

Revelation 1:8a I am the Alpha and the Omega.

What Alpha and Omega are, the following words make clear: that is,

Revelation 1:8b the beginning and the end.

As he himself said to the Jews: I am the beginning, I who also speak to you (Jn. 8:25). Our Savior can also be called the beginning because he himself is the beginning, that is, the creator of humanity and the author of salvation; and the end, because he is the end of the law, the end of death, and of every transgression.

Revelation 1:8c Who is, and who was, and who is to come, the Almighty.

He is now in the world, ruling his Church; he was, before the foundation of the world, eternally abiding with the Father; he is to come for judgment, so that he may judge the world in righteousness.

Revelation 1:9 I, John, your brother and partner in tribulation, and in the kingdom, and in endurance in Jesus.

He calls the disciples brothers, because they had been redeemed by the one blood of Christ and brought forth by the one mother, the Church. He also calls himself their partner in persecution, because the persecution of Domitian had disturbed all the Churches; a partner in the kingdom, because he acknowledged that he himself was a member of the Church, just as they were, whom he had won for God; he also declared himself their partner in endurance, because they too bore adversities calmly, just as he himself did, who was kept in exile on the island of Patmos for the love of Christ.

Revelation 1:10a But I was in the spirit on the Lord’s day.

He says that he was in the spirit, because such great mysteries, which follow, could be seen not with bodily but with spiritual eyes: for the natural man does not receive the things that are of the Spirit of God (1 Cor. 2:14). Now the Lord’s day signifies the time of the Gospel; of which time the Apostle speaks, saying: Behold, now is the acceptable time; behold, now is the day of salvation (2 Cor. 6:2). In this time the prophets foretold that the divine mysteries would be made known to the nations.

Revelation 1:10b And I heard behind me a voice like a trumpet, saying.

What we do not know is, as it were, behind us. Another explanation: The voice of the Lord is “behind us,” because we are like runaway slaves; but the Lord cries out behind our back to call us back. John therefore heard the voice behind him, because he had not yet fully understood the mysteries that were then revealed to him. This voice is said to have been like a trumpet; about this trumpet the prophet speaks, saying: “Cry out, do not hold back; lift up your voice like a trumpet, and declare to my people their crimes, and to the house of Jacob their sins” (Isa. 58:1). A trumpet is typically used to rouse soldiers to fight; in the same way, the word of God does not cease to stir us up to fight against the Devil and to conquer him.

Revelation 1:11 What you see, write in a book, and send to the seven Churches: to Ephesus and Smyrna, Pergamum and Thyatira, Sardis and Philadelphia and Laodicea.

By these seven Churches one catholic Church, as the interpretation of the names teaches, is signified. For Ephesus is interpreted as “will” or “counsel”; Smyrna, “their song”; Pergamum, “division of the horns”; Thyatira, “enlightened”; Sardis, “beginning of beauty”; Philadelphia, “saving the inheritance of God”; Laodicea, “a tribe beloved by the Lord.”7 Ephesus, therefore, which is set first, shows the beginning of the conversion of each person; for it is interpreted “will” or “counsel.” For what better counsel is there than good will, which urges a person to renounce the Devil and follow Christ, to abandon vices and to lay hold of virtues? And because those who wish to be converted to God must necessarily implore the mercy of the Lord, so that they can accomplish what they desire, Smyrna is placed next to this, which is interpreted “their song.” For that person sings well who, with a repentant heart and a humbled spirit, begs for the mercy of his Maker; of which song the Apostle speaks, saying: “Singing and making melody in your hearts to the Lord” (Eph. 5:19). And because whoever wishes to be heard by God must of necessity abandon vices, and, so far as strength allows, turn himself to doing good works, Pergamum follows in order, which is interpreted “division of the horns.” The two horns in this place signify evil and good. We divide the horns when, distinguishing between good and evil, we drive evil away from ourselves and embrace the good. And because, as long as we lie in sins, we are in darkness; but when we cast away evil from ourselves and begin to do good, we proceed, as it were, from darkness to light, it is not unfitting that after Pergamum there follows Thyatira, which is interpreted “enlightened.” And because from the doing of the commandments of God the beauty of the soul increases, and the more someone grows in the doing of the commandments of God, the greater beauty he imparts to his soul, it is not inappropriate that after Thyatira there follows Sardis, which is interpreted “beginning of beauty.” It is called the beginning of beauty because the beauty of the saints is begun in this life and is perfected in eternal blessedness. Philadelphia follows, which is said to mean “saving the inheritance of God.” But what is the inheritance of God, if not the whole multitude of the elect, as the Lord says to the prophet: “But Israel, my inheritance” (Isa. 19:25)? And in what does the salvation of the elect consist, if not in the keeping of the commandments of God (Luke 19:10)? For he who came to seek and to save what was lost grants the salvation of souls to those who, each according to his own strength, keep his commandments. Laodicea is placed seventh, because it is interpreted “a tribe beloved by the Lord.” But a tribe beloved by the Lord is the whole multitude of the elect, which is the Church, which through renunciation of vices and the practice of virtues comes to this point, that it is called a tribe beloved by the Lord.

Revelation 1:12a And I turned to see the voice that was speaking with me.

Why did he stand turned away for so long? Why did he not, at the first stroke of the word that struck his ears, turn toward the one who was speaking from behind? But he gave us an example; for he did not first turn toward the Lord who was speaking with him until he heard the names of the Churches to which he was commanded to write. And above we said briefly that, through the interpretations of the names of the seven Churches, the path is shown which leads to life. Whoever therefore desires to go to God by this path must investigate, either by reading or by hearing from others, how he ought to walk along this path; for he is a foolish traveler who desires to go to the place he longs for and does not know the way, if he has not first endeavored to learn what the length of the way is, what its roughness, what its windings, what its dangers; so that when he begins to set out on the journey, he may pass through the dangers more easily because they were foreknown. For we have seen many leave the world in order to gain eternal life by the path of contemplation; but because they did not know the harshness of the path, they suddenly fell. But it can happen that someone may bring forward against these things a testimony from divine Scripture, which says: Do not delay to be converted to God (Sirach 5:8). To this I reply that I am already being converted to God when I do not cease to investigate the path that leads to him. And what does it mean that he says: And I turned to see the voice, since a voice is perceived not by the eyes but by the ears? But John, placed in the spirit, merited to see with spiritual eyes the things that follow. Nor was there any need there for the clamor of spoken words, where within was God himself, who was teaching. In another sense: that person hears the words of God fittingly who does not cease to gaze with the eyes of the mind upon what he has heard, so that he may fulfill them in action.

Revelation 1:12b-13a And turning, I saw seven golden lampstands, and in the midst of the seven golden lampstands one like the Son of Man.

The seven golden lampstands signify the seven Churches, as he himself explained; and the seven Churches prefigure the one catholic Church. These are called golden lampstands either on account of the persecutions which the saints endure through the ages, or on account of the wisdom with which they are enlightened. In the midst of them Christ was seen to stand, as he himself says in the Gospel: Behold, I am with you all days, even to the consummation of the age (Matt. 28:20).

Revelation 1:13b-16 It follows: Clothed in a long garment, and tightened about the breast with a golden belt; but his head and hair were white like white wool, and like snow; and his eyes were like a flame of fire; and his feet like brass, as in a burning furnace; and his voice like the sound of many waters. And he had in his right hand seven stars; and from his mouth came a sharp two‑edged sword; and his face was like the sun shining in its strength.

If you wish to take the seven lampstands and the seven stars as two ornaments, you will find ten ornaments with which the Lord’s body was seen to be adorned. But what the two ornaments, that is, the seven lampstands and the seven stars, signify, he himself has explained; what the remaining eight signify is for us to investigate. For if you examine carefully the holy city of God, which is composed of all the elect and extends from the beginning to the end, you will find it divided into seven parts. The first part of it is the elect who existed from the beginning until the Flood; the second, the patriarchs and the other righteous ones until the Law was given; the third, those who were under the Law; the fourth, the prophets; the fifth, the apostles and those who believed from among the Jews; the sixth part is the multitude of the nations who came to the faith; the seventh, the elect who are to be born at the end of the world and will fight against Antichrist. Through these seven parts, as the Lord grants, let us proceed by speaking; concerning the eighth we will speak in what follows. The long garment, which is an ankle‑length tunic, represents the righteous who lived before the Flood. Of what color it was is not stated, because they had no law other than the natural law. Now the natural law is to love one’s Creator, and not to do to another what one does not wish to be done to oneself. No one who is supported by reason is allowed to be ignorant of this law. By the belt, however, are represented the patriarchs and the other righteous ones who lived before the Law. It is called golden because they are known to have been proven through many tribulations, like gold in a furnace: like Abraham, who was rescued from the fire of the Chaldeans; like Lot, whom the Sodomites afflicted; like Isaac, who endured blindness; like Jacob, who was oppressed by the persecution of his brother and his father‑in‑law; like Joseph, who endured captivity and prison; like Job, who bore the loss of his possessions and grievous suffering. In the same way we must understand this also of the others whom Scripture does not mention. This belt is said to have tightened about the breasts; for by the breasts are signified the two precepts of love, by which it is certain that the holy fathers were pleasing to God.

But his head and hair were white like white wool and like snow. By the head the Law is signified, and by the hairs, which grow from the head, the multitudes are signified who have been saved through the Law. These hairs are said to be white like white wool and like snow, because that people has been cleansed from the darkness of vices through the Law. But his eyes were like a flame of fire. Eyes behold everything that is around them, far and wide; therefore by the eyes the prophets are signified, who deserved to foresee long beforehand the things that were to come. They are said to be like a flame of fire, because, enlightened by the Holy Spirit, they contemplated heavenly mysteries.

And his feet were like fine brass, as in a burning furnace. By the feet, the apostles are signified, and the others who believed from among the Jews. Of these feet the prophet speaks, saying: How beautiful upon the mountains are the feet of one who proclaims peace, announces good, preaches salvation, saying: Zion, your God will reign (Isa. 52:7). They are said to be like fine brass; for bronze in the furnace, mixed with certain mixtures, is melted for so long until it takes on the color of gold, and it is called fine brass. By bronze, then, which is resonant and which quickly turns to blackness, the Jews are signified, who seemed to sound forth the Law according to the letter; but, with their mind covered by the veil of blackness, they were unable to understand the spiritual meaning that lay hidden within it. Therefore bronze is changed into fine brass when from among the Jews the apostles are chosen. By the furnace, moreover, either the world in which Christ was present for a time, or the tribulations that he endured, can be signified. In this furnace also the apostles were joined to him, as he himself says: For you are those who have remained with me in my trials (Luke 22:28). In this furnace, therefore, through the teaching of the Gospel they merited to receive the splendor of gold. And his voice was like the sound of many waters. By the voice of many waters the multitude of nations is signified, who have hastened to faith in Christ.

And he had in his right hand seven stars. What this signifies, he himself explains in the following. And from his mouth came a sharp two‑edged sword. By the sword are indicated the elect who are to be born at the end of the world and to fight against Antichrist. And because the greatest multitude of the nations, abandoning the worship of Christ, will adore Antichrist, a most wicked man, as God, it is not inappropriate that those who will go forth to cut down their errors are signified by the sword. It is seen to have two edges, because these teachers, instructed in the doctrines of the two Testaments, will easily vanquish their enemies. For the fact that the sword is seen to go forth from the mouth of the Lord signifies what he himself said in the Gospel: For it is not you who speak, but the Spirit of your Father who speaks in you (Matt. 10:20). In these seven ornaments, therefore, the whole Church of the elect, which we divide into seven parts, is contained. Without these seven parts, therefore, it does not seem to me that anyone can understand this book in an orderly way; but something must be said about the eighth part which remains.

And his face was like the sun shining in its strength. By the face is signified the Church, as it will be after the resurrection; and therefore by the face, because then the righteous will see God face to face, as the Apostle says (1 Cor. 13:12). It was seen to shine like the sun, because, as the Lord says: Then the righteous will shine like the sun in the kingdom of their Father (Matt. 13:43). Certain things are found in Scripture that correspond to these seven parts, such as that which is read in Joshua (Josh. 6:6 and following), where the Lord commanded that the ark of God should go around the city of Jericho for seven days, once each day, with the priests carrying it, the trumpets sounding, and the people following. For Jericho signifies the whole body of the depraved, which is the city of the Devil; by the children of Israel, the elect are signified; by the priests, the preachers; by the trumpets, the doctrine of preaching; by the people who followed them, all the righteous are signified, who followed their teaching and imitated their deeds; by the ark which they carried, Christ is understood, who is borne by the saints, dwelling in their hearts. Concerning the preachers who were after the coming of Christ, there is no doubt that they carried Christ not only by preaching him with their tongue, but also by loving him in their heart. But those who preceded his coming, how did they carry him? They too indeed carried him; for some of them foretold him with words, some prefigured him by deeds. Now we must ask what this going around, which was done for seven days, signifies. For when we go around a place, we avoid entering it; for when we travel along the road, if we find a pit or deep mud or some danger, by going around it, if we can, we avoid entering it, that we may escape the danger. We go around Jericho if we carry out the journey of the present life according to the will of God; for although we live among the wicked, we go around Jericho if we abstain from their wickedness. Of this going around the Psalmist seems to me to have spoken: I have gone around and sacrificed in his tabernacle a sacrifice of jubilation (Ps. 26:6). For he went around, and afterward he sacrificed; because he first despised the deeds of the wicked, so that his sacrifice of jubilation might become acceptable to God. For there are many who continually praise God; but because they are unwilling to drive vices away from themselves, the sacrifice of their praise is not acceptable to God. The first going around, then, refers to those righteous men who were before the Flood, who were called sons of God. They went around the city of Jericho as long as they kept themselves apart from the fellowship of the depraved. After the ark of God and the people went around the city of Jericho, they returned to the camp; because when the time of each righteous person’s going around is completed, he is led after death to the fellowship of the elect, which is the camp of the saints. The going around of the second day refers to those who lived after the Flood up to Moses: they too went around the city of Jericho; because they separated themselves from the sins of the depraved. For Abraham, although he lived among the Amorites, shrank not only from their vices but even from bodily association with them to such a degree that he was unwilling to take a wife for his son from among their daughters. And about Lot, who lived among the Sodomites, blessed Peter speaks (2 Peter 2:7), who was righteous in hearing and seeing, living among them who day by day tortured his righteous soul with their wicked deeds. In like manner the others as well. The third going around refers to those who were under the Law: with what zeal they ought to separate themselves from the fellowship of the wicked, the Law itself shows clearly. But the Prophets, to whom the fourth going around refers, so greatly despised and reproved the sins of rulers and wicked men that some of them were stoned for this, some were sawn in two, and some died by the slaughter of the sword. (Heb. 11:37) The Apostles, and those who believed from among the Jews, to whom the fifth going around refers, with what eagerness they separated themselves from the counsel and deeds of the unbelieving Jews, Luke relates in the Acts of the Apostles. The Gentiles, to whom the sixth going around refers, with what passion they separated themselves from the fellowship of the wicked, let the martyrs who were examined testify. The seventh going around refers to those who are to be in the times of Antichrist; and this book also shows how they will avoid the unbelief of Antichrist and his ministers. Therefore, when on the seventh day the priests went around Jericho with the ark, and raised a shout, the walls of Jericho fell, and all the people with their king were killed; because when, at the end of the world, the preachers of truth preach and announce the end of the world, suddenly the end of the world will come, and the whole city of the Devil will be destroyed, and with its head, the Devil himself, will be condemned to eternal death.

We also read in the book of Judges that the very strong Samson had seven locks of hair (Judg. 16:13). Samson represents Christ; by the hair, however, all the elect are signified. And just as hair clings to the head, so all the righteous cling to Christ, who is the head of all his elect; and as Samson’s hair was divided into seven parts, so too the whole number of the elect can be divided into seven parts according to the qualities of the times, as we have shown above.

But also that which is written in Solomon: “Wisdom has built herself a house, she has cut out her seven pillars” (Prov. 9:1), can fit this understanding of ours. For Wisdom built herself a house when our Lord Jesus Christ, from the beginning of the world to its end, has constructed and will construct the Church for himself from the multitude of the elect; she has cut out her seven pillars when, in those seven periods, he appointed in his Church preachers suited to each period, who would uphold it both with saving warnings and with examples of good works.

We can also adapt to this our exposition what the same Solomon sets forth in another place, saying: “Give a portion to seven, and also to eight” (Eccles. 11:2). First, then, we must ask what that is of which we are commanded to make portions. For there is a certain good, and this is the highest good, namely love, which, unless it is divided into parts, will not be a perfect good; for it is divided into love of God and love of neighbor. Though it is one, it nonetheless has two arms, which it stretches around everywhere, joining God to humans and humans to God. There are also two lives, one called active, the other called contemplative. Love of neighbor belongs to the active life, but love of God properly belongs to the contemplative life. Therefore we can understand by “seven” the whole multitude of the elect, and by “eight” our Lord Jesus Christ, because He rose on the eighth day. So the holy ones of God give a portion of their love to seven when they are engaged in the exercises of the active life, that is, when they bestow care upon their neighbor, not only bodily but also spiritual care. They also give a portion to eight when the love of God again calls them back to contemplation of Him. Another interpretation: We give a portion to seven when we show due veneration to the saints of God; we give a portion to eight when we offer due worship to God, our Creator. Next:

Revelation 1:17a And when I had seen him, I fell at his feet as though dead.

Behold John, who seemed to himself to stand firm, when he saw the Lord, fell as though dead; because every holy man, the higher he raises himself to contemplate the divinity of God, the lower he falls within himself; for he easily perceives how small his greatness is in comparison with God. And so he fell as though dead, because in comparison with God he perceived that he was nothing. He fell at his feet, because he perceived that he could in no way comprehend the mystery of his incarnation, which is figured by the feet.

Revelation 1:17b And he laid his hand upon me, saying: Do not fear.

Just as the hand in the body has many functions, so also the hand of God in the divine Scriptures has many meanings; for Job calls the scourge of God the hand of God, when he says: “Keep your hand far from me, and let not your dread terrify me” (Job 13:21). And again he says to his friends: “Have pity on me, have pity on me, at least you my friends; for the hand of the Lord has touched me” (Job 19:21). And the Lord also touched Jeremiah with the hand of wisdom, as he himself says: “And the Lord stretched out his hand and touched my mouth, and said to me: Behold, I have put my words in your mouth,” and what follows (Jer. 1:9). Likewise Daniel was touched by the hand of God’s strengthening, when, lying prostrate on the ground because of the angelic vision, he saw a hand sent to him, which strengthened him, to whom he also said: “Speak, Lord, for you have strengthened me” (Dan. 10:10, 19). By this same hand of strengthening, then, John himself was touched, and by strengthening him it raised him up, so that he might not cast himself down beyond due measure.

Revelation 1:18a I am the first and the last.

If he is the first, why did Arius say that the Son of God had a beginning? For if he had a beginning, then he himself would not be the first, but the one from whom he took his beginning would be. Therefore he himself is the first and the last, because just as he will not have an end, so he did not have a beginning.

Revelation 1:18b And I was dead, and behold, I am living unto the ages of ages.

This is clear.

Revelation 1:18c And I have the keys of death and of hell [inferni].

Whoever has the key of a certain house admits whom he wishes, and whom he wishes he keeps from entering the house. Through the key, therefore, the power of the Son of God is understood. Therefore he himself possesses the keys of death and of hell; because those whom he wills, he mercifully frees from the condemnation of eternal death; and those whom he wills, he allows to remain in the same danger of condemnation by a just judgment.

Revelation 1:19 Therefore write the things you have seen, and the things that are, and the things that must happen after these.

Here he shows that he has woven his prophecy not only about present and future things, but also about past things. Therefore write the things you have seen, that is, the things about the past that have been shown to you; the things that are, and the things that must happen after these, that is, the present and the future.

Revelation 1:20a The mystery of the seven stars that you saw in my right hand, and the seven golden lampstands.

It is understood that this concerns what they signify. And what they signify he makes clear by adding:

Revelation 1:20b The seven stars, he says, are the angels of the seven churches, and the seven golden lampstands are the seven churches.

We must therefore understand by the seven angels the rulers of the seven churches, because “angel” is interpreted as “messenger”; and those who announce the word of God to the peoples are fittingly called angels, that is, messengers. And just as through the seven churches is signified the one Catholic Church, so also through the seven rulers of the seven churches are signified all the rulers of the Catholic Church.

Chapter 2

Revelation 2:1 And to the angel of the Church in Ephesus write: These things says the one who holds the seven stars in his right hand, who walks in the midst of the seven golden lampstands.

By the right hand of God, the power of God can be indicated in this place, by the stars the holy preachers. Therefore the holy preachers are held in the hand of God, because they are guided and ruled by him, and they are joined to him, just as he speaks in the Gospel, saying: As the branch cannot bear fruit unless it remains in the vine, so neither can you unless you remain in me (Jn. 15:4). But Christ walks in the midst of the seven golden lampstands, as he himself says through the prophet: I will dwell among them and walk among them; and I will be their God, and they shall be my people (Lev. 26:12).

Revelation 2:2a I know your works,

That is, those which you used to do previously, before love grew cold in you,

Revelation 2:2b and your work and your patience,

That is, the persecutions which you patiently endure for my name.

Revelation 2:2c And you cannot tolerate the wicked.

Since Holy Scripture instructs us to tolerate the wicked, (Matt. 13) why is he here praised for not being able to tolerate them? It seems to me that this is said about heretics; for we ought sometimes to correct the faults of our neighbors by reproving them at a suitable time, and sometimes to endure them patiently, so that those whom correction does not improve may be led to change by patience. For there are some who do not know how to reprove their neighbors unless they are inflamed with anger. If one of these people is stirred up against his neighbor not only for a great matter, but even for some small thing, he carries anger in his heart until he sees something in his neighbor’s conduct that he can blame; and then he becomes glad when he has inflicted the vengeance of rebuke upon his neighbor. If he sees that this displeases someone, he says that he did it out of zeal for God. I am therefore speaking to you, who are such a person. Why do you seek to deceive the One who considers the secrets of hearts? If the zeal of God is in you, why do your eyes wander here and there in a diabolical manner, desiring rather to see evil than good, desiring to see in your neighbor an evil that you may reproach, and not a good by which you may be edified? There are also many who impatiently assail those from whom they have suffered some injury, even for the slightest offenses; but those whom they love they not only fail to reprove, even though they have gravely sinned, but even defend them with all their strength. O unhappy friendship, which by keeping silence and defending the one it loves, hands him over to the Devil, but by reproving the one against whom it is angry, draws him away from the Devil! Therefore, that correction is just which flows from zeal for God and brotherly love; which is carried out not for the sake of avenging anger, but for the sake of saving the soul of one’s neighbor. But since we have said this briefly, let us return to what we began. Therefore this statement which is said by the Lord to the bishop of the Church of Ephesus, “Because you cannot tolerate the wicked,” must be understood of heretics; for everyone who is a shepherd of the Church ought, as far as he can, to avoid association with heretics, lest by the familiarity of such people the hearts of the simple be corrupted by the poison of their malice. For just as a wolf at peace among sheep cannot move about without tearing those same sheep, so neither can a heretic among simple folk. It continues:

Revelation 2:2d And you have tested those who call themselves apostles and are not, and you have found them liars.

Certain men who taught twisted things in the times of the apostles claimed to be apostles, so that by the reverence for the name they might more easily plant corrupt teaching in the hearts of their hearers (2 Cor. 10:12). The Apostle Paul condemns these men, and the Lord in the Gospel teaches us to beware of them when he says: “Beware of false prophets, who come to you in sheep’s clothing, but inwardly are ravenous wolves” (Matt. 7:15). This man therefore proved that these are false men, and that their teaching is false.

Revelation 2:3 And you have patience, and you have endured for my name’s sake, and you have not failed:

That is, in this your patience has appeared, because in the labors that you endured for my name, you did not fail.

Revelation 2:4 But I have against you that you have abandoned your first love.

There are many who, at the beginning of their conversion, are fervent in the service of God, but in the course of time grow lukewarm; and it is clear that this happened to this bishop of the Church of Ephesus.

Revelation 2:5a Remember therefore from where you have fallen.

From where have you been cut off? From love; if you have been cut off from love, you have been cut off from God, because God is love.

Revelation 2:5b And do penance.

Namely, because through your sluggishness and laziness you have been separated from love.

Revelation 2:5c And do your first works: but if not, I will come to you quickly and will move your lampstand from its place, unless you do penance.

If by the lampstand the Church of Ephesus is signified, why will the Church lose its place because of the sin of the pastor, since Scripture says: “The fathers shall not die for the sons, nor the sons for their fathers, but each one shall die in his own sin” (Jer. 31:30)? But by “place” it seems to me that the reward which was owed him for his pastoral care is signified, so that the sense is: Unless you do penance for the fact that you have forsaken your first love and have not returned to your first works, so that what you preach with your mouth you may fulfill in deed, I will withdraw the reward which was owed you for your pastoral care.

Revelation 2:6 But you have this (namely, that the lampstand is not moved), because you have hated the deeds of the Nicolaitans, which I also hate.

And the meaning is: Because you have hated the deeds of the Nicolaitans, which I also hate, the reward that was owed to you for your pastoral care still remains with you. Now the Nicolaitans were heretics, whom he said above that he could not endure.

Revelation 2:7a Whoever has ears to hear, let him hear what the Spirit says to the Churches.

For to all who have ears he says: Whoever has ears to hear, let him hear; without doubt he requires the ears of the heart.

Revelation 2:7b To the one who conquers I will give to eat from the tree of life, which is in the paradise of my God.

There were two trees in the middle of paradise, of which one was called the tree of life, and the other the tree of the knowledge of good and evil. Their power was such that if anyone ate from the tree of life, he would not die eternally; but if anyone ate from the tree of the knowledge of good and evil (Gen. 2:17), although he was immortal, he would immediately become mortal. Paradise therefore signifies the Church; but the tree of life in the middle of paradise is Christ in the midst of his Church. The tree of the knowledge of good and evil, which is also said to have been in the middle of paradise, signifies the Devil, who does not cease to move about among the faithful, so that he may deceive whomsoever he can. The tree of life therefore belongs to the elect, but the tree of the knowledge of good and evil to the wicked. The elect eat the fruit of the tree of life, so that they may live forever, because they keep the teachings of the Gospel, so that they may live forever with the Lord; but the wicked eat the fruit of the tree of the knowledge of good and evil, and they die, because they feed on the suggestions of the Devil and on all vices and pleasures; from this they draw down eternal damnation upon themselves. And just as the fruits of the tree of the knowledge of good and evil were more delightful to the woman than the fruits of the tree of life, so also the glory of this present age, which they see with the eyes of the body, is more delightful to the wicked than the glory of the heavenly homeland, which is seen only with spiritual eyes. But a question arises as to why it is called the tree of the knowledge of good and evil, since it brought to the first man the knowledge of evil, not the knowledge of good. It seems to me that this is to be solved in the following way: for the first angel, when he was created, had knowledge of good, not knowledge of evil; but he discovered the knowledge of evil through himself, on account of which he was cast down from that heavenly glory. The first man therefore ate from the tree of the knowledge of good and evil when he followed the suggestions of him who possessed the knowledge of good and evil; for the tree itself could not teach the knowledge of evil, but the Devil, who persuaded the man to transgress the command of God. Therefore he says: To the one who conquers I will give to eat from the tree of life, which is in the paradise of my God. He conquers who perseveres in good works until the end of his life. Christ therefore will give the conqueror to eat from the tree of life, because to those who keep his teachings until the end of life he will grant, together with himself, the glory of his immortality in heavenly blessedness, so that they may live eternally with him whose teachings they strove to keep while placed in this world. But that he calls the Father his God refers to his humanity.

Revelation 2:8-9a And to the angel of the Church in Smyrna write: These things says the First and the Last, who was dead and lives: I know your tribulation and your poverty.

It can be that the Church of the Smyrnaeans endured the same persecution from their own countrymen in the plundering of their goods as that which was in Jerusalem. And therefore its tribulation and poverty, which it endured for him, are praised by the Lord. Through this voluntary poverty can also be understood, of which the Lord says in the Gospel: Blessed are the poor in spirit, for theirs is the kingdom of heaven (Matt. 5:3).

Revelation 2:9b But you are rich:

poor in goods, namely, rich in virtues.

Revelation 2:9c And you are blasphemed by those who say that they are Jews and are not, but are a synagogue of Satan.

Jews is interpreted as confessors8: Jews who deny Christ by blaspheming him are not Jews, that is, confessors; but they are fittingly called a synagogue of Satan.

Revelation 2:10a Do not fear any of these things that you are about to suffer.

Troubles are foretold to him so that he may be able to endure what comes more easily.

Revelation 2:10b Behold, the Devil is about to cast some of you into prison so that you may be tested:

That is, some of you the Devil, that is, the servants of the Devil, will send into prison, so that you may be tested whether, through fear of punishments, you will withdraw from the faith, or decide to die for love of Christ.

Revelation 2:10c And you will have tribulation for ten days.

He has shown the time during which this persecution would rage more cruelly.

Revelation 2:10d Be faithful unto death, and I will give you the crown of life.

The crown of life signifies eternal glory.

Revelation 2:11 He who has ears, let him hear what the Spirit says to the Churches. He who overcomes will not be harmed by the second death.

What the second death is, we will explain in the following.

Revelation 2:12 And to the angel of the Church of Pergamum write: These things says the one who has the sword sharp on both sides.

By the sword one can understand the judicial power of the Lord, which is said to be sharp on both sides because of two judgments: one that was carried out through water, the other that will be carried out through fire. But we are excluded from this meaning, because that judgment has already been completed and has been abolished by the mercy of God. For the sharp edge of that judgment was removed when God said to Noah: “By myself I have sworn that I will not again bring the waters of a flood upon the earth” (cf. Gen. 9:11; Isa. 54:9). It therefore seems to me that by the sword we should understand divine Scripture, which is said to be sharp for the cutting away of vices, and is shown to be sharp on both sides because of the two Testaments. For both the Old and the New Testament continually admonish us to abandon vices and to train ourselves in good works, so that we may be able to find the salvation of our souls.

Revelation 2:13a I know where you live, where the throne of Satan is:

That is, you live among those who have the Devil as their sitter.

Revelation 2:13b And you hold my name and have not denied my faith:

That is, although you constantly endure persecutions from those among whom you live on account of my name, yet you hold my name and have not denied my faith.

Revelation 2:13c And in those days Antipas, my witness, who was killed among them where Satan dwells.

And in those days, that is, in the days when persecution raged more fiercely among you, Antipas, my witness, it is understood, arose, who was killed among you where Satan dwells. He was killed among you, and not by you, but by those among whom you live, in whom the Devil dwells.

Revelation 2:14-15 But I have a few things against you, because you have there those who hold the teaching of Balaam, who taught Balak to put a stumbling block before the children of Israel; in the same way you also have those who hold the teaching of the Nicolaitans.

We read in the book of Numbers that Balak, king of the Moabites, at the suggestion of Balaam, set attractive women in the sight of the children of Israel, women who sacrificed to their gods, whose beauty not only stirred them to commit lust, but also led them to eat of their sacrifices (Num. 25:1). The Nicolaitans, however, were heretics who detested marriage and taught the faithful that they should join themselves to whomever they wished, after the manner of beasts. Just as through the teaching of Balaam the children of Israel fell into the evil of fornication, on account of which many of them perished, so also the Nicolaitans, by their depraved teaching, cast down some of the faithful who were at Pergamum into the evil of fornication, teaching them that they should in no way entangle themselves in the bond of marriage, but freely mix with whomever they wished. But when he says, “I have a few things against you,” he signifies that a few of the faithful have been deceived by the heretics.

Revelation 2:16a Likewise, do penance.

This word he says, “likewise,” seems to me to look back to the angel of the Church of Ephesus, to whom it is said: “Do penance,” (Rev. 2:5) because you have left your first love. As if he were saying: Just as I commanded the angel of Ephesus to do penance because he had left his first love, so you likewise do penance, because you have turned aside from love, so that you might lose some of your own people through your silence.

Revelation 2:16b Otherwise, I will come to you quickly.

The coming of the Lord is usually understood as death; for the Lord comes to each person when, through death, he separates the soul from the body. “Quickly,” that is, unexpectedly, just as the Lord speaks in the Gospel about the negligent servant: “But the Lord of that servant will come on a day he does not know, and at an hour he is unaware of; and he will cut him in two, and will place his portion with the hypocrites” (Matt. 24:51). For a human being is composed of soul and body: the Lord “cuts” a person in two when he separates the soul from the body; he places his portion with the hypocrites when he hands over the soul, which is the second part in a human being, to be tormented with the wicked for its iniquities. In what he says, “I will come to you quickly,” this seems to me to be the meaning: If you do not do penance, I will come to you quickly, so that by death, which you neglect to foresee, I may dissolve you. It continues:

Revelation 2:16c And I will fight against them with the sword of my mouth,

That is, I will place another in your place, and I will put the sword of my words in his mouth, with which he may cast down the adversaries of the truth.

Revelation 2:17a Whoever has ears, let him hear what the Spirit says to the Churches.

Wherever this sentence is placed, whether in the Gospel or in the Apocalypse, we must understand that what is said is mystical and difficult to understand.

Revelation 2:17b To the one who overcomes I will give hidden manna.

We read in the Old Testament (Exod. 16:14) that the Lord fed the people of the Jews, as they walked through the deserts, with manna until they came to the land of promise. By manna, therefore, wisdom can be understood, which is Christ: for he is the living bread who came down from heaven (Jn. 6:41). All the elect are fed with this bread, placed in the desert and on the journey of this world, until they come to the land of promise, that is, to the heavenly blessedness which God has promised to those who love him. It is called hidden for this reason, because the wisdom of God is hidden from the proud and revealed to the humble, as he himself says in the Gospel: “I confess to you, Father, Lord of heaven and earth, because you have hidden these things from the wise and sensible and revealed them to little ones” (Matt. 11:25).

Revelation 2:17c And I will give him a white stone.

By the stone, which is a small rock and is so called from “calculating,” we can understand the Gospel. For just as the ancients used to gather to themselves a great number of these stones for the dividing and distributing of numbers9, so the teachers of the Church gather many words of the Gospel in their hearts; and when they open to their hearers the obscurities, spiritual meanings, and benefits of these words, they are, as it were, dividing and distributing their numbers.

Revelation 2:17d And on the stone a new name written, which no one knows except the one who receives it.
