Therefore the saints give glory to God when, whatever good they do, they do it with this intention: that God may be glorified through them, as he himself says in the Gospel: Let them see your good works and glorify your Father who is in heaven (Matt. 5:16). For John also says that Peter by his death glorified God (Jn. 21:19). They give honor when in all that they do, they seek the honor of God, not their own. Above, “blessing” is placed in the third place, but here “power”; and because the saints, while they accomplish great deeds, are blessed (praised) by human beings, “power” fits well together with “blessing.” But how greatly the saints detest the praises of people for the great works they do, the book of the Acts of the Apostles shows us. For when Peter and John had healed the lame man and all eyes were fixed on them, as though they had done this by their own power, Peter, avoiding the “blessings” (admiring looks) of their eyes, said to them: Men, brothers, why do you look at us, as though by our own power or virtue we had made this man walk? The God of Abraham and the God of Isaac and the God of Jacob has raised up his Son Jesus (Acts 3:12). And a little later: In his name this man stands before you sound. Likewise Paul and Barnabas, when they had healed a sick man in Lystra and the crowds wanted to offer sacrifice to them, tore their garments, saying: Men, why are you doing this? We too are human beings, with the same nature as you (Acts 14:14). We can also understand this of Christ, for it is clear that, according to his humanity, he has received both glory and honor and power. It follows:

Revelation 4:11b For you created all things, and because of your will they existed and were created.

If this is to be understood in the same sense as that other saying: “He who made the things that are to be”; then all creatures were in the power and will of God before they were created, but were in fact created at the time which God appointed. Therefore, may he himself, who created all things out of nothing, consent to create in us a pure heart, he who lives and reigns for ever and ever, amen.

On the Third Vision

Not without reason, after the description of the form of the Church, the opening of the book is set forth, by which the opening of the divine Scriptures is signified; because after the Church had been founded among the nations by the holy apostles and the other preachers, the books of the Old and New Testament were expounded by the holy doctors according to apostolic faith.

Chapter 5

Revelation 5:1 And I saw in the right hand of the one seated upon the throne a book written within and without, sealed with seven seals.

About the right hand and the seals which the Lord has given, we shall speak in what follows; but the book written within and without signifies not only the Old Testament, but also the New, which is written within according to allegory, without according to history. This book, therefore, contains within itself the Old and the New Testament; because the spiritual understanding in the Old Testament is nothing other than the New Testament.

Revelation 5:2a And I saw a mighty Angel, proclaiming with a loud voice.

The mighty Angel signifies the Fathers of the Old Testament, who preached with a loud voice, because they foretold that Christ would come for the redemption of humanity.

Revelation 5:2b Who is worthy to open the book and to loose its seals?

The angel’s question signifies the desire of the saints; for the saints longed to see Christ in the flesh and to hear his words, because they knew that all the mysteries of the Old Testament were to be revealed by him, just as he himself said to his disciples: Truly I say to you, many prophets and kings have wanted to see what you see, and did not see it, and to hear what you hear, and did not hear it (Luke 10:24).

Revelation 5:3 And no one was found worthy, neither in heaven nor on earth nor under the earth, to open the book and to break its seals.

For no Angel dwelling in heaven, no righteous person living on earth, and none even of those who had paid the debt of death, ever appeared in the world who could redeem humanity from the power of the Devil; and this redemption is the opening of the Old Testament.

Revelation 5:4 And I wept greatly, because no one was found worthy to open the book or to look upon it.

John in this place takes the role of those of whom we have just spoken, that is, the Fathers of the Old Testament; for the saints wept greatly, because the mysteries of the Scriptures were hidden from them and there was no one who could reveal them to them, and no one who could rescue lost humankind from the power of the Devil. But we must ask why he says that this book cannot be seen by anyone, when above he says that he saw it in the right hand of the One who sits upon the throne. For the ancient Fathers, whose figure John holds in this place, did both see and not see the book of the Old Testament: they saw it according to the letter, and they did not see it according to the spiritual understanding.

Revelation 5:5 And one of the elders said to me: Do not weep. The Lion of the tribe of Judah, the root of David, has conquered, to open the book and to loose its seven seals.

It seems to me that the elder has the same meaning as John, and also as the mighty angel; for he signifies the Fathers of the Old Testament. For the mighty angel asks who would be worthy to open the book; John weeps because no one was found worthy to open the book; but the elder says that the book was to be opened by the Lion, that is, by Christ. Therefore, by the mighty angel are signified those who, desiring the coming of Christ, were asking at what time he would come; John, however, signifies those who were weeping because he was so long in delaying; but the elder signifies the prophets, who said in what manner and at what time he would come. By the Lion of the tribe of Judah, therefore, Christ is understood, who was born from the tribe of Judah and the lineage of David. Now he is called a lion on account of his strength, because he conquered the Devil and snatched his elect from his power. Therefore the Lion of the tribe of Judah, the root of David, conquered the Devil, so that he might open the book and its seven seals; because the book could not be opened unless the Devil were first conquered, and this victory was the opening of the Old Testament. How he opened the seven seals we will say in what follows.

Revelation 5:6 And I saw, in the midst of the throne and of the four living creatures and of the elders, a Lamb standing as though slain, having seven horns and seven eyes, which are the seven spirits of God sent out into all the earth.

By the throne and the four living creatures and the elders, one Church with all its orders is signified. But the Lamb was not slain, but was seen as though slain, because he tasted death in passing, and a little afterward, rising again, now no longer dies; death will no longer have dominion over him (Rom. 6:9). He was seen to have seven horns and seven eyes. What the seven eyes are he himself explains when he adds: “Which are the seven spirits of God, sent into all the earth.” By horns, however, kingdoms are often signified. It seems to me, therefore, that the horns have one meaning and the eyes another; for by the seven horns all the elect are signified, who are called the kingdom of God, whose whole multitude we have divided above into seven parts. To the first horn, then, belong the elect who were before the Flood; to the second, those who were after the Flood up to the Law; to the third, those who were under the Law; to the fourth, the prophets; to the fifth, those of the Jews who believed in Christ; to the sixth, the Gentile people; and to the seventh, those who are to be born at the end of the world and to fight against Antichrist.

Revelation 5:7 And he came and took the book from the right hand of the one sitting upon the throne.

Above we said that by the one sitting on the throne Christ was signified; but because he himself said: “I am in the Father, and the Father is in me; and whoever sees me, sees the Father” (Jn. 14:9 and so on), in this passage the one sitting on the throne signifies the Father; the right hand of the Father, however, is the Son; and the Lamb signifies the man whom Christ assumed. Therefore the Lamb took the book from the right hand of the one sitting upon the throne, because the man Christ received from his own divinity the power to unlock the mysteries of the divine Scriptures.

Revelation 5:8 And when he had opened the book, the four living creatures and the twenty-four elders fell down before the Lamb, each having harps and golden bowls full of perfumes, which are the prayers of the saints.

We must inquire how the book could have been opened before the unsealing of the seals. But this must be understood spiritually. For the prophets foretold the coming of Christ, his passion, and his resurrection, sometimes through certain figures, sometimes with plain words; and the patriarchs also prefigured all these things in their own deeds. But Christ, when he was born of the Virgin, when he suffered, and rose again, and ascended into heaven, opened the book of the Old Testament, because the things that were signified there he fulfilled in deed. Afterwards, what in the Old Testament was obscure, and likewise in the New, he opened to the teachers of the Church through the inspiration of the Holy Spirit. And this opening is the unsealing of the seals. But what follows, that the four living creatures and the twenty-four elders fell down before the Lamb, refers to those same teachers of the Church; for as often as they recall to memory the birth, passion, and resurrection of the Lord, and call to mind in whatever ways these things were prefigured by the holy fathers and foretold by the prophets; as often as they do not cease to investigate those things which in the Gospel are set forth obscurely, so often is the sealed book opened to them. They fall down before the Lamb when, by meditation on the divine Scriptures, considering the immeasurable mercy of God, they humble themselves in the sight of their Creator; and the more they cast themselves down in humility, the more they burn with love for him, because the humility that is practiced in the sight of God nourishes the love of God. By the harps, moreover, which the living creatures and the elders are said to have had, the mortification of the flesh is signified, as the Apostle says: “Put to death your members which are upon the earth: fornication, uncleanness, covetousness, and the rest” (Col. 3:5). But the golden bowls are the hearts of the saints, filled with spiritual wisdom, which are said to have been full of perfumes. And what the perfumes are he himself explains when he adds: “Which are the prayers of the saints.” And so that these perfumes may ascend to God, the psalmist prays when he says: “Let my prayer be directed as incense in your sight.” (Ps. 140:2)

Revelation 5:9a And they were singing a new song, saying:

The new people sing a new song; the new song is the song of the Gospel.

Revelation 5:9b You are worthy, Lord God, to receive the book and to open its seals.

What he says here refers to his humanity; for according to his divinity he could receive nothing, because he has always possessed all things. Why he was worthy to receive the book and to loose its seals he makes clear by adding:

Revelation 5:9c Because you were slain, and you redeemed us for God in your blood from every tribe and people and nation.

Here he shows that from all the nations the Church has been gathered together; for there were certain heretics who denied this.

Revelation 5:10 And you have made us for our God a kingdom and priests, and they shall reign over the earth.

If the saints of God are the kingdom of God, how are they said to reign over the earth, that is, over the holy Church? Therefore the saints of God are the kingdom of God, because God reigns in them; and they are kings, because they know how to rule themselves and others in the best way; they are also called priests, because, set on fire daily with the flame of love, they are accustomed to offer themselves to God through regret of heart and tears, as the Psalmist says: “A sacrifice to God is a broken spirit” (Ps. 50:19). Or they are called priests of God because they are members of the supreme Priest, of whom the Psalmist says: “You are a Priest forever according to the order of Melchizedek” (Ps. 109:4).

Revelation 5:11-12 And I saw and heard the voice of many angels around the throne, and of the elders, and of the four living creatures; and the number of them was thousands of thousands, saying with a loud voice: Worthy is the Lamb who was slain to receive power and divinity, and wisdom, and strength, and honor, and glory, and blessing.

By the throne, and the four living creatures, and the elders, the whole Church with its teachers is signified; but the angels who were seen to encircle the throne, that is, the Church, are the angels who are appointed by God for the guardianship of the Church, as Paul the apostle speaks, saying: Are they not all ministering spirits, sent to serve for the sake of those who are to obtain the inheritance of salvation (Heb. 1:14)? Moreover, by their very great number is signified the countless multitude who are said to bless the Lamb with a loud voice; because to whatever tasks the angels are sent to fulfil, they never cease from the praises of God. And just as this book is entirely arranged in the number seven, for indeed the prophet says that the seven gifts of the Holy Spirit have rested upon Christ, so also seven blessings are set down in this place, with which the Angels are said continually to praise God.

Revelation 5:13a And every creature which is in heaven, and which is on earth, and which is under the earth, and which is in the sea, and which are in it, all I heard saying to Him who sits on the throne and to the Lamb.

By “creature which was in heaven,” angels are meant; by “that which was on earth,” humans; by “that which was under the earth,” those who have already paid the debt of death are indicated. But when he says, “And those which are in the sea,” we must understand the waters of the sea; and by “the sea,” the place in which the waters are contained. When he adds, “And those which are in it,” we can understand the fish who dwell in the waters. But if by “every creature which is on earth” we can understand all humans, how is it that all humans praise God, when pagans and Jews ought rather to be called blasphemers of God than praisers? We also find certain scandalous people who are called Christians, who, although they seem to praise God, do not praise God; because, as Scripture says: “Praise is not becoming in the mouth of a sinner” (Sirach 15:9). And if by “every creature which is on earth” we must understand all souls whose bodies rest in the earth, how are those who are tormented in hell [inferos] to be believed to praise God? Rather, by “every creature which is on earth and under the earth,” we must understand only the elect: who both on earth do not cease to praise God by their works and words, and, placed with the Lord in heaven, never cease from His praises. By “the sea,” moreover, we can understand the Church; by “the waters of the sea,” the multitude of the faithful; by “the fish who dwell in the waters,” the teachers of the Church. There follows a blessing, in which the faithful are said to bless God continually:

Revelation 5:13b Blessing, honor, glory, and power be to our God unto the ages of ages;

for the elect indeed bless God unceasingly, when they join works to words.

Revelation 5:14a And the four living creatures were saying: Amen.

Amen is the confirmation of a statement; therefore the four living creatures say Amen when the holy preachers constantly desire that their hearers may advance more and more in good works.

Revelation 5:14b And the twenty-four elders fell down and worshipped.

In this place the twenty-four elders have the same meaning as the four living creatures; therefore the elders fall down and worship when the holy preachers, the more they see their hearers have advanced in good works, the more they humble themselves in the sight of God, as Scripture says: The greater you are, humble yourself in all things, and you will find favor before God (Sirach 3:20). Next follows the opening of the seals, which must be believed to refer only to the opening of the divine Scriptures. For even the things that are said to have been done under the openings of the seals refer to the opening of the Old and New Testaments. And just as the whole body of the elect consists of seven parts, so there are seven seals, which correspond to each part, to show those things that are obscurely set down in the divine Scriptures, or what the deeds of the holy fathers signify. The opening of the first seal, then, refers to the things that were done before the Flood; the second seal to the patriarchs; the third to those who were under the Law; the fourth to the prophets; and the three openings that remain refer to the New Testament. The fifth opening refers to the martyrs; the sixth to the casting down of the Jews and the calling of the Gentiles; the seventh refers to the coming of Christ. And why the coming of the Lord is placed in the seventh place we shall say in what follows. But we must ask why the opening of the Old Testament is prefigured through the vision of four horsemen; for the horseman is, on the part of the horse, irrational, but on the part of the rider is recognized as rational. Likewise, certain legal precepts, on the side of the letter, are irrational, but on the side of allegory are shown to be very rational.

Chapter 6

Revelation 6:1a And I saw when the Lamb opened one of the seven seals.

The opening of the first seal, as was said above, refers to the things that were done before the Flood. Therefore the Lamb opened the first seal when, by the grace of the Holy Spirit, he made known to the teachers of the Church how the events that took place before the Flood are to be understood spiritually. And so that we may show more fully what the opening of the seals is, let us say a little about the opening of each seal. In Genesis it is written that God planted a paradise of delight, in which he placed the man whom he had formed, which is described as being planted with various trees (Gen. 2:8). By the paradise, therefore, the Church is indicated; by the trees of paradise, on the other hand, the teachers of the Church are signified. And just as the trees of paradise supplied the first humans with the nourishment of their fruits, so the teachers of the Church do not cease to provide the nourishment of divine words to the souls of the faithful established in the Church. We have spoken above about the tree of life and the tree of the knowledge of good and evil. But the fountain that went out from the midst of paradise signified the Gospel, which springs up in the midst of the holy Church. This was then divided into four heads, because the teaching of the Gospel is divided into four books and goes around all the boundaries of the earth.

For Adam also prefigured Christ, and Eve the Church: and just as Eve was made from the side of the man while he slept, so also the Church was fashioned from the side of Christ as he hung upon the cross; because by his blood she was redeemed and cleansed from defilements. Two sons were born to Adam, Cain and Abel: Cain signified the people of the Jews; Abel, however, signified Christ. Cain offered to the Lord gifts from the fruits of the earth; for the people of the Jews, who were earthly, offered earthly gifts, that is, first-fruits, tithes, sacrifices, and the other things commanded in the Law; but Abel offered from the firstborn of his flock and from their fat. The firstborn of the flock are the apostles, whom the Church first brought forth, and from whom the Church was born; the fat, however, is the faith of the apostles. Abel therefore offered from the firstborn of his flock and from their fat, because Christ offered to God the Father the apostles whom he had chosen from the world, and their faith. Cain, led by envy because the Lord had regard for Abel and for his gifts, killed him; likewise the Jews, seeing the signs that Christ worked by his own power and that of the Father, killed him, who was their brother according to the flesh. God cursed Cain because he had killed his brother Abel, and told him that he would be a wanderer and a fugitive upon the earth. Similarly the Jews are cursed not only by God, but by themselves, when they said of Christ: “His blood be upon us and upon our children” (Matt. 27:25). And they are wanderers and fugitives upon the earth, because, having no settled homeland of their own, they roam among all nations. Of the opening, then, of the first seal, let what has been said be enough.

Revelation 6:1b I heard one of the four living creatures saying: Come and see.

The first living creature has the same meaning as the second, the third, and the fourth; for they signify the teachers of the Church. But when it says: Come and see, it urges us toward spiritual understanding, as if it were saying: Come, not with the steps of the body, but with the steps of the mind; and understand in a spiritual way the things which you have read as having been done historically in the earliest time.

Revelation 6:2 And behold, a white horse, and the one who sat upon it had a bow, and a crown was given to him, and he went out conquering, so that he might conquer.

The white horse signifies the righteous who were before the flood, who are called white because of their innocence. But the rider of the horse is the Lord, who eternally presides over his saints. By the bow, which sends arrows far from itself and wounds, the vengeance of the Lord can be signified; by this he condemned the first humans because of the guilt of disobedience, and punished Cain sevenfold because of the crime of murdering his brother [fratricidii]. By the crown likewise, just as by the white horse, the righteous who were before the flood are signified. And he went out conquering, so that he might conquer, when he decreed that through the waters of the flood the whole multitude of the wicked should be destroyed.

Revelation 6:3a And when he had opened the second seal.

The opening of the second seal refers to the construction of the ark and to the righteous who were before the Law. To show, therefore, the opening of the second seal, let us say a few things about these. In Genesis it is written that God said to Noah: Make yourself an ark of smoothed woods; you shall make little dwellings in the ark, and you shall coat it with pitch inside and outside, and thus you shall make it. The length of the ark shall be three hundred cubits, its breadth fifty cubits, and its height thirty cubits (Gen. 6:14–15). The ark signifies the Church; and Noah, the builder of the ark, prefigured Christ, the builder of the Church. This ark is described as having been made from smoothed woods: by the smoothed woods, which both kept out the waters and preserved the life of all the animals placed within it, are signified the teachers of the Church, who by their teaching and their writings prevent the waters of various errors from entering the Church, and by their teaching and examples preserve the life of those who are within the Church. They are said to be smoothed, that is, cleansed from every roughness of vices. By the little dwellings, moreover, which were commanded to be made in the ark, the different orders in the Church are signified; for one person, as it were, dwells in one dwelling, another in another: one in the married state, another in the life of chastity, another in the monastic life, another in the priestly office, while they strive to serve God. By the pitch, however, with which it is said to have been coated, Holy Scripture is signified. Therefore the planks from which the ark was made, which we have said signify the teachers of the Church, were coated with pitch inside and outside; because the teachers of the Church are coated inside by the wisdom and teaching of Holy Scripture, and outside by works of virtue.

However, the length of the ark is described as three hundred cubits, its width as fifty cubits, and its height as thirty cubits. Therefore, through the length of the ark faith is prefigured, through the width love, and through the height hope. Therefore, the length of the ark is extended to three hundred cubits, because our faith consists in the confession of the Holy Trinity; moreover, the number one hundred is a perfect number. So in three hundred cubits our faith is foreshown, because the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit have equal perfection, as it is written: Such as the Father is, such is the Son, such is the Holy Spirit (Athanasian Creed).

Nevertheless, its width is extended to fifty cubits, because faith works through love. But it is not unfitting that love is represented by the fifty cubits of width; for seven times seven makes forty-nine, and if you add one to this number, you find fifty. Therefore love of neighbor is not inappropriately contained in the number seven, because it cannot be brought to completion without the seven virtues. Now in seven times seven, each believer extends love of neighbor, in that, by whatever ways needs or adversities can happen to neighbors, by those same ways he himself stands ready to come to their aid: for example, when he provides what is necessary to the hungry, the naked, the stranger, the needy, the one placed in prison; when he consoles the sorrowful, when he moderates the angry with a word, when he brings peace to the one who bears hatred in his heart, and leads the quarrelsome back to peace; when he rouses the one lying in sins to repentance; when he strives to draw with himself to eternal life all whom he can; and, to speak briefly, by whatever ways he wishes that someone else would help him in a wholesome manner, by those same ways he himself, as far as he is able, is ready to give help. But if you add one to the seven times seven, they become fifty; likewise, if you add love of the one God to love of neighbor, then all love is brought about in you as whole and perfect.

But the height of the ark was raised to thirty cubits, because he possesses a beneficial hope who does not disagree, either in deed or in belief, with the faith of the Holy Trinity as it has been handed down to us by the apostles, and with the other teachings. For whoever claims to himself hope of obtaining eternal life without right faith undoubtedly deceives himself, because without faith it is impossible to please God (Heb. 11:6). Therefore the height of the ark arose from its great length and width, and was brought to completion upward in a cubit; because love, which is born from faith, extends itself in great width in caring for one’s neighbors. It is, however, brought to completion upward in a cubit, because all the hope and desire of the faithful tends toward the vision of the one God.

But that Noah, at the Lord’s command, brought into the ark from all the clean and unclean animals males and females (Gen. 7:8), signifies that the evil and the good would be brought into the Church. But of the clean animals there entered the ark seven and seven; of the unclean, however, two and two, male and female. For by the clean animals the elect are signified, and by the unclean the wicked. Therefore the clean animals are set in the number seven, because only those are elect who, through the grace of the Holy Spirit, are not estranged from the exercise of the gifts of that same Holy Spirit; but the wicked are set in the number two, because they exist in the Church only in body and soul, but in mind they are outside the Church.

In the following it is also written: When Noah opened the window of the ark that he had made, he sent out a raven, which went out and did not return, until the waters were dried up from upon the earth (Gen. 8:6). By the raven, a very black bird, the Jews, darkened by unbelief, are indicated. Therefore Noah sent the raven from the ark, because our Lord Jesus Christ separated the Jewish people from the fellowship of the holy Church. After the raven he sent out a dove, which, when it had not found where its foot might rest, returned to him into the ark. A second time he sent the dove from the ark, and it came to him in the evening, carrying a branch of olive with green leaves in its mouth. A third time he sent out the dove, and it did not return to him again. By the dove the Holy Spirit is often prefigured, since in the form of a dove he descended upon the Lord (Luke 3:22).

But we must inquire what the three sendings out of the dove and its two returns signify. The three sendings signify three periods: the first before the Law, the second under the Law, the third under grace. In this passage, Noah represents God, while the ark signifies the heavenly dwelling of God. Noah first sent the dove out of the ark, because God almighty gave the grace of the Holy Spirit to the holy men who lived before the Law; and although they did not have the Law, they fulfilled the divine teachings that were later given, through the gift of the Holy Spirit. But the dove did not find where her foot could rest, because not only the other nations, but even the Jews themselves, settled in Egypt, abandoned the worship of God to such an extent that at the time when Moses was born, hardly anyone could be found in whom the Holy Spirit would consent to dwell. And in a certain sense, the grace of the Holy Spirit returned to Him by whom it had been sent, when it abandoned sinful humans.

Secondly, Noah sent out a dove from the ark; for God gave the grace of the Holy Spirit to men who keep the Law with a pure heart. Now the evening when the dove returned to Noah signifies the time when Christ was born. Of this time the Lord says in the Gospel: “The Law and the prophets were until John” (Luke 16:16); because after the Gospel began to be preached, circumcision and many legal observances were of no benefit. As long as the observance of the Law pleased God, so long did the grace of the Holy Spirit accompany the men who kept the Law. And just as the dove returned to Noah in the evening, so too the grace of the Holy Spirit, leaving the unbelieving Jews, in a certain sense returned to Him from whom it had been sent. By the olive branch we can understand, or take, the Law; and by the green leaves, those who were saved through the Law. Therefore the dove carried the olive branch with green leaves in its mouth when the grace of the Holy Spirit, in a certain sense, offered to God those whom it had saved through the Law.

Thirdly, Noah sent the dove out from the ark, when he gave the grace of the Holy Spirit to those who keep faith in the Gospel. She did not return to him again, because the faith of the Gospel will remain with the elect until, when the Lord comes for judgment, the elect who are found in this world will be caught up before the Lord in the air, and therefore will always be with the Lord (1 Thess. 4:16).

But to demonstrate more clearly the opening of the second seal, let us say a few words about the three patriarchs. It is written in Genesis that God tested Abraham and said to him: Take your son Isaac, whom you love, and go to the land of vision, and there you will offer him as a burnt offering on one of the mountains that I will tell you of (Gen. 22:2). Abraham, rising up during the night, saddled his donkey and took with him two youths and Isaac his son. Abraham signifies Christ according to his divinity; Isaac signifies the man whom Christ assumed; and by the donkey the apostles are signified. Abraham therefore rose up by night, because the Son of God, when the world lay in the darkness of ignorance, consented to be born of the Virgin Mary. He saddled his donkey when he handed down to his apostles the commands of the Gospel. By the two youths are signified the two commandments of love. He took the two youths with him, because he impressed upon his disciples the two commandments of love more strictly and more frequently. He also took his Son, because the man whom he assumed he handed over to die for humanity, just as he himself said in the Gospel to his disciples: Behold, we are going up to Jerusalem, and all things that have been written by the prophets about the Son of Man will be fulfilled. For he will be handed over to the nations, and will be mocked and scourged and spat upon; and after they have scourged him, they will kill him, and on the third day he will rise again (Luke 18:31-33).

But on the third day, when he lifted up his eyes, he saw the place from afar. Three days signify three decades of years, and three times two months, by which spans of time Christ is said to have lived in the world. The place, moreover, foreshadowed Jerusalem, where Christ suffered. But why did he say that he saw the place from afar, since he preached in the temple every day? Because although the Jews were physically near, yet on account of their unbelief they had withdrawn far from him.

And Abraham said to his servants: Wait here with the donkey; I and the boy will go over there in a hurry, and after we have worshiped, we will return to you (Gen. 22:5). In like manner Christ also ordered his disciples to wait until, having risen, he would return to them, when he said: You will all fall away because of me this night; but after I have risen, I will go before you into Galilee (Matt. 26:32). And when he was arrested, he said to the Jews: So if you seek me, let these men go (Jn. 18:8).

Abraham therefore took the wood for the burnt offering and laid it on Isaac his son; but he himself carried in his hands the fire and the sword. We have said above that our sins were signified by the wood; therefore Isaac carried the wood, because Christ bore our sins in his body on the tree, so that, having died to sins, we might live to righteousness. By the hands of Abraham, moreover, the power of our Lord Jesus Christ is understood; for it was in his power to die and not to die, if he willed, as he himself says: I have authority to lay down my life, and I have authority to take it up again (Jn. 10:18). By the fire, however, the Passion is understood, and by the sword, his death.

When he had come to the place, he built an altar and arranged the wood on top of it. And when he had bound Isaac his son, he placed him on the altar, upon the pile of wood. By the altar the cross is understood: therefore the Son of God built the cross, on which the man whom he had taken on would be sacrificed; because it was done with his permission. On the altar indeed he arranged the wood, and on the wood he placed Isaac his bound son; for that which we wish to suffocate, we place beneath ourselves, so that it may be suffocated and destroyed by our weight: in like manner our Redeemer, hanging on the cross, placed beneath himself the wood, that is, our sins; so that by his weight and power they might be suffocated and wiped out. Moreover, the bonds of Isaac foreshadowed the Lord’s affliction on the cross. But the things that the angel spoke to Abraham do not refer to this interpretation.

Abraham lifted up his eyes and saw a ram caught by its horns in a thicket. Taking it, he offered it as a burnt offering instead of his son. The ram prefigured Christ: through the two horns, the two commandments of love are signified; through the thicket, the Jews. Therefore Christ was caught among the thicket by his horns, because, loving the Jews as brothers and knowing that the calling of the Gentiles would mean the loss of the Jews, he did not at first wish to entrust the faith of the Gospel to the Gentiles, but sending his disciples to preach, he commanded them, saying: “Do not go into the way of the Gentiles, and do not enter into the cities of the Samaritans; but go rather to the lost sheep of the house of Israel” (Matt. 10:5). And to the Canaanite woman he said: “I was not sent except to the sheep that have perished of the house of Israel” (Matt. 15:24); and again: “It is not good to take the children’s bread and throw it to the dogs” (Matt. 15:26).

But Abraham took a ram from the thicket and offered it as a burnt offering instead of his son; because the Father separated the Son, and the Son himself separated himself from the love of the wicked Jews, and offered the man whom he assumed as a burnt offering for the salvation of humanity.

But concerning this same Isaac it is written that he had two sons, Esau and Jacob. Esau was a hunter and a man who worked the land; Jacob, however, was simple and lived in tents. By Esau the Hebrew people are represented, and by Jacob the people of the nations. Isaac, as father of both, held the figure of the Lord, who made both peoples his children: that one through the Law, and this one through faith. Esau was a man of the soil, because the people of the Jews served God for earthly advantages. For it was not said to them: If you are poor in spirit, if gentle, if peacemakers, if merciful, if you endure persecutions for my name’s sake, you will be blessed, and your reward will be great in heaven; but it was said to them: If you keep my commandments and observe my judgments, I will give you rain in its seasons, and the earth will bring forth its produce, and the trees will be filled with fruit, and the other things that refer to the pleasures of this age (Lev. 26:3). Esau was also a hunter, because he was accustomed to appease God by the shedding of the blood of rams, goats, and calves. Jacob, however, was simple and lived in tents, because those chosen from the nations have sought to please God by faith alone and by good will. By the tents we can understand either the various churches in which throughout the whole world the one catholic Church is divided, or the various orders.

Isaac loved Esau because he ate the food of his hunting; but Rebecca loved Jacob. Isaac, as we have said, in this passage holds the type of the Lord; through Rebecca, however, we can understand the foreknowledge of God. Isaac therefore loved Esau because he ate the food of his hunting, since God loved the Jewish people because he was pleased by their continual offerings; but the mother loved Jacob, because God, knowing through the grace of his foreknowledge that the people of the nations would be far better than the Jewish people, loved him much more. While Esau was delayed in hunting, so that he might bring his father food from wild animals and thereby receive a blessing from him, the mother brought Jacob to his father to receive the blessing; for while the Jewish people were devoted to the observances of the Law and came more slowly to the reception of faith in Christ, the mother, grace, offered the people of the nations to God to receive the blessing that was owed to the Jews, because they had embraced faith in Christ with eagerness. How the very people of the nations merited this gift of blessing is attested in figure by the deeds of Jacob, which are described as having been done in order to receive the blessing.

So Rebecca said to her son Jacob: Bring me two of the best young goats, and I will make from them dishes for your father, such as he likes to eat; and he will bless you before the Lord, before he dies (Gen. 27:9). He went and brought them, and gave them to his mother. She prepared the food, as she knew his father liked it, and she dressed him in Esau’s clothes, which she had at home with her; and she wrapped the skins of the young goats around his hands and covered his bare neck, and she gave him the stew and the bread she had baked, and handed them over. By the two young goats we can understand the confession of the sins of the Gentile people and repentance. Jacob therefore offered two excellent young goats to his mother, because through confession of sins and repentance, which is the way to faith for those who are going to it, the Gentile people hurried to receive that same faith. These young goats are said to have been excellent, because the repentance of a sinner together with renunciation of vices is very pleasing to God, just as our Lord himself says in the Gospel: There is joy in heaven over one sinner who repents, more than over ninety-nine righteous persons who do not need repentance (Luke 15:7); meaning by the one sinner the people of the nations, and by the ninety-nine righteous, the Jews. They are called righteous for this reason, because on account of their observance of the Law they falsely thought themselves righteous; for the Lord speaks to these same righteous, saying: Woe to you who justify yourselves before men, but God knows your hearts (Luke 16:15). They are set in an imperfect number, because the Law brought no one to perfection (Heb. 7:19).

But by the delightful foods that were prepared for Isaac to be taken from the two kids, we must understand works of penance. John the Baptist speaks of these foods when he says: “Produce fruit worthy of repentance” (Matt. 3:8). By the very fine garments, however, which Esau, that is, the Jews, had left at home, we can understand the excellent teachings of the Law, such as this one: “You shall love the Lord your God with all your heart and with all your soul, and your neighbor as yourself” (Deut. 6:5), and many others, without which no one can be righteous. These are the finest garments which the Jews left at home, that is, in the Old Testament, because they were unwilling to observe them. Clothed and adorned with these, the Gentile people went to God the Father with the food of good works. And together with the foods he also brought breads. By bread the word of God is often signified, because that person fittingly goes to God the Father with the food of good works who also does not cease to admonish all others he can to perform good works.

But we must also ask why the mother covered the hands of Jacob and his bare neck with goatskins. For when the skin is removed from a young goat, the flesh is exposed, from which tasty foods can be prepared; in the same way, when the vices are taken away from any sinner, the good of nature, together with reason, becomes visible, and if this is boiled with faith, delightful food is prepared for God from the good of nature. By the neck, therefore, because pride often shows itself in the neck, we can understand the loftiness of the mind; by the hands, however, works are indicated. Therefore each faithful person covers his neck with goatskins when, in order to lay aside the loftiness of the mind and to guard his humility, he does not cease to call his past sins back before the eyes of his mind. He covers his hands when, so that he may not be exalted in good works, he often brings his past sins back before the eyes of his mind.

After Isaac had taken food, Jacob also offered him wine. If the works of the active life are signified by food, we can understand by wine, which alienates the minds of men, the contemplative life, which renders the minds of certain of the elect insensitive to love of the world. Therefore, after food and drink had been taken, Isaac blessed Jacob; for those receive the blessing of the heavenly homeland from God who strive to please him with good works until the end of their life.

The same Jacob, when he was fleeing from Esau his brother, came to a certain place; and when he had put a stone under his head, he slept there. In his sleep he saw a ladder at his head, set up from the earth all the way to heaven, and the Lord leaning upon the ladder, and also the angels of God ascending and descending on it (Gen. 28:12). By the stone Christ is understood; indeed our foundation is Christ, of whom the Apostle says: For no one can lay another foundation besides that which has been laid, which is Christ Jesus (1 Cor. 3:11). Therefore we place our head upon the stone if, from good works, we build our wall upon Christ.

The vision of the ladder agrees with this meaning; for the two larger pieces of wood of the ladder, which are on each side and are raised from the lower parts to the upper, signify the two precepts of love. By the steps, however, which are inserted crosswise on each side into those two pieces of wood, the other virtues are signified. And just as the two pieces of wood cannot be held together without the steps, nor the steps without the two pieces of wood, so love cannot exist without the other virtues, nor the other virtues without love. And just as the various steps, unless they are inserted on each side into the two larger pieces of wood, cannot provide ascent for people, so the other virtues, unless they are born from love of God and love of neighbor, cannot provide ascent for those who wish to go to heaven, as the apostle John says: “For whoever does not love his brother whom he sees, how can he love God whom he does not see?” (1 Jn. 4:20). And just as the ladder was seen to touch the earth on one side and heaven on the other, so love is joined to our lower neighbors and to God who is above. Therefore also the angels of God were shown ascending and descending upon it; because the holy ones of God, who are signified by angels, are sometimes lifted up to the heights by love of God through the grace of contemplation, and sometimes brought back down to the lower things by love and care of neighbors. Moreover, he saw the Lord leaning upon the ladder; because whoever strives to ascend to the Lord by the path of the virtues, after they have completed the journey of their way, find the Lord, the author of all good things and the giver of rewards, with whom they will rejoice without end.

But when this same Jacob was returning from Mesopotamia of Syria and had come to the Jordan, after he had led across all that belonged to him, he remained alone. And behold, a man wrestled with him until morning. And when he saw that he could not overcome him, he touched the muscle of his thigh, and it immediately withered, and he said to him: Let me go, for now the dawn has risen. But he answered: I will not let you go unless you bless me. And he said to him: What is your name? He answered him: Jacob. And he said: You shall no longer be called Jacob, but Israel shall be your name (Gen. 32:26). In this passage, by “Jacob” any sinner and penitent is signified. That the angel wrestled with Jacob in the figure of the Lord, and he with the angel, signifies what the Lord says in the Gospel: The kingdom of heaven suffers violence, and the violent seize it (Matt. 11:12). But who are the violent, if not sinners and penitents? When they see that, on account of their crimes, they are guilty of everlasting death, they knock at the gate of paradise with their prayers so long until the entrance to the heavenly kingdom is opened to them. Jacob therefore wrestles with the Lord when any sinner, fearing the punishments of eternal fire, perseveres in prayer until divine mercy comes to his aid, as the following makes clear.

When he saw that he could not overcome him, he touched the muscle of his thigh, and it immediately withered. And since lust is born from the thigh, through the muscle of the thigh we can understand carnal pleasures. Therefore the Lord touched the muscle of the sinner’s thigh when he extinguished all carnal desires from which sins arise; for it is impossible for us to abstain from sins as long as we lie dissolved in carnal pleasures. Therefore the angel said to him, that is, the one who wrestled with him in the person of the Lord: “Let me go, for now the dawn has risen” (Gen. 32:26); as if he had said: “Cease now to weep for fear of eternal fire; for now peace has returned, with vices extinguished, which had plunged you into the pit of destruction.”

And because it is not enough to abandon evil unless we also strive to do good, it continues: “And Jacob said: I will not let you go unless you bless me,” that is, I will not cease from prayers unless you enrich me with virtues; so that I, who until now have lived according to the flesh, may from now on live according to the spirit. And he said to him: “What is your name?” He answered him, “Jacob”; and he said to him: “You shall no longer be called Jacob, but Israel shall be your name,” that is: You shall no longer be called the supplanter of vices, but you shall be called a man who sees God; and you who formerly were weeping lest you be plunged into hell [infernum], now will weep because for some time you are kept far from the vision of almighty God, saying with the Psalmist: “Alas for me, because my sojourning has been prolonged” (Ps. 119:5)!

Likewise Jacob also, after the struggle that he had with the Angel, is said to have limped with one foot: but the one who limps leans his whole weight on the good foot, while the other touches the ground only for a short while. Therefore the two feet signify two kinds of nourishment; for there is nourishment of the body, and there is nourishment of the soul. The nourishment of the body is food, drink, clothing; but the nourishment of the soul is the word of God and good works. Therefore each believer, if with one foot he touches the earth for a short time, leans entirely on the other; because he uses the things of the world only to relieve the needs of the body, as the Apostle says: “But having food and clothing, let us be content with these” (1 Tim. 6:8). But in those things that refer to the salvation of the soul, he throws himself wholly; lest he should ever turn aside from the way of God, which is the salvation of the soul. To explain the opening of the second seal we have said a few things out of many; now let us pursue the sequence. Next

Revelation 6:3b I heard the second living creature saying, “Come and see”;

That is, understand in a spiritual way what you have come to know was done by the patriarchs.

Revelation 6:4 And behold, a red horse; and the one who sat on it was given power to take peace from the earth, so that people should kill one another. And a great sword was given to him.

By the red horse are signified the righteous who lived after the Flood up to the time of the Law. And because the red color comes somewhat close to the golden color, as if you were to mix a blood-red kind with gold, it is not unfitting that the holy men who lived before the Law are compared to this color: in that they shone with wisdom, which is figured by gold, and were unwavering in persecutions and adversity, which are figured by blood. The rider of this horse, however, is the Lord, who dwells in his saints. To this rider it was given to take peace from the earth. If good peace is from God, how was it taken from the earth? But there is good peace and there is bad peace. Bad peace the sons of God had with the sons of men before the Flood, when they took their daughters and gave their own daughters to them (Gen. 6:1); and therefore they were wiped out with them by the waters of the Flood. And the Lord also commanded the sons of Israel not to make a covenant with the Canaanites, nor to take their daughters, nor to give their own daughters to them (Exod. 23:32). For the Psalmist speaks of good and bad peace, saying: “With the chosen you will be chosen, and with the perverse you will be perverted” (Ps. 17:27). This peace, then, God took from the earth when he separated righteous men from the wicked in mind, not in body. “So that they should kill one another.” From those whom peace is taken away, it follows that one kills another; for often the righteous kill the unrighteous, and the unrighteous kill the righteous. Thus Aram, Abraham’s brother, according to the tradition of the Jews, died in the fire of the Chaldeans because he refused to worship the fire; thus Abraham killed the four kings with their armies for their wickedness; thus the sons of Jacob, because of the violation of their sister, killed all the Shechemites. By the great sword we can understand the waters of the Flood, by which the whole world was destroyed. We can also understand by the great sword the destruction of the five cities, whose inhabitants perished by fire and brimstone, and whose land was sunk into the abyss along with them.

Revelation 6:5a And when he had opened the third seal.

The opening of the third seal pertains to the Law and to those who were under the Law. To show, therefore, the opening of the third seal, it has seemed good to say a few things about Moses and about those matters in the Law that appear to be irrational. In Exodus it is written that when Moses was born, he was hidden for three months by his parents; afterward his mother made for him a little basket of reeds, coated it with tar and pitch, and after placing the infant inside, set him among the reeds at the riverbank (Exod. 2:2-3). Moses prefigured Christ; and by the three months during which Moses was hidden, three periods of time are indicated: one before the flood; the second from after the flood up to Moses; the third from Moses up to the coming of the Lord. In these three periods the coming of Christ according to the flesh was hidden from men; yet it was foretold by the perfect. By the little basket of reeds the blessed Virgin Mary is signified: the mother therefore prepared the little basket of reeds in which Moses was to be placed, because the wisdom of God, which is the Son of God, chose the blessed and glorious Mary, ever virgin, in whose unaltered womb he would form the man to whom he would be joined by unity of person. By the tar, which cannot be dissolved by water, we can understand the virginity of blessed Mary, which could not be violated by any carnal heat; and by the pitch, which is the guardian of wine, humility, the guardian of the other virtues. Of this same humility she speaks, saying: “Because he has regarded the humility of his handmaid; for behold, from now on all generations will call me blessed” (Luke 1:48). By the river the world is signified; by the reeds in which Moses was placed, the Jews are signified; for later it is read that he was found in a papyrus basket by the daughter of Pharaoh. The reeds in this passage have the same meaning as the papyrus; for reeds are accustomed to wound the hands that touch them, while from papyrus in ancient times books used to be made. By the reeds, therefore, the Jews are signified, who killed the prophets, namely, spiritual physicians who wished to heal their wounds. The same people are also signified by the papyrus, because they boasted in the Scriptures handed down to them by God, which they read but did not keep. The daughter of Pharaoh, that is, of the Devil, prefigured the people from among the nations. The daughter of Pharaoh therefore took Moses up from the papyrus (Exod. 2:5); because the Church from the nations, receiving faith in Christ, took that same Christ away from the Jews who were unwilling to believe.

Afterwards, when that same Moses was pasturing the sheep of Jethro his kinsman, the Lord appeared to him in a flame of fire from a bush, and he saw that the bush was burning and was not being consumed (Exod. 3:2). By the bush which produced flames from itself and was not consumed, we can understand the blessed Virgin Mary, who brought forth the Son of God from her womb and did not lose her virginity; but because of the things that follow, I judge that by the bush Christ is to be understood, inasmuch as he is man. The flame of the bush, however, prefigured his divinity. Therefore the bush was burning and was not consumed, because when the Son of God assumed man, just as his divinity was not changed into humanity so as to lose what it was, so also his humanity, burning by divinity, was not so absorbed by divinity as to lose humanity; but from two substances, of humanity and of deity, one Christ remained and remains, as it is written: perfect God, perfect man, subsisting from a rational soul and human flesh (Athanasius in the Creed).

And the Lord spoke to Moses from the midst of the flame of the bush: Do not come near here; take the shoes from your feet, for the place where you stand is holy ground. I have surely seen the affliction of my people who are in Egypt, and I have heard their groaning, and I have come down to deliver them. But come, I will send you to Pharaoh, that you may lead the sons of Israel out of the land of Egypt (Exod. 3:5-10). And a little later: And Moses said to the Lord: Who am I, that I should go to Pharaoh, and lead the sons of Israel out of Egypt (Exod. 3:11)? And in what follows he speaks to the Lord about the sons of Israel, saying: They will not listen to my voice, but they will say: The Lord has not appeared to you (Exod. 4:1); and again: I beg you, Lord, send whom you will send (Exod. 4:13). These words show that Moses obeyed the commands of the Lord reluctantly, to lead the sons of Israel out of the land of Egypt; but we must inquire why so great a man carried out the command of the Lord unwillingly. Indeed, we must understand this stubbornness of his in a spiritual sense; for the Apostle says: Now all these things happened to them as a figure; and they were written for our correction, upon whom the ends of the ages have come (1 Cor. 10:11). By Moses, therefore, in this passage the apostles are signified; for at the beginning of the preaching of the Gospel the apostles, knowing that the election of the Gentiles was the rejection of the Jews, did not wish to impart to the Gentiles the teaching of the Gospel, so that they might be rescued from the power of the Devil. Therefore also the Lord, in a vision of four-footed animals and creeping things, rebuked Peter for despising the Gentiles, saying: What God has cleansed, do not call common (Acts 10:15). To this sense, then, belongs that which the Lord said to Moses: Do not come near here; take the shoes from your feet, for the place where you stand is holy ground (Exod. 3:5), as if the Lord had said to one of the great apostles: Even if you raise the dead, even if by the shadow of your body you heal the sick, do not dare in any respect to compare yourself with me; for the great things that you do are not from you, but I work through you. Now shoes protect the feet so that they do not touch the ground; by “ground,” in fact, the Gentiles are signified. Remove, he says, the shoes from your feet; that is, take away hatred and contempt of the Gentiles from your heart. For the place where you stand is holy ground: that is, the world in which you dwell has been redeemed by my blood.

And in the following verses the Lord said to Moses: What is that you hold in your hand? And he said: A rod. He said to him: Cast it on the ground. When he had cast it, it was turned into a serpent, so that Moses fled; and the Lord said to him: Take it by the tail. When he had taken hold of it, it was turned into a rod (Exod. 4:2). By the rod the Jews are signified. Moses held the rod in his hand, because the apostles held the Jewish people in their hearts through love, and with all their strength they labored to lead them to salvation through the teaching of their preaching. Moses cast the rod from his hand, because the apostles, seeing that the Jews in no way wanted to believe in Christ, cast their love for them out of their hearts. By the serpent, however, the Devil is signified. The rod was therefore turned into a serpent, because the Jews became worse through the preaching of the apostles; those who were formerly called the people of God were made sons and members of the Devil. Moses fled, because the apostles, leaving the Jews behind, passed over to the Gentiles. By the tail, which is the end of the body, the Jews who are to be born at the end of the world are signified. Moses therefore took hold of the serpent’s tail, and it was turned into a rod; because holy preachers at the end of the world will in a certain way lay hold of the Jews, when through their teaching they are converted to faith in Christ, and from children of the Devil they are made children of God.

For that event too, when at the Lord’s command Moses put his hand into his chest and drew it out leprous, and then put it in again and it was made healthy, has the same meaning: by the hand of Moses the Jews are signified. Moses put his hand into his chest, because the Apostles, desiring the salvation of the Jews, did not cease to preach to them the doctrine of faith. Moses drew back his hand and found it leprous, because the Jews, made much worse by the preaching of the apostles, fell into the most grievous leprosy of unbelief and blasphemies. Moses again put his hand into his chest and drew it back healthy, because in the end of the world the holy preachers will receive the Jews into their heart, will preach to them faith in Christ, and will restore them to the health which they had lost.

In what follows it is also written that when Moses was returning to Egypt, the Lord met him and wanted to put him to death. At once Zipporah took a very sharp stone and circumcised the flesh of her son’s foreskin, and touched his feet and said: “You are a bridegroom of blood to me” (Exod. 4:25). And He let him go, after she had said: “You are a bridegroom of blood to me, because of the circumcision.” If the Lord wanted to kill Moses, why did He not do what He wanted, since the Psalmist says: “Whatever the Lord willed, He has done” (Ps. 134:6)? But “will” is often used in place of “threat”: the Lord therefore threatened Moses with death because he was carrying a son who was uncircumcised, just as He speaks to Abraham, saying: “Every soul among you that shall not have been circumcised shall perish from among its people” (Gen. 17:14). But leaving these things aside, let us return to spiritual understanding. In this passage Moses represents those who have believed from among the nations; for at the beginning of the faith the apostles and the other preachers, considering the weakness of the nations that were receiving faith in Christ, did not wish at first to hand on to them the stricter teachings of Christ, lest, frightened, they would not dare to come to the acceptance of faith in Christ. The apostle Paul shows that this is so, as he speaks by letter to those who had believed from the nations, saying: “As to little children in Christ I gave you milk to drink, not solid food; for you were not yet able, nor indeed are you even now able; for you are still carnal” (1 Cor. 3:2). And the apostles also gave orders to those who had believed in Christ at Antioch, saying: “It has seemed good to the Holy Spirit and to us to place upon you no burden except that you abstain from foods sacrificed to idols, and from fornication, and from what is strangled, and from blood.” (Acts 15:28–29) The Lord therefore threatened Moses with death because he was neglecting to circumcise the flesh of his son’s foreskin: for to those who believed from the nations, because they were lukewarm in love, the Lord threatened death, when He set before them the teachings of His Gospel, where it is written: “Narrow and constricted is the way that leads to life, and few there are who go by it. Broad and spacious is the way that leads to death, and many there are who go by it” (Matt. 7:14); and again: “Whoever does not carry his cross and follow me is not worthy of me” (Luke 14:27); and many other things that are found in the Gospel and in apostolic sayings concerning the cutting away of vices.

By the rock, however, Christ is understood, and the sharp point of the rock is the hard teachings of the Gospel. Therefore Zipporah took a very sharp stone and circumcised the flesh of her son’s foreskin; because the Church, seeing that her sons can in no way attain perfect salvation unless all carnal desires are rooted out from their bodies, took the stricter teachings of the Gospel and cut away from the bodies of her sons all nourishment of vices. Now in the feet is the end of the body: therefore, after the circumcision, Zipporah touched her son’s feet; because he receives saving circumcision who perseveres in the good he has begun until the end of life. But after Zipporah touched her son’s feet, she no longer calls him son but bridegroom; now the bridegroom of the Church is Christ. And she herself is called his bride, as John the Baptist says: He who has the bride is the bridegroom (Jn. 3:29). And she calls him a bridegroom of blood, because she has been redeemed by the blood of Christ. Therefore the sons of the Church, after the circumcision of vices and the completion of life in good works, are now called not sons but bridegrooms; because through the joining of members they are united in heaven to him who on earth is called, and indeed is, the bridegroom of the Church.

And since we cannot recount everything that was done in Egypt through Moses, let us set down a few of the things that were done by them after the departure of the people of Israel from Egypt. The sons of Israel, in order to escape Pharaoh and the Egyptians, passed through the midst of the sea on dry ground, and the waters were for them like a wall on the right and on the left (Exod. 14:22). Likewise, the people of the nations, in order to escape the Devil and all his followers, passed through the water of baptism. And those who previously were children of the Devil, by water and the Holy Spirit, who was prefigured by the pillar of fire, having been reborn, were made children of God. Therefore, the water of the sea saved the sons of Israel, but it slew Pharaoh with all his followers; because the water of baptism saves the image of God and extinguishes the sins which had been served. The Devil, however, is extinguished for each person who faithfully renounces him with all his pomps. And the waters that were for them like a wall on the right and on the left signified our faith, which we received in baptism, which is our wall, defending us on both sides, both from invisible enemies and from visible ones.

After crossing the Red Sea they came to Marah, where they found bitter waters which they could not drink. And the Lord showed Moses a piece of wood which, when he had thrown it into the waters, they were turned into sweetness (Exod. 15:23). By the bitter waters the Law of Moses is signified; by the wood, however, the passion of Christ, since it was fulfilled upon the wood of the cross, as we should understand. The people therefore who came out of Egypt found bitter waters, because they received the Law, which they could not fulfill; but when the nations came to faith in Christ, the bitterness of the Law was turned into sweetness through the passion and resurrection of Christ, because it was understood by them spiritually, not carnally. That event also, in which at Elim they found twelve springs and pitched their camp beside the waters (Exod. 15:27), has the same meaning; for by the waters of the twelve springs the teaching of the New Testament is signified, which flowed forth into the whole world from the twelve springs, that is, from the twelve apostles; and by the seventy palm trees the Old Testament is signified, because through the translation of the Septuagint translators it came to the knowledge of the nations.

Nevertheless, leaving these things aside, let us say a few words about those matters which in the Law seem to be irrational. The Lord commanded the sons of Israel: You shall not cook a young goat in the milk of its mother (Exod. 23:19); and again: You shall not plow with an ox and a donkey together; and you shall not wear a garment that is woven of wool and linen together (Deut. 22:10). For what sin does someone commit if he cooks a young goat in its mother’s milk? Or in what way does a farmer do wrong if he tills the earth with oxen and donkeys together pulling the plow? Or how does anyone offend if he wears a garment woven of wool and linen together? But all these things must be understood spiritually; for by the young goat every sinner is signified. Our first parents, from whom we all have been begotten, were our mother; and the mother’s milk is the vices which have arisen from original sin; for if original sin had not existed, there would be no vice. Of this mother’s milk the Psalmist speaks, saying: For behold, I was conceived in iniquities, and in sins did my mother bear me (Ps. 50:7). Therefore the young goat is cooked in its mother’s milk when any sinner is consumed by the fire of vices right up to the day of his death. Therefore the Lord commanded that the young goat should not be cooked in its mother’s milk, that is, that the sinner should not be allowed to remain in his sins until the day of his death, but before the day of his death he should be called back to the performance of good works, lest he perish.

You shall not plow with an ox and a donkey together (Deut. 22:10). By the ox we can understand the apostles and the other preachers; by the donkey we can understand the people of the Jews. We truly plow when, according to the tradition of the saints, we exercise ourselves in good works. Therefore, whoever tries to keep at the same time the teaching of the apostles and the traditions of the Jews, plows with an ox and a donkey together. From this teaching the Apostle Paul tried to call the Galatians back when he said: O foolish Galatians! Who has bewitched you, that you should not obey the truth? For I tell you that if you are circumcised, Christ will be of no benefit to you (Gal. 3:1; 5:2). We can also understand by the ox any wise preacher, and by the donkey a foolish one. Therefore it is commanded that one shall not plow with an ox and a donkey together, that is, that a fool must not be joined to a wise man in the office of preaching. But this which he says: You shall not wear a garment woven of wool and linen together (Deut. 22:11), we can understand according to the previous meaning. For by wool, which is the clothing of sheep, we can understand the teaching of the apostles; by linen, which comes forth from the earth, we can understand the teaching of the Jews. We put on a garment when we surround ourselves with good works; but a person uses a garment woven of wool and linen who wants to observe the traditions of the Jews together with the traditions of the apostles.

The Lord also commanded Moses that if any leper were cleansed, he should come to the priest and offer two sparrows to the priest. Of these the priest, sacrificing one, should let its blood run down into living waters; and dipping the living sparrow into the waters into which he had made the blood of the sacrificed sparrow run down, together with cedar wood, scarlet wool, and hyssop, he should sprinkle the leper seven times, and then he would be duly cleansed (Lev. 14:1–7). By the priest Christ is understood; and by the two sparrows the teachers of the two Testaments are understood. By the living waters, moreover, the teachings of the Gospel are indicated, of which the Lord says in the Gospel: “Whoever believes in me, as the Scripture says, rivers of living water will flow from his belly” (Jn. 7:38). He calls them living waters because they grant eternal life to those who do them. By the sparrow therefore that was sacrificed, as we have said, the teachers of the Jews are signified, namely the priests, the scribes, and the Pharisees. The blood, however, which gave life to the sparrow, is spiritual understanding, which lay hidden in the observances of the Jews. And as a sparrow without blood is rendered dead, so also many observances of the Jews, if spiritual understanding were lacking, would appear to be vain. After the blood of the sparrow appeared and ran down into the living waters, the sparrow died; because after the spiritual understanding, which lay hidden in the observances of the Jews, was revealed through the grace of our Lord Jesus Christ, certain legal observances were of no benefit; and even those who, for the sake of keeping the Law, rejected faith in Christ are dead in soul. The blood of the sparrow, then, is mixed with the living waters; because all Scripture that refers to the New Testament is filled with examples of the Old Testament spiritually understood. By the cedar wood the Father is signified; by the hyssop, the Son; and by the scarlet wool, which has the brightness of fire, the Holy Spirit is signified. With these three the one who wished to be duly cleansed was sprinkled; because no one can be cleansed from the leprosy of sins through the water of baptism, except under the call of the Father and the Son and the Holy Spirit.

But if through these three the Father and the Son and the Holy Spirit are signified, why is a living sparrow added as a fourth? For we have said that through the living sparrow the preachers of the New Testament were signified. Therefore the priest added the living sparrow to the cedar wood, the scarlet, and the hyssop, because our Lord Jesus Christ, in the call of his name, and of the Father and of the Holy Spirit, joins to this the ministry of priests, and washes us from our sins, represented by the leper, through their petition and through the water of baptism. This sparrow is commanded to be dipped in living waters, because whoever teaches others must be instructed in the divine Scriptures; for if a blind man tries to lead a blind man, both fall into a pit (Matt. 15:14). The leper is ordered to be sprinkled seven times, because no cleansing can be perfect without the seven gifts of the Holy Spirit. For who can be saved without the fear of God, without piety, and the other virtues? But when each person is taught by the preachers of the faith how he ought to live according to the commandments of God, he is sprinkled seven times with living water. After the cleansing of the leper, the living sparrow was then allowed to fly off into the field, because the holy preachers, after completing the duty of their preaching, earn the right to fly into the field of their heavenly homeland.

In another place also it is written that the Lord commanded Moses that the sons of Israel should offer to the priest the scapegoat, and that the priest should lay his hands on its head and confess over it the sins of the sons of Israel and send it by a man who was ready into the wilderness, so that it might carry the sins of the sons of Israel into the wilderness. But the one who led the scapegoat into the wilderness was not allowed to enter the camp unless he first washed his garments (Lev. 16:21-26). Therefore the priest has the same meaning as the scapegoat; for they signify our Lord Jesus Christ. So the priest confessed over the scapegoat the sins of the sons of Israel, because the Son of God placed the sins of humanity upon the man whom he assumed, so that he himself would die for them. By the wilderness, whose dwelling all lovers of luxury dread, the cross is signified, very terrible to all who are guilty. Therefore the man who was ready led the scapegoat into the wilderness, because the Jews, who had been foreknown for the responsibility of this crime, crucified our Lord Jesus Christ. But it was commanded that the one who had led the scapegoat into the wilderness should first wash his garments and so enter the camp, because the Jews, the killers of Christ, could not enter the camp of the Church unless they were first washed with the water of baptism.

And in Deuteronomy Moses commanded the sons of Israel, saying: If you pass through a field and find a bird sitting on its young; do not keep the bird with its young, but let the bird fly away into the open field, and keep the young you have taken; so that you may live a long time in the land which the Lord your God has given you (Deut. 22:6). Could the releasing of a little bird increase anyone’s lifespan? No, but it is to be understood spiritually. By the bird, each holy soul is signified; by its young, good works are understood. For just as a bird gathers its young under its wings, so that by warming them it may nourish them, so each holy soul stores its works within the secret places of the heart, and by always increasing them multiplies and nourishes them, so that from them it may receive a most full reward. We find the bird with its young when we lovingly embrace a just person on account of his good works. We allow the bird to fly away into the field when we rejoice that the one we loved has, through death, ascended to the heavenly realms. But we keep his young captured if we retain his good works in our mind and advance by imitating them. And in this we acquire length of life for ourselves, which is given only to those who are established in heavenly blessedness. We have said a few things out of many about the opening of the third seal; now let us continue the arrangement.

Revelation 6:5b I heard the third living creature saying: Come and see;

That is, understand the Scripture of the Law spiritually.

Revelation 6:5c And behold, a black horse, and the one who sat upon it had a balance in his hand.

By the black horse we can understand the teachers of the Law; for the blackness of the horse signifies either the obscurity of the Law which they taught, or its hardness. For Moses also had a veil over his face, because the radiance of his countenance, that is, the spiritual understanding in the Law, could not be seen by eyes that were unfocused. Now the rider of the horse is the Lord; and the balance shows the equity of legal judgment, as in this: A life for a life, an eye for an eye, a tooth for a tooth, a foot for a foot (Exod. 21:23), and whatever blemish someone has inflicted, such a one is also compelled to endure.

Revelation 6:6a And I heard a voice in the midst of the four living creatures saying.

The voice which was made in the midst of the four living creatures can be understood as the voice of the Church imploring the Lord's mercy.

Revelation 6:6b Two pounds of wheat for a denarius, and three double-pounds of barley for a denarius, and do not harm the wine and the oil.

Great are the things he brought forth, and very obscure. Through the wheat from which the best bread is made, we can understand the divine Scripture, which is the bread of souls: but the two pounds of wheat signify the two Testaments, by which the divine Scripture is divided. From these two pounds of wheat the holy fathers have prepared for us a great abundance of food, who have expounded it in many ways: but the denarius signifies the Lord. Therefore the two pounds of wheat are joined to the denarius; because what the holy Scripture speaks refers to the omnipotence, greatness, goodness, and severity of one God; for whatever good we do towards our neighbors, it is from him, not from us. But through the harshness of the barley the works of the saints are signified: which indeed seem harsh through their operation, but hope makes them sweet, as the Lord says: For my yoke is easy, and my burden is light (Matt. 11:30). But there are three double-pounds of barley: therefore not inconveniently through the number six, which is perfect, the works of the saints are signified, because the perfection of the saints increases from good works. Therefore the three double-pounds of barley are joined to the denarius; because whatever good the elect do in this world, whatever difficulties they suffer, they do and suffer for the love of one omnipotent God.

Alternatively: by the horse those who have been made safe through the Law are indicated; by its rider, the very giver of the Law; and by the balance, the equity of his judgment. It follows that by the two measures of wheat and the three double-measures of barley we are to understand the Old Testament. Therefore by the two measures of wheat are signified the two commandments of love, which are especially celebrated in the Law and the Prophets, as the Lord says in the Gospel: “On these two commandments the whole Law and the Prophets depend” (Matt. 22:40).
