But since he says that those who share in the first resurrection are blessed, this does not seem to me to refer to baptism, because not all who appear to rise from the death of the soul through baptism are blessed: some, after baptism, bring death upon themselves by sinning, by lying about what they promised in it, and by continuing in their evil ways. There are also some who, after committing crimes by which they brought death upon themselves, return to life through repentance and tears, as well as other good works. The Apostle speaks of them, saying: “Awake, you who sleep, and rise from the dead, and Christ will shine upon you” (Eph. 5:14). Yet we cannot call this the first resurrection either, since our souls are recalled to life first through baptism, and then through repentance and tears. We must therefore investigate what he calls the first resurrection.

There are four stages in the lives of the faithful: the first is evil, the second both good and evil, while no evil can cling to the third or the fourth. The first is before baptism, and is wholly evil. The second is after baptism, and may be called both good and evil, for no believer can pass through the span of this present life without some sins, as John says: “If we say that we have no sin, we deceive ourselves, and the truth is not in us” (1 Jn. 1:8). The third is wholly good, and the order of this passage concerns it. It extends from Christ’s Passion to the end of the world, during which the saints, apart from their bodies, enjoy eternal life with the Lord. He also calls it the first resurrection, saying: “This is the first resurrection.” Therefore, the period from Christ’s Passion to the end of the world is called the first resurrection. The fourth life of the saints will begin after they receive their bodies, and will have no end. Since, then, the present life may rightly be called death in comparison with heavenly life, the saints rise in the first resurrection when, withdrawn from this wretched life, they are placed with the Lord in heavenly blessedness.

Just as this is the first resurrection of the saints, so too is it the first death of the wicked: when they leave this present life and are plunged into punishments. The second death, however, will be the final condemnation, as he explains in what follows. Blessed, therefore, will be those who share in the first resurrection, and over them the second death has no power. For only those are and will be blessed who, before the end of the world, are deemed worthy to be joined among the company of the saints. For when the end of the world draws near, purgatorial fire will burn many as it purges them; but before the Judgment, once absolved, they will be received into the company of the saints. And over them the second death, that is, the final condemnation, will have no power, because they will no longer be placed with the goats on the left, but with the sheep on the right. (Matt. 25:33)

Revelation 20:6b The priests of the Lord and of Christ sat down, and reigned with Christ for a thousand years.

By “priests” he means all the elect, for all the elect are called priests, either because they are members of the supreme Priest, or because they do not cease to offer themselves to God through good works.

Revelation 20:7-8a And when the thousand years are completed, Satan will be released from his prison, and he will go out and deceive the nations that are in the four corners of the earth, Gog and Magog.

This prison is called the abyss above. But meanwhile a question arises: for earlier, following the opinion of some19, I said that the hearts of the wicked could be signified by the abyss, in which the Devil, expelled from the hearts of the faithful, is held bound. If, then, the hearts of the wicked are signified by the abyss, how is he said to come out of the abyss, that is, from the hearts of the wicked, whom he will never abandon? Is he perhaps going to abandon the wicked and enter the elect? By no means. I therefore think that the Devil’s binding should be understood literally, so that he is held bound by divine power in some place of the abyss, or certainly in hell [inferno], until the day of his release. He will deceive the nations that are in the four corners of the earth, because his most wicked teaching through Antichrist will spread among all the nations contained within the four regions of the world. He will deceive no other nations than those spiritually called Gog and Magog. For Gog means “roof,” and Magog means “from the roof.” Therefore, Gog, which means “roof,” signifies those who hide their malice in their hearts and will appear just to other people, though they will be wicked in mind. Magog, which means “from the roof,” signifies those who burst forth from the roof of their hearts into open wickedness and reveal to everyone that they are wicked. The Devil will therefore easily deceive such people through Antichrist.

Revelation 20:8b And he will gather them for battle, whose number is like the sand of the sea.

He will gather them for battle, because he will arm them to persecute the Church of God.

Revelation 20:9a And they went up over the span of the earth and surrounded the camp of the saints and the beloved city.

We said above that the disciples of Antichrist would be advocates of vices and would teach everything concerning bodily pleasures. Therefore they went up over the span of the earth, because they will walk the broad paths of this age, which lead to death. The camp of the saints and the beloved city have one meaning: they signify the Church. Therefore they will surround the camp of the saints, because they will persecute the Church of God with all their might, so as to destroy the name of Christ entirely from the world.

Revelation 20:9b And fire came down from heaven from God and devoured them.

Fire will come down from heaven, for when the Lord comes to judge, fire will go before him. It will consume not only the whole host of the wicked, but the world itself, as David says in the ninety-sixth psalm: “Fire goes before him and sets his enemies ablaze all around” (Ps. 96:3). For although the wicked live among the elect, they are all around them; just as enemies surround a city to capture it, so the Church is surrounded by the wicked. David also says in the forty-ninth psalm: “Fire blazes before him, and a mighty tempest surrounds him” (Ps. 49:3). Some interpret this20 verse to mean that after the elements have been kindled, rain will fall to wash away the ashes of the conflagration. But this interpretation seems to me utterly absurd. For we know that rain rises from the sea and the earth, either through mist or through the sun’s evaporation. Just as water lifts lighter things from the bottom to the surface, so air lifts water, whether turned into mist or, through the sun’s heat, reduced to the thinnest, almost invisible vapor, to the place where it reaches its limit. There it gathers and first becomes a dense mist, which we call a cloud; then, driven by the wind, its tiniest drops mingle together and grow heavy through their union, fall to the earth, and make rain. Behold: the earth, the air, the sea, and the other elements will be consumed by fire. (2 Peter 3:10) From where, then, will rain arise, especially since, according to the Apostle (Rom. 8:21), creation will then be freed from its bondage to decay and brought into the glorious freedom of the children of God? Can God, who created all things from nothing, not cleanse his creation without the aid of rain, especially when the world itself will be restored after the fire to a better and far more beautiful state? And what are ashes but clean earth? For whatever impurities fire consumes, it turns into clean ashes. Can God not restore clean ashes to a better state alongside another earth, so that rain for washing will not be necessary?

Let us put it another way: The washings of all filth are either absorbed by the earth or flow into rivers, and with the rivers into the sea. How will the earth, after being tested by fire, absorb its own filth and become filthy again? Or how will it flow into rivers and, with the rivers, into the sea, when the sea will no longer exist, as the following passages of this book seem to admit? But if the sea is restored to a better state, like the heavens and the earth, how will it be polluted by the earth’s filth? And if someone says that it will suffer pollution before this renewal, and that these things will be reduced to nothing when it is transformed, can God not reduce the same filth to nothing on land just as in water, so that it need not flow into the sea? We may therefore understand the mighty storm as the final destruction of the wicked, which Wisdom, through Solomon, declares will befall the wicked at the final judgment, saying: “I too will laugh at your destruction and mock you when what you fear comes upon you; when sudden calamity strikes and destruction comes upon you like a storm” (Prov. 1:26-27).

Revelation 20:10a And the Devil who was deceiving them was cast into the lake of fire and sulfur, where the beast and the false prophet are.

This will be fulfilled when the Lord says: “Depart from me, you cursed, into the eternal fire prepared for the Devil and his angels” (Matt. 25:41).

Revelation 20:10b And they were tormented day and night for ever and ever.

May our Redeemer, in his mercy, agree to free us from this torment, he who lives and reigns with the Father and the Holy Spirit for ever and ever. Amen.

On the Sixth Vision

By the mercy of our Lord Jesus Christ, we have come to the sixth vision, which is shorter than the others, since it briefly describes only the general resurrection, the condemnation of the wicked, and the glorification of the righteous. And rightly the final resurrection is placed sixth, because the number six is perfect, and whatever has been promised to the saints will be fulfilled in the resurrection.

Revelation 20:11a And I saw a great white throne, and the One seated upon it.

This throne of God will not be adorned with gold and precious stones, but with righteous people; for this is the throne of which the Psalmist says: “Your throne, O God, is forever and ever; the scepter of righteousness is the scepter of your kingdom” (Ps. 44:7). Therefore, the throne of God is the whole company of angels and saints.

Revelation 20:11b At the sight of Him, earth and heaven fled, and no place was found for them.

How heaven and earth flee at the sight of the One seated upon the throne, the Psalmist shows, as we have already said above, when he says: “Fire blazes before Him” (Ps. 49:3); and again: “Fire goes before Him” (Ps. 96:3). Earth and heaven will flee, that is, the appearance of earth and heaven; for through fire they will be changed from their present appearance, so that they may be reshaped into another, much better and more beautiful one. When he says, “No place was found for them,” he does not mean that they will lose their former place, but their appearance.

Revelation 20:12a And I saw the dead, great and small, standing before the throne.

By the great and small dead are signified only the wicked, who will stand dead before God on the day of resurrection, with that death which we said was the first, soon to be cast down into the second death. By the great dead, I think, are signified those who were either greater in their crimes or higher in the dignities of this world; by the small, those who were subject to them, or certainly those who sinned less.

Revelation 20:12b And the books were opened; and another book was opened, which is the book of life.

The many books belong to the wicked; the one book, which is the book of life, belongs to the elect. The wicked have many books because they have fallen into many errors: for the pagans worshipped many gods and plunged themselves into many errors; likewise, heretics devised many false doctrines repulsive to God; and false Christians, too, are daily entangled in many crimes. Therefore the wicked possess many books because they offend God in many ways; but the elect have one book, because they worship one God, profess one Catholic faith, and are bound together by one bond of love. But we must ask what these books are: will books be made from some material, visible or invisible, in which the deeds of each person are recorded? The many books, then, are the deeds of the wicked: the many books will be opened when the crimes of the wicked are made known to all. By the book of life, however, we can understand the predestination of almighty God. The Lord makes clear in the Gospel that the names of the disciples are written in this book when he says: “Rejoice, because your names are written in heaven” (Luke 10:20). The apostle Paul speaks of this book, saying: “Those whom he foreknew, he also predestined; and those whom he predestined, he also called; and those whom he called, he also glorified” (Rom. 8:30).

Revelation 20:12c And the dead were judged according to what was written in the books, according to their deeds.

The dead were judged according to what was written in the books, because each will be judged according to his own errors and the nature of his crimes. For heretics will be judged one way, pagans another, and false Christians another; but the worse each person has been in his own error, the more severely he will be judged.

Revelation 20:13 And the sea gave up its dead, and death and hell gave up the dead who were in them; and each was judged according to their deeds.

Now he returns to what he had passed over. For the general resurrection will take place first, and afterward judgment will be carried out. The Lord makes this clear in the Gospel, saying to his disciples: “At the renewal of all things, when the Son of Man sits on his glorious throne, you also will sit on twelve thrones, judging the twelve tribes of Israel” (Matt. 19:28). He first mentioned the renewal of all things, that is, the resurrection; then he said that they would sit to judge the peoples. And in the Gospel of this John, the Lord says: “The hour is coming when all who are in the tombs will hear the voice of the Son of God, and those who have done good will come out to the resurrection of life, while those who have done evil will come out to the resurrection of judgment” (Jn. 5:28–29). At the Lord’s command, therefore, the dead will come out of their tombs, because the bodies of all the dead, brought to life through the reception of their souls, will emerge from their graves. Those who have done evil will come out to the resurrection of judgment, because they will rise for this purpose: to be judged in the very bodies in which their souls sinned, so that those who were partners in their wickedness may share in their torments. The present passage also shows that the resurrection will take place before the judgment, since it says that after the dead have been given up by the sea, death, and hell [infernum], the wicked will be judged. The Catholic faith, which we often recite, likewise makes this clear when it says of the Lord: “At whose coming all people must rise with their bodies and give an account of their own deeds” (Athanasian Creed).

We must inquire what are meant by death, the sea, and hell. For we know that waters have been consecrated for the cleansing of our sins, as the Lord says in the Gospel: “Unless someone is born again of water and the Holy Spirit, he cannot enter the kingdom of God” (Jn. 3:5). The sea, therefore, refers to the resurrection of the elect, while death and hell pertain to the wicked. Therefore, in this passage, the sea signifies baptism. The sea will, as it were, give up its dead when the elect, who received in baptism the purity in which they will also rise, do so without any stain of sin. The sea will give up its dead because the washing of holy baptism and the grace of the Holy Spirit will present all the elect, at the time of the resurrection, pure and clean before their Creator. By death we can understand the bodies of the wicked, held dead in the depths of the earth; and by hell, their souls, since they are confined in hell’s prison. Hell, then, will give up its dead, because it will send back the souls it holds, devoured by the first death, to receive their own bodies. Death, too, will give up its dead, because it will present the bodies it had reduced to dust, so that by divine power they may be brought to life through the reception of their souls.

Revelation 20:14-15 And death and hell were thrown into the lake of fire . . . and anyone not found written in the book of life was thrown into the fiery furnace.

Death and hell were thrown into the lake of fire, that is, souls together with their bodies. By “death” we may also understand the demons, since they were the authors of death. Death and hell will be thrown into the lake, because the demons, along with the other wicked, will be plunged into hell.

Chapter 21

Revelation 21:1 And I saw a new heaven and a new earth; for the first heaven and the first earth had passed away, and the sea was no more.

Heaven and earth, and the other elements that we now see, will be consumed by fire, not so that they are utterly destroyed, but so that, as we have already said above, they are restored by fire to a better state, as the Psalmist indicates when he says: “They will perish, but you will endure; they will all grow old like a garment, and like a robe you will change them, and they will be changed” (Ps. 101:27). When it says, “The first heaven and the first earth had passed away,” it shows that the appearance of that same heaven and earth will pass away. Their appearance, not their essence, will pass away, as they are transformed into a better state. But when it adds, “And the sea was no more,” we may understand this literally, as meaning that the sea will be utterly destroyed. Alternatively, the sea will no longer exist because it will lose its present form, as will heaven and earth, and be restored in a more beautiful form. But I think this should be understood spiritually as well: everything said about the renewal of heaven and earth should be understood both historically, as we said above, and spiritually. Therefore, by heaven we may understand the souls of the saints, and by earth, their bodies. Heaven will be made new when the souls of the saints, through the reception of their bodies, are restored to human nature and form. Earth will be made new when the bodies of the saints, which were once mortal and corruptible, are made immortal and incorruptible. The wicked, meanwhile, are often represented by the sea, as the prophet Isaiah says: “The wicked are like a raging sea that cannot rest, whose waters cast up mire and mud” (Isa. 57:20). The sea, then, will be no more, because the whole multitude of the wicked, cast into hell [infernum] with their deceivers, the demons, will leave the present face of the world cleansed of their defiled habitation.

Revelation 21:2 And I saw the holy city, Jerusalem, coming down from heaven, from God, prepared like a bride adorned for her husband.

The city of Jerusalem is the Church, built from all the righteous; it will come down from heaven, because when the Lord comes for judgment, the whole multitude of the saints will come with him, as the prophet Isaiah says: “The Lord will come to judge with the elders of his people and its princes” (Isa. 3:14). And the wisest Solomon says in Proverbs: “Her husband is renowned at the city gates, when he sits with the elders of the land” (Prov. 31:23). It is said to be prepared and adorned like a bride, because just as a bride adorns herself with various jewels and ornaments to please her bridegroom, so the Church is adorned with various virtues, that she may please her Creator, who is her bridegroom.

Revelation 21:3 And I heard a loud voice from the throne saying: “Behold, God’s dwelling is with humanity, and he will dwell with them; they will be his people, and the Lord himself will be with them as their God.”

We know that Jerusalem and the Lord’s dwelling are often used to signify the Church. Therefore, in this passage, the city and the dwelling are called the Church, because God dwells in it, as he says through the prophet: “I will dwell among them and walk among them; I will be their God, and they will be my people” (Lev. 26:12).

Revelation 21:4a And God will wipe away every tear from their eyes.

The souls of all people were created immortal, as the Lord says in the Gospel: “Do not fear those who kill the body but cannot kill the soul” (Matt. 10:28). For although they die through sin, or by the death we called the first and the second death, they do not die in such a way that they cease to exist entirely; rather, their state of misery is called death. God also appointed two periods for them: the present life, which ends quickly, and the life to come, which cannot end. He assigned one to weeping and sorrow, the other to joy and gladness. For example, if anyone wished in this brief present life to weep and mourn for their sins, and by afflicting themselves to extinguish bodily desires, in the other life that follows, which is endless, they would rejoice and glory forever. But if anyone chooses to rejoice in the present life and fulfill the desires of the flesh, in the life to come they will mourn and grieve forever. It seems to me that Solomon spoke of these two periods when he said: “A time to weep, and a time to laugh” (Eccles. 3:2). The time for the chosen to weep is now; the time to laugh will be then, when what the Lord says in the Gospel is fulfilled: “Blessed are those who mourn now, for they will be comforted” (Matt. 5:5). But to sinners he says: “Woe to you who laugh now, for you will weep and mourn!” (Luke 6:25). Therefore God will wipe away every tear from the eyes of the saints, because after the resurrection nothing sorrowful can happen to them from which tears could arise.

Revelation 21:4b And death shall be no more.

The first man was made immortal; but by sinning he lost immortality and brought death into the world. After the resurrection, however, there will be no death, because the Devil himself, the prince of death, will be kept forever in hell [infernum] with all his followers, and the immortality that the first man lost by sinning will be restored to the righteous. The ability to sin, which brought death into the world, will also be taken away from them.

Revelation 21:4c Nor will there be mourning.

Since above he says that God will wipe every tear from the eyes of the saints, why was it necessary to mention mourning here? But there is some difference between shedding tears and mourning. For sadness often brings tears, as do joy, yearning, and delight; mourning, however, always arises from great grief. Mourning, indeed, is weeping that lasts many days. Paul mourned those who had sinned and had not repented. (2 Cor. 12:21) And Solomon says: “The mourning for the dead lasts seven days, but the mourning for a fool lasts all the days of his life” (Sirach 22:13). The Church is now in mourning because, through the deceptions of demons, she daily loses most of her own. But in that life there will be no mourning, because through the deceptions of demons she will be able to lose none of her own.

Revelation 21:4d Nor will there be crying.

In Genesis it is written that God said to Abraham: “The crying of Sodom and Gomorrah has come to me” (Gen. 18:20). The prophet Isaiah explains what the crying of Sodom is when he speaks of Israel: “They proclaimed their sin like Sodom and did not hide it” (Isa. 3:9). Therefore the proclamation of sin is called crying. We can also understand “crying” in another way, for the Psalmist says: “In my distress I called to the Lord, and he heard me” (Ps. 119:1). And Moses, when he was in dire straits, cried out to the Lord. Therefore, where there is neither sin to be proclaimed nor any distress, no crying will be needed.

Revelation 21:4e Nor will there be pain.

There is bodily pain and there is pain of the mind. These pains are so closely connected that one cannot exist without the other: bodily pain afflicts the mind, and likewise pain of the mind torments the body. In that life, then, there will be no pain, since no adversity can befall either body or soul.

Revelation 21:4f For the former things have passed away.

These former things have passed away because they passed with the world. The elect, who, as we have already said above, regarded their current life as one of pain and affliction, found eternal happiness after the end of this current life.

Revelation 21:5a And he who sat on the throne said, “Behold, I make all things new.”

We have said something above about the renewal of creatures.

Revelation 21:5b And he said to me, “Write, for these words are most faithful and true.”

But this, too, was said above.

Revelation 21:6a And he said to me, “I am the Alpha and the Omega.”

He explains what Alpha and Omega mean by adding,

Revelation 21:6b “The beginning and the end.” I will freely give from the fountain of the water of life to the thirsty.

Holy Scripture is often signified by the fountain of the water of life, as the Lord says in the Gospel: “Whoever believes in me, as the Scripture says, rivers of living water will flow from his belly” (Jn. 7:38). But, to keep to the order of the text, by the fountain of the water of life we should understand the glory of the heavenly homeland: this fountain, proceeding from the Lord himself, never ceases to satisfy the minds of all the righteous with its indescribable sweetness. But how is this glory given freely to the righteous, when they endure many hardships to receive it, shed many tears, offer countless prayers, give many alms, and devote themselves to many other good works? They receive eternal life freely, because they receive from him the price of eternal life, which is good works; for he said, “Without me you can do nothing” (Jn. 15:5). For he himself gives the price, and he himself gives eternal life as its reward.

Revelation 21:7 The one who conquers will inherit these things, and I will be his God, and he will be my son.

The one who conquers the Devil, overcomes every vice, and perseveres in good works will inherit these things, that is, what was said above: namely, that he himself will drink from the fountain of eternal life, which is everlasting glory.

Revelation 21:8 But as for the cowardly, the unbelieving, the accursed, murderers, the fornicators, sorcerers, idolaters, and all liars, their portion will be in the lake of burning sulfur, which is the second death.

Since fear is very necessary for people, why did he place fear first among the eight very worst vices? There are good fears and bad fears. There is a very good fear, of which the Psalmist says: “The fear of the Lord is holy, enduring forever” (Ps. 18:10). There is also the very necessary fear of death and the fear of eternal fire; and there is the fear of good people, when they are loved by those who fear them. There are other fears that sometimes help and sometimes harm, such as fear of wicked people, fear of judges, and fear of masters. There are other very bad fears, which I think are meant here. For there are some who, often pierced with remorse and tears by fear of punishment, nevertheless dread abandoning their vices and embracing the rigors of the good life because they fear the harshness of the path that leads to life. There are also many to whom God has given knowledge of temporal laws. When appointed to judge, they see some being unjustly oppressed by the powerful; yet, fearing human anger more than God’s anger, they allow those whom they could have helped and delivered from danger through the knowledge they received to come to great harm by remaining silent. Others not only fail to help them, but, out of fear of people, pronounce a sentence of condemnation against them. There is also the fear born of greed, along with other very useless fears, about which it would take too long to speak.

But he calls unbelievers not only those who do not believe, such as the Jews and pagans, but also those who do not believe in the resurrection. There are also many others who are called Christians but do not believe in God’s justice. Because they love their vices and want to gratify the desires of their flesh, they do not believe the warnings of the divine Scriptures, thinking they will not be condemned for these things. For the sexually immoral say that no one will perish for having sexual relations; drunkards likewise say they will not be punished for drinking too much wine. In the same way, the greedy, the pleasure-seeking, and the proud defend their vices.

I think those are accursed who curse the commands of the divine Scriptures and the corrections of good people. By “accursed,” we may also understand those who are so shameless that they are not ashamed to commit abominable acts in full view of everyone, and who are often cursed not only by the righteous but also by some unrighteous people.

He calls murderers not only those who kill people, but also those who harbor hatred in their hearts, as John says: “Everyone who hates his brother is a murderer” (1 Jn. 3:15).

Next comes fornication. There are many kinds of fornication: any gratification of lust by any means other than with one’s lawful wife is called fornication. The Apostle Paul speaks of the evil of fornication, saying: “Whoever commits fornication sins against his own body” (1 Cor. 6:18). He sins against his own body because he violates and corrupts its integrity, forcibly extracting and spilling its harmless fluids. There is also fornication of the mind, of which the Lord says: “Whoever looks at a woman with lustful intent has already committed adultery with her in his heart” (Matt. 5:28). The prophet also called Jerusalem a fornicator because she forsook God and worshiped idols (Ezek. 16:15). Likewise, when we obey the ideas of the demons and offer ourselves to them, we are appropriately called fornicators.

Sorcerers21 are those who poison people’s bodies in order to kill them. There are other sorcerers far worse: those who sow discord among brothers. The former kill bodies; the latter, souls. And the death of the soul is far worse than that of the body.

Idolaters are not only those who worship idols, but also all the greedy, as the Apostle Paul shows when he calls greed the worship of idols (Col. 3:5).

But what follows is very dreadful: “and all liars.” Then he adds: “Their portion will be in the lake of burning fire and sulfur, which is the second death.” And the Psalmist says to the Lord: “You will destroy all who speak lies” (Ps. 5:7); and elsewhere: “Every person is a liar” (Ps. 115:11). If, then, every person is a liar, and God will destroy all who speak lies, and all liars will share the fate of the other ungodly people about whom we have spoken so far, who can be saved? For we know that no one can pass through this life without ever telling a lie, unless that person is an infant who cannot yet speak. We should not understand this to refer to every kind of lie, just as we should not understand the words sung in Psalm 36, “Sinners will perish” (Ps. 36:20), or in Psalm 144, “The Lord watches over all who love him, but all sinners he will destroy” (Ps. 144:20), as referring to every sinner. If sinners will perish, and he will destroy all sinners, and we are all sinners, according to the saying of this same John, “If we say that we have no sin, we deceive ourselves, and the truth is not in us” (1 Jn. 1:8), then we will all perish. But we should understand this as referring not to all sinners, but only to those who plunge themselves into grave sins and refuse to repent of their evils. Likewise, we should understand this passage as referring not to all liars. For there are lies counted among the lesser sins, from which I think no one can entirely abstain; and there are also grave lies, which plunge a person into the condemnation of death.

Since in these times many people lie almost constantly without any reverence for almighty God, it has seemed good to us to say something about this vice. But first let us speak of trivial lies, and afterward of more serious ones. We often lie through misjudgment, supposing that we see what we do not see, hear what we do not hear, or know what we do not know. For often, when we look at certain things from far away, our sight deceives us, and we say that things are there which are not. Often, too, in the dark, when we see stones or bushes at a distance, the darkness itself deceives us, and we say they are people or animals. We are often misled by forgetfulness when we forget what we have done or seen, or what we have said, and claim to have done what we did not do, or not to have done what we did; to have heard what we did not hear, or not to have heard what we did; to have seen what we did not see, or not to have seen what we did; to have said what we did not say, or not to have said what we did. Often, when we hear what we wish to hear, we say that what we heard is true, though it often happens that it is false. And when we hear what we do not wish to hear, we say it is false, even though it is true. We also often lie in joke to make our listeners laugh. Indeed, we often lie out of excessive credulity. For when we have heard certain people whom we consider truthful describing conceivable things, we believe what we hear. When we repeat these things to others, we say they are true, even though they are false; and we often confirm them under oath.

There are also many who, having read or heard accounts of the Fathers in the divine Scriptures, forget some of them; when they begin to tell these accounts to others, they either mix in certain falsehoods or try to make one account out of several. Just as someone who mixes tin with gold or silver makes a worthless material, so these people, when they strive to make one account out of several, render it worthless by lying. For it is better to be silent than to relate the deeds of the saints falsely. There are also many learned in divine letters who, believing the Jews more than they ought, accept some of their utterly vain fables and insert them into their own traditions. For we know that the Jews strive to twist into perverse meanings everything found in the Old Testament concerning Christ, so as to extinguish the glory of Christ’s name wherever they can. They not only overturn the prophecies concerning Christ, but also insert many utterly vain and absurd fables in other passages. I therefore wonder why anyone believes the enemies of Christ and of all our religion, or accepts anything from them, except only what is known to have been approved by the ancient Fathers. For they have the Devil as their father, who did not remain in the truth, because he is a liar, and the father of lies; and the Devil makes liars of all whom he possesses (Jn. 8:44).

We often lie out of fear as well. When we fear that someone more powerful may inflict some harm on us, we sometimes invent lies to escape it. Travelers, too, often make up stories to escape robbers. Likewise, servants and children often invent some lies to avoid being beaten. There are also many who, finding themselves in unfamiliar places, lie because of poverty or some other need, so that they may gain food or other necessities of life. We also often lie out of love or anger: praising those we love beyond measure, and condemning those with whom we are angry more than we ought. But these seem to me more serious than the foregoing. There are other lies like those just mentioned, which would take too long to discuss; for even the righteous, not only the unrighteous, often fall into such lies. For this reason, it seems to me that the Psalmist said: “Every man is a liar” (Ps. 115:11).

There is another kind of lie that might seem blameless, were it not that Scripture says every lie comes from evil; and some such lies are found in the Holy Scriptures (1 Jn. 2:21). For in Genesis it is written that Pharaoh commanded the Hebrew midwives to kill the boys and spare the girls (Exod. 1:16). But they feared God and kept the boys alive. When Pharaoh rebuked them for this, they said that all Hebrew women knew how to act as midwives and gave birth before the midwives could reach them. Was it not better to save the children by lying than to commit so many murders by telling the truth? It is also written in Joshua that he sent two spies to explore Jericho (Josh. 2:4-6). They entered the house of a prostitute named Rahab, who hid them in the inner part of her house. When their pursuers came, she said that they had escaped in the dark as the gate was closing. By this lie she freed them from their pursuers, and she herself, together with all her family, was saved from the danger of death. For this deed she is praised by the blessed Apostle Paul in his Epistle to the Hebrews. He says: “By faith Rahab the prostitute did not perish with the unbelievers, because she had received the spies in peace” (Heb. 11:31). It is also written in the Book of Kings that David, fleeing from his son Absalom, sent two spies to Jerusalem to learn what his son was doing (2 Sam. 15:32-36). When Absalom heard of it, he sent men to capture them. But they fled and entered the house of a man whose wife hid them in a well in the courtyard, covering it over. When their pursuers came and asked for them, she replied: “They went on after taking a little water” (2 Sam. 17:20). Therefore, by telling the truth, she could have betrayed those whom she saved by lying. The stain of such lies, then, is wiped away by the love from which they spring, because “love covers a multitude of sins” (1 Peter 4:8).

Now let us speak of the more serious lies. There are lies of unbelief, and there are blasphemies; others proceed from hatred, others are caused by envy, others by greed, and others by boastfulness. The remaining vices, too, have lies suited to them. For unbelieving Jews and pagans have many lies. Besides denying that Christ came in the flesh, they hold many errors, deceived by which they perish. There are also lies of blasphemy, by which these same Jews and pagans, as well as heretics, blaspheme God and His Church, and all the divine mysteries in many ways. There are also lies that proceed from hatred. Some, because they harbor hatred in their hearts against their neighbors, bring false charges against them; and those whom they cannot kill with the sword, they drive by false accusations into great ruin, and often even to death. There are other lies born of envy. For when the envious see some people excelling in worldly honors, wisdom, or particular skills, they never cease to scorn and mock them, and to put themselves above them, so that they may appear more famous to those who believe their lies than those whom they despise. There are also lies of mockery, which properly belong to the envious. The envious always mock the deeds of the better, and seek to cloud with their lies the actions of those whom they cannot equal. For there are many who, pretending to be friends of those they hate or envy, draw them by their cunning persuasion into committing certain crimes. Once they have plunged them into some crime as into a pit, they turn against them and persecute them more fiercely than others do; and those who seemed to be friends are shown to have been the worst of enemies. Greed, too, has its own lies. How many people devise all manner of deceit and various schemes to seize other people’s property, so that they may take from others and satisfy, at least a little, the abyss of their greed, which is unquenchable! There are also many who, for the sake of receiving rewards, become false witnesses against their neighbors and, by lying, either drive them into servitude or take away their inheritance or property, and often even bring about their death.

There are other lies that produce boastfulness. For there are many lovers of boastfulness who, in order to be considered great and outstanding by others, claim to have done great things they have not done, to be capable of what they cannot do, to possess what they do not possess, or to know what they do not know. Their lies raise them to heights they cannot climb to. And because some regard this vice as insignificant, we know that some in monasteries are stained by it as well. Indeed, many people, after seeming to have left the world, claim to be great and famous among simple folk and those raised in monasteries. They say they once owned great wealth, boast that they were warriors and hunters, and tell many other lies about themselves so that they may be esteemed by those they can deceive with their lies. So I speak to you, who are such a person: Behold, when you were in the world, you could scarcely labor all year to obtain plain food sufficient for you and all your household. But now, in the monastery, you have become the possessor of treasure, estates, and great wealth. You could scarcely catch a single little fox, yet once you became a monk, you became a mighty hunter. You feared some of your lowliest neighbors, yet once in the monastery, you made yourself a most valiant warrior. Behold, this was not leaving the world, but acquiring it. You say you killed a man, and have laid upon yourself the guilt of murder. Your lying tongue did what your hand could not accomplish. And do you want me to prove that by lying you made yourself guilty of murder? Saul, as we read in the Book of Kings (1 Sam. 31:4), killed himself with his own hand out of fear of the Philistines. A certain man came to holy David and said that he himself had killed Saul, who was pursuing him. But David, understanding through the spirit of prophecy that the man was lying, ordered him to be punished as a murderer. Laying the guilt of murder upon him, he said: “Your blood be upon your own head, for your mouth has spoken against you, saying, ‘I killed the Lord’s anointed’” (2 Sam. 1:16). We know, moreover, that the wealthy of the world who come to conversion commit a grave sin if they begin to boast of their wealth and power. And if the one who tells the truth is guilty, how much more so are you, who lie in almost everything!

There are also others who lie almost nonstop and can scarcely say anything without mixing in a lie. Whatever they hear, they always invent some lies about it: they claim to have seen what they have not seen, heard what they have not heard, and done what they have not done. When truthful people call them out, they say that no one can speak the truth well unless he can lie well. O fool, how empty your claim is! Behold, Christ spoke many things well and truthfully, though he could never lie; you ought rather to have followed in his footsteps, as far as your strength allowed. Likewise, the apostles and other preachers taught and wrote many things without any mixing of lies. And Scripture says that every lie is from evil (1 Jn. 2:22). If lying is evil, how can evil be spoken well? O wretched man, how near is the wrath of almighty God, whom you do not fear to offend continually and without cause! For although the other vices are contrary to the Lord, they nevertheless seem to offer something to the body. Greed, though hateful to God in every way, seems to offer something to the body, since it gathers wealth with which to satisfy its desires. Likewise, lust seems to offer something to the body, since in its fulfillment the pleasure of the flesh is satisfied. And drunkenness, although it offends God, fulfills the desires of gluttony. The other vices, too, are taken up to fulfill the desires of the flesh. There are also certain lies that seem to add something to the body, such as the lies born of greed, which we mentioned above. But you gain nothing for the body, and yet do not fear to offend God without ceasing. Behold, your lie would be light if it occurred rarely; but through constant repetition it becomes most grave and unbearable. For just as mud is lighter than lead, yet through its accumulation becomes heavier and more dangerous, so that animals often sink into it, so too certain light sins, by accumulating, become graver than some that are considered grave. And know that, as Scripture itself says, God is truth (1 Jn. 5:6); nothing can be more contrary to truth than a lie.

Their portion, therefore, will be in the lake of burning fire and sulfur, which is the second death. In this passage he clearly explains what the second death is. May the mercy of our Redeemer agree to free us from the vices mentioned above, lest we fall into the second death; he lives and reigns with the Father and the Holy Spirit for ever and ever. Amen.

On the Seventh Vision

The seventh vision, which by the Lord’s gift we have reached, concerns completely the time that will follow the resurrection (above in chapter 15). The order of the account required that, after describing the general resurrection, he should describe the glory of the saints, which they will possess without end after the resurrection; in this vision it is portrayed under the figure of the city of Jerusalem.

Revelation 21:9 And one of the seven angels who had the bowls came and spoke to me, saying: “Come, and I will show you the bride, the wife of the Lamb.”

Earlier, in the fifth vision, (Rev. 17) John says that he was shown seven angels holding seven bowls, and later that one of them showed him the city of the devil and its condemnation in the form of a prostitute. Likewise, here he says that one of those same seven angels showed him the city of God in the figure of Jerusalem. Each of these angels therefore has the same meaning as those seven: they signify the preachers who have been and will be until the end, while John represents the other faithful. The angel promises John that he will show him the city of God because the holy preachers strive, as far as they can, to show the other faithful how great is the blessedness of the righteous, so that they may kindle in them a desire to attain eternal life. The angel also calls it the bride, the wife of the Lamb. John the Baptist shows that the Church is rightly called the Lamb’s bride, saying: “He who has the bride is the bridegroom” (Jn. 3:29). Scripture shows that she is also called a wife, saying: “Therefore a man shall leave his father and his mother and cleave to his wife, and the two shall become one flesh” (Gen. 2:24). Therefore the Son of God, as it were, left his Father when, descending from that heavenly blessedness to earth, he assumed human nature, which he would give for his bride, who is the Church. He left his mother because, setting aside a mother’s affection, he surrendered for his Church the body he had taken from her. He united the Church to himself in such a way that he is the head and she is the body.

Revelation 21:10a And he carried me to a great and lofty mountain.

This mountain is Christ. The Prophet speaks of this mountain, saying: “In the last days, the mountain of the house of the Lord shall be established above the tops of the mountains” (Isa. 2:2). The mountains above whose tops the mountain of the house of the Lord is said to be established signify the Fathers of the Old Testament. Therefore the mountain of the house of the Lord is said to be established above their tops because they themselves foretold him, and when he came in the flesh, he surpassed them by a wondrous height. Therefore the house of the Lord, that is, the Church, was built upon this mountain, because it was founded upon Christ, as the Apostle Paul says: “For no one can lay any foundation other than the one already laid, which is Jesus Christ” (1 Cor. 3:11).

Revelation 21:10b-11a And he showed me the holy city, Jerusalem, coming down out of heaven from God, having the glory of God.

It should be noted that Ezekiel’s final vision agrees in some places with this vision of John; therefore, it pleased us to insert a few things from that vision into this work. For he says (Ezek. 40:2) that a mountain was shown to him, on which stood a city, and within it a temple. Although he mentions only the city, he speaks at length about the structure of the temple. The temple there therefore has the same meaning as the city here, for both signify the Church. The temple signifies the Church of the present age, while the city of Jerusalem signifies the Church as it will be after the end of the world. But someone may ask: How does this description of Jerusalem, that is, of the Church, refer to a future time, when this vision seems to describe its form in the present age? We answer that the Church cannot be fully known as long as it remains in this life, but is kept hidden, as it were, beneath a veil, since it is unknown who is rejected or chosen, or to what end each person will bring his deeds. In that life, however, its form will be revealed to all. For then it will clearly appear to everyone who was a wall, who were the gates, who were the foundations, who were the stones of the structure, and who were the precious stones. But we must ask how it is said to descend from heaven, since it ascends daily to its heavenly dwelling, where it is believed to remain forever. As long as we reflect upon the deeds and merits of the saints, they continually grow in our hearts; and the more we consider them, the more their magnificence is exalted in our eyes. But when we raise the eyes of our minds to contemplate the greatness of almighty God, the greatness of the saints, compared with God’s greatness, is greatly diminished in our estimation. The saints therefore ascend in our eyes when we consider their deeds and merits; but they descend when we raise the eyes of our minds to contemplate God’s almighty power. It is also said that the city had the glory of God. Indeed, it had God’s glory, not its own. For the sun often shines upon water and lends its brightness to it, so that the water dazzles the eyes of those who look upon it just as the sun does. Therefore, just as water has the sun’s brightness, not from itself but from the sun, so the saints have God’s glory, not from themselves but from God, by whom they are enlightened.

Revelation 21:11b Its radiance was like a precious stone, like jasper, like crystal.

Behold, he set three stones, to whose radiance he compared the light of the Church: one without a name, but naming the other two. Therefore we can understand the Father as the precious stone, the Son as jasper, and the Holy Spirit as crystal. The first is unnamed because the Father is from himself; the second and third are named because the Son is from the Father, while the Holy Spirit proceeds from both. We can also understand hope as the precious stone, faith as jasper, and love as crystal. But someone might say that faith and hope will not be needed in that life. For what does anyone hope for if they see it (Rom. 8:24)? We reply that just as a garment dyed from some material holds its beautiful color after the material is discarded, so in that life, when faith, hope, humility, abstinence, and the other virtues reach their end apart from love, the splendor the saints acquired through practicing them will remain in them forever.

Revelation 21:12a And it had a great and high wall.

This is the wall of which the prophet says: “Our strong city is Zion; the Savior shall be set within it as a wall and fortification” (Isa. 26:1). By this wall, therefore, Christ is signified together with all his members, so that its foundation represents him, while the rest of its structure represents his members.

Revelation 21:12b Having twelve gates.

In Ezekiel (Ezek. 46:1 and so on) it is written that the temple shown to him had three gates: the eastern, the southern, and the northern. God commands that the eastern gate always remain shut, with only the Prince entering and leaving through it. The eastern gate represents the blessed and glorious Virgin Mary, who remained shut before the Prince, that is, Christ, entered, and remained shut forever after he left. The southern gate represents the Old Testament, while the northern gate represents the New. Those said to come from the south represent the Jews; those from the north represent the Gentiles. The Jews, then, who knew God and to whom the Law had been given from the direction of the sun, that is, from the south, where the sun burns more fiercely, are rightly said to come to the Church. They entered through the southern gate because, recognizing that Christ had been prophesied in many ways and prefigured in various forms in the Old Testament, they were drawn by their own Scriptures to embrace faith in Christ. The Gentiles, however, abandoning idolatry and renouncing the one who said, “I will sit on the mountain of the covenant, on the sides of the north; I will ascend above the heights of the clouds; I will be like the Most High” (Isa. 14:13), are fittingly said to come from the north. They entered through the northern gate because, hearing the teaching of the Gospel and marveling at the miracles performed through Christ and his ministers, they rushed to embrace faith in Christ. It was also commanded that no one return by the way he had entered, but that he leave by the gate opposite: whoever entered through the southern gate was to leave through the northern, and whoever entered through the northern was to leave through the southern. The Jews, whom understanding the Old Testament led to embrace faith in Christ, were not to return by the way they had come, that is, to observe the legal ordinances in a bodily manner. Rather, they were to leave through the northern gate, turning to ponder and examine the teaching of the Gospel, so that they might learn to fulfill spiritually what they had previously attempted to do in a bodily manner. The Gentiles, who entered through the northern gate, that is, who became children of the Church through the teaching of the Gospel, were not to return by the way they had come, namely, by going back to investigate the errors of the philosophers. Instead, after embracing the faith and teaching of the Gospel, they were to turn to the southern gate, that is, to examine the teaching of the Old Testament. When they found Christ foretold there in many ways, their faith in Christ would be strengthened. The twelve gates said to belong to Jerusalem signify the twelve apostles and their teaching.

Revelation 21:12c And I saw twelve angels at the gates.

The twelve angels represent the other preachers, namely, the followers of the apostles, who are said to have been at the gates because, holding fast to the unwavering faith of the apostles and following their teaching, they strove to draw as many people as they could to faith in Christ.

Revelation 21:12d And the names of the twelve tribes of the children of Israel were written there.

By the names of the twelve tribes we may understand the fathers of the Old Testament, who did not stray from faith in the Holy Trinity; we may also understand the Old Testament itself by those same names. The names of the twelve tribes were written on the gates because the teaching of the apostles and the other preachers is full of examples from the Old Testament.

Revelation 21:13 Three gates on the east, three gates on the north, three gates on the south, and three gates on the west.

He set out the four quarters of the world to show that nearly all the nations contained within those four quarters had come together in the Church. He said that he saw three gates facing each quarter, twelve in all, to show that no one can enter the Church except through confession of the Holy Trinity. And since the teaching of the apostles is nothing other than the Gospel, and the Gospel itself is divided into four books, while the teaching of each book rests on faith in the Holy Trinity, this city is rightly said to have four sides, each with three gates; together they make twelve.

Revelation 21:14 And the city wall had twelve foundations, and written on them were the twelve names of the apostles and of the Lamb.

He explained what the twelve foundations signified when he added that the names of the twelve apostles and of the Lamb were written on them. I was rebuked by someone for saying that Peter was the foundation of the Church, in the passage where the Lord says: “You are Peter, and upon this rock I will build my Church” (Matt. 16:18). If Peter is the foundation of the Church, as are the other apostles, as the present passage plainly shows, then the Church was built upon him, just as it was upon the others. But if the Church was not built upon Peter, then neither was it built upon the others. Therefore this present assertion of John is false, since he says that the twelve apostles are the twelve foundations of the Church. Nor does what the Apostle says discourage us from our understanding: “For no one can lay any foundation other than the one already laid, which is Jesus Christ” (1 Cor. 3:11). For Peter is not one foundation and Jesus Christ another; Peter is a member of Jesus Christ, as Christ himself said to his disciples: “You in me, and I in you” (Jn. 14:20). For Christ is the foundation of all his apostles, and they likewise are foundations for those who believed through them. There is therefore one foundation, namely Christ, by whom all the foundations are held together, and upon whom the whole structure of the Church has been built. Paul speaks of this foundation, saying: “If anyone builds upon this foundation with gold, silver, precious stones, wood, hay, or straw, each one’s work will be tested by fire to show what it is” (1 Cor. 3:12). Here he named six kinds: three valuable and three worthless. Gold often signifies wisdom. A person builds gold upon Christ the foundation when he stores in his heart the wisdom that crushes vices and nurtures virtues, following the Psalmist, who said: “I have hidden your words in my heart, that I might not sin against you” (Ps. 118:11). Silver signifies the word of preaching, of which the same Psalmist says: “The words of the Lord are pure words, silver refined by fire” (Ps. 11:7). A person builds silver upon Christ the foundation when he teaches others the good he has learned, so as to lead them to do good works. Precious stones signify good works. A person builds precious stones upon Christ the foundation when he fulfills in deeds the good he teaches. Some do not have words of encouragement with which to instruct others fully, but by exercising themselves in good works, they build precious stones upon Christ the foundation. Likewise, those who know little but teach it to others build silver upon Christ, provided they fulfill in their deeds what they teach. Many build upon Christ by teaching others the understanding they have received, but through evil deeds they destroy within themselves what they have built. Wood, hay, and straw signify minor sins. But there is another interpretation that prevents us from understanding them in this way, for sins are not built upon Christ; rather, works of virtue are. When we sin, we withdraw from the building constructed upon Christ, and the more grievously we sin, the farther we are separated from it. But when through repentance we return to doing good works, we return to the building constructed upon Christ. We can therefore understand wood as earthly labor: a person devotes himself to such work to earn a living, lest he be driven by need to steal. For the Apostle commands that each person work quietly and eat his own bread; and again: “Whoever is unwilling to work,” he says, “shall not eat” (2 Thess. 3:10, 12). By hay we can understand earthly affection, as when someone loves his wife, children, and brothers out of bodily attachment. The Apostle also commands that husbands love their wives, wives their husbands, and children and parents one another (Eph. 5:25, 28). By straw we can understand those who take wives because they are troubled by lust and unable to restrain themselves. The Apostle also commands this, saying: “Because of sexual immorality, each man should have his own wife, and each woman her own husband” (1 Cor. 7:2). And because such ways of life cannot be free from frequent failings, he says that such people will be saved through fire, that is, through purgatorial fire. This will happen if they abstain from grave sins and, by giving generously in alms, merit the possibility of being saved after death through purgatorial fire. For just as gold made impure by admixtures is purified by fire, so these people are cleansed by fire of the impurities they contracted through worldly deeds, so that they may become worthy of being joined to the company of the elect.

Revelation 21:15 The one who spoke with me had a golden measuring rod to measure the city, its gates, and its wall.

The angel who spoke with John signifies Christ in this passage: Christ now rules all nations with an iron rod, as has already been said above; (Rev. 2:27, 12:5, 19:15) but in the future, he will measure with a golden rod the city of God, which will be made up of all nations. We said above that the iron rod represents the discipline of the Gospel; iron, however, is heavy and very hard. The Lord speaks of the hardness of the iron rod in the Gospel: “The way is narrow and constricted, leading to life” (Matt. 7:14). But the love of God and the hope of our heavenly homeland make this hardness sweet and light, as he says again: “For my yoke is sweet, and my burden is light” (Matt. 11:30). Therefore the iron rod will be changed into a light, sweet, and golden reed, because the saints will receive sweetness and everlasting glory in return for the hardships they endured for Christ. He will measure the city, its gates, and its wall when he repays each person according to the quality of their deeds. But we must ask: since the city is said to have been measured, why are its gates and wall added, when all these are included in the name “city”? Yet, as what came before and what follows show, the gates signify the apostles, the city signifies apostolic men, and the walls signify the remaining multitude of the faithful. What follows makes this clearer. Next:

Revelation 21:16a And the city is laid out as a square, and its length is as great as its width.

By the city’s square shape, the perfection of the saints is signified: a square stone, whichever way you turn it, will stand upright. And if you examine carefully a square stone, or anything else that can be made square, you will be able to distinguish that the perfection of the Church is shown in it. For it has six faces, eight corners, and twelve ledges. The six faces demonstrate the perfection of good works, because six is a perfect number. It has eight corners, of which four face heaven and four face earth. The four corners therefore signify the Gospels, which are doubled because they especially praise love of God and neighbor. Four face heaven to show that God is to be loved above all things; four face earth to show that our neighbors are to be loved as ourselves. The twelve ledges demonstrate the faith of the twelve apostles. Three ledges extend from each corner to show that, through the teaching of Christ, which is the Gospel and is divided into four books, faith in the Holy Trinity passed to the apostles, so that through them it might spread to all nations. We have said all this about the square stone to show that the city’s square shape signifies the perfection of the Church. And because this city has equal sides, its length is said to be as great as its width. For its length signifies eternal glory, while its width signifies love. The length of this city, then, is as great as its width, because the greater each believer’s love in this life, the greater the glory they will possess in heavenly blessedness; and the lesser their love, the lesser their glory.

Revelation 21:16b And he measured the city at twelve thousand stadia: its length, width, and height are equal.

The twelve stadia have the same meaning as the twelve gates, for they signify the apostles. Above, however, it is said that the city has equal length and width; here it is said that its length, width, and height are equal. Therefore, by these three things mentioned here, we may understand faith, hope, and love: by length, faith; by width, love; and by height, hope. But how can they be equal, when the Apostle says, “Now these three remain: faith, hope, and love; but the greatest of these is love” (1 Cor. 13:13)? For although love is greater than all the virtues because it endures forever, equality may nevertheless be attributed to these three virtues in a certain respect. The Apostle says that faith works through love, and that faith without works is dead (Gal. 5:6; James 2:26). If it is dead, the one for whom it is dead is nothing; and if faith works through love, it is dead for the one who has no works of love. Therefore, faith is as great as the works of love. Likewise, hope is as great as faith and love, because the glory for which we hope is as great as the works of faith, which is love. For the more each believer succeeds in expanding himself through works of love, the more surely he may expect the glory of the heavenly homeland to increase for him.

Revelation 21:17 And he measured its wall: one hundred and forty-four cubits, by human measurement, which is also an angel’s measurement.

If we consider what was said earlier, this cannot stand literally. For earlier he says that he measured the city at twelve stadia, whereas here he says that the city’s walls were measured at one hundred and forty-four cubits. The distance of twelve stadia is much greater than that of one hundred and forty-four cubits. Therefore, by the greater distance we can understand those of greater merit. Earlier we said that the twelve apostles were represented by the twelve stadia. Therefore the martyrs and confessors, and the other perfect men who followed the apostles’ teaching in all things and imitated their deeds, will be measured within their circuit, because they will possess the same glory as the apostles. The walls, however, which are made of smaller stones, represent the rest of the multitude of the faithful. They will occupy a smaller circuit than those of whom we spoke earlier, because those of lesser merit will receive lesser rewards. Their number is based on twelve, because they kept the apostolic faith undefiled: twelve times twelve makes one hundred and forty-four. By saying that the city and its walls were measured by human measurement, which is also an angel’s, he shows that the angel appeared to him in human likeness. We can understand this in another way. He says that the measure of a human being is the measure of an angel, and that by this measure the city and its walls were measured, because holy men will be equal to the angels and will be measured by their measure: they will possess the same glory as the angels, as the Lord says in the Gospel: “At the resurrection they neither marry nor are given in marriage, but are like the angels of God in heaven” (Matt. 22:30).

Revelation 21:18 And the structure of its wall was jasper; the city itself was like pure gold, like clear glass.

Here, too, he made some distinction between the walls and the city. He said that the walls were built of jasper, while the city itself was like pure gold and clear glass. For the green manner of jasper signifies faith; therefore, jasper rightly represents all simple and imperfect men, who are saved through faith, as the prophet says: “The righteous shall live by his faith” (Hab. 2:4). The holy and perfect men, represented by the city, are rightly likened to pure gold, because they have been filled with wisdom, often symbolized by gold, and tested by various tribulations, like gold purified by fire. Clear glass, which is brighter than all metals, represents the angels, whom no stain of sin has ever been able to taint.

Revelation 21:19a The foundations of the city wall are adorned with every precious stone.

We said above that the twelve foundations represent the twelve apostles; all the righteous, however, are represented by the precious stones. Therefore, the twelve foundations will be adorned with twelve stones, because in that blessedness the apostles and the other preachers will have as their adornment those who believed through them, since they will receive their reward on behalf of all of them.

Revelation 21:19b The first foundation is jasper.

Jasper is green and a deep color; it signifies worldly believers who abstain from vices, although their greenness, that is, their faith, is somewhat clouded by worldly pursuits. Nevertheless, they do not cease to redeem their sins through almsgiving and other works of mercy. Nor is it without reason that such people are placed first: the apostles, as has often already been emphasized, were afraid to lay harsher commandments of the Lord upon those among the Gentiles who had only recently believed, lest they be frightened by the severity of the commandments and not dare to come forward to embrace the faith of Christ. So they wrote to them: “It has seemed good to the Holy Spirit and to us to impose no further burden upon you, except that you abstain from fornication, from food sacrificed to idols, from what has been strangled, and from blood” (Acts 15:28). And elsewhere, recounting the conduct of the Gentiles, Peter says to the Jews: “And God, who knows the heart, bore witness to them, giving them the Holy Spirit just as he did to us, cleansing their hearts by faith” (Acts 15:8-9). Therefore, all who imitate these people are included in the figure of the jasper stone.

Revelation 21:19c The second sapphire.

Sapphire is blue in color, like hyacinth, but it has drops sprinkled throughout it of the same color, though darker. It seems to me that certain worldly men are also represented by this stone: they long for heavenly things, yet are still occupied with worldly affairs, from which occupation they seem to acquire certain stains. Because they put heavenly things above all concerns, they are said to have the color of heaven. They are placed second in number because they strive to love God above all things and their neighbors as themselves.

Revelation 21:19d The third is the chalcedony.

Chalcedony is red in color, yet dark unless it is made thin by cutting; the hollow itself, however, gives it a most beautiful and pleasing color. This stone also signifies certain worldly men whose great holiness before the time of persecution had gone unnoticed. But when they were worn down by their persecutors through various tribulations, the full splendor of the holiness hidden within them became clear to all. They are fittingly placed in the third place, because, unwavering in their confession of the Holy Trinity, they deserved to achieve the honor of martyrdom.

Revelation 21:19e The fourth, an emerald.

The emerald is of exceptional beauty and greenness, so much so that it tinges with its own green everything around it. It represents holy preachers, green in faith and distinguished by their virtues, who spread the radiance of their faith everywhere through their preaching. They are therefore placed in the fourth place, because they spread the teaching of the Gospel, which is divided into four books, across many places.

Revelation 21:20a Fifth, sardonyx.

Sardonyx is white, tending slightly toward red: it signifies all the simple and innocent men, who are represented by the white stone because of the purity of their lives. Since, by keeping vigil, fasting, abstaining, and afflicting their bodies in various ways, they impose martyrdoms upon themselves, they seem to have a reddish manner. They are appropriately placed in the fifth place, for the five senses of the body are often represented by the number five. Blessed Gregory22 also says that the knowledge of external things is represented by the five bodily senses. And because these men strove to share with others the knowledge of external things that they had acquired, they rightly occupy the fifth place in the structure of the holy city.

Revelation 21:20b Sixth, the sardius.

The sardius is yellowish-brown in color, and because it seems to incline somewhat toward red, it is slightly darkened. The yellowish-brown manner, which approaches the color of gold, therefore signifies the tribulations the saints endured at the hands of their enemies; the red color signifies martyrdom. Therefore the sardius signifies holy and perfect men who, after experiencing every virtue, enduring various persecutions and suffering enormous torments at the hands of their enemies, were deemed worthy to achieve the honor of martyrdom. They are correctly placed under the number six, which is perfect, because they were perfect in virtue and crowned that perfection with martyrdom.

Revelation 21:20c Seventh, the chrysolite.

The chrysolite is entirely golden, and wisdom is often represented by gold. Therefore, the chrysolite represents wise men in the Church, who, because of the radiance of their wisdom, may rightly be called golden among men. They are fittingly placed in the seventh place, because, filled with the seven gifts of the Holy Spirit, they have turned many away from wickedness through their words and examples.

Revelation 21:20d The eighth is beryl.

Beryl is of exceptional brilliance, with a blue manner blended into it. It signifies people of pure lives, wholly removed from the deeds of the world. So that they might not in any way stain the radiance of their lives, they even chose to separate themselves from the company of other people. Their radiance turned blue, because they are shown to have led an angelic life among people, with their minds ever fixed on heaven. They are fittingly placed in the eighth place, for twice four makes eight. These people fulfilled the number four because they followed the Gospel command: “Go, sell all that you have, give it to the poor, and you will have treasure in heaven; then come, follow me” (Matt. 19:21). They doubled the number four because they persuaded many others to ascend with them to that same peak of perfection.

Revelation 21:20e The ninth is topaz.

Topaz is said to be the color of bronze and gold, spreading its radiance all around. Proven men are often represented by gold: those tested in the fire of tribulation. The tribulations themselves are various. At times, holy men are tested by the different temptations they suffer from demons; at times, by tribulations inflicted on them by evil people; at times, by afflictions they impose upon themselves; at times, they are tested by God’s scourges, and by the disciplines of the active life. It therefore seems to me that the stone topaz represents those who are first tested through the works of the active life and afterward pass on to the contemplative life. For a person ascends to the contemplative life by the proper steps only if first tested through the disciplines of the active life; whoever passes suddenly to the heights of the contemplative life without the disciplines of the active life easily falls. The claim that this stone scatters its rays all around signifies that the men it represents have drawn many to the works of the active life through preaching, and have also led many with them to the heights of the contemplative life. They are fittingly placed in the ninth place, for three times three makes nine. Therefore holy men who, out of love for the Holy Trinity, first afflict themselves through good works in the active life and, by their appeals, draw as many as they can to imitate them; and who then ascend to contemplate the divinity of that same Holy Trinity, bringing many with them, are fittingly placed in the ninth place, since they not only doubled the talent they received from the Lord, but tripled it as well.

Revelation 21:20f Tenth, chrysoprase.

Chrysoprase is said to be a stone of green and golden color. Because it is placed in the tenth place, we may understand it to represent certain teachers of the Church, who taught many by their words while present in the body, and instructed, and continue to instruct, many more through their writings, such as Jerome, Augustine, Gregory, and many others. They may rightly be called the lights of the Church, represented by the green and golden colors: they were unwavering in the greenness of faith and radiant with wisdom like gold. By the number ten, we may understand the Old Testament, on account of the Ten Commandments of the Law. Since no one can understand the Old Testament without the teaching of the New Testament, for spiritual understanding in the Old Testament is nothing other than the New Testament, it is appropriate that these teachers be placed in the tenth place, since they possessed the fullest knowledge of both the Old and New Testaments. We may also understand the number ten in another way. The number ten divides, orders, and completes all numbers. Likewise, these teachers, in a sense, divided the Scriptures when they explained them, arranging them into passages and books; they ordered them when they revealed their obscurities and showed what proper order those things that seemed disordered had; and they completed them when they supplied what seemed to be lacking.

Revelation 21:20g Eleventh is hyacinth.

Hyacinth whose appearance gave its name to the hyacinth color is most precious, having the color of a clear sky, like the sapphire. But the sapphire has spots, while this stone’s beauty is marred by none. It signifies holy men free from every stain of sin, who always dwell on earth in body and in heaven in mind. Among them was the Apostle, who said: “But our citizenship is in heaven” (Philip. 3:20). They are fittingly placed in the eleventh place: for, as we said above, the number ten already represents knowledge of the divine Scriptures; while the one that follows ten, making eleven, refers to the contemplation of the one God. These holy men, then, fulfilled the number ten, because, filled with knowledge of the divine Scriptures, they led many to eternal life through their preaching. They fulfilled the number one because they were constant in contemplation of almighty God. Neither the urgency of preaching drew them away from contemplation, nor did constant contemplation keep them from preaching. They bore the likeness of angels, who always fulfill their angelic ministry and are never separated from contemplating almighty God. The prophet seems to me to have spoken of such men when he said: “The lips of a priest should guard knowledge, and people should seek instruction from his mouth, for he is the angel of the Lord of hosts” (Mal. 2:7). John the Baptist was one of them. Of him the Lord said: “This is he of whom it is written: ‘Behold, I send my messenger before your face, who will prepare your way before you’” (Luke 7:27).

Revelation 21:20h The twelfth is amethyst.

The apostles are often represented by the number twelve. Amethyst, however, is purple, and we know that purple is a royal adornment. Therefore, by amethyst are represented the apostles and those like them, who were an adornment to God because they preached him throughout the whole world. Peter was an adornment to God, of whom it is written that he glorified God by his death (Jn. 21:19). Likewise, the other apostles were an adornment to God, for they glorified him by preaching, performing miracles, and dying for his name.

Revelation 21:21a And the twelve gates were twelve pearls, each gate made from a single pearl.

This cannot hold literally. If each gate was made from a single pearl, how can it be said that there were twelve pearls at each of the twelve gates? But it must be understood spiritually: there are twelve pearls, from which the individual gates are said to be made, that is, one hundred and forty-four.
