These signify the Church’s teachers, successors to the apostles, who followed the apostles’ faith and teaching and imitated their deeds. Because of the purity and exceptional holiness of their lives, they are represented by the whiteness of pearls.

Revelation 21:21b And the street of the city was pure gold, like see-through glass.

Earlier, we said that perfect men and holy angels were signified by pure gold and clear glass. But here, the street of the city signifies the minds of the faithful, while pure gold and see-through glass represent the purity of human and angelic minds. For just as no vain thought could ever enter the minds of angels and stain them, so after the resurrection no useless thought will ever come to the saints and defile their minds.

Revelation 21:22 And I saw no temple in it, for the Lord Almighty is its temple, and the Lamb.

The temple, in which offerings and sacrifices were made, signifies the present Church, in which not bodily but spiritual sacrifices are offered to God every day. For just as the rite of sacrifice was practiced there according to the kinds of sins, so in the Church the sacrifices of repentance, prayer, tears, and remorse of heart must correspond to the measure of one’s faults. The Prophet speaks of such sacrifices, saying: “A remorseful spirit is a sacrifice to God” (Ps. 50:19); and again: “Then you will accept a sacrifice of righteousness” (Ps. 50:21). If there is a sacrifice of righteousness, there is also one of patience, abstinence, humility, and the other virtues. These are the sacrifices that offer the Lord a most pleasing fragrance, not the flesh of cattle burned on the altar. John therefore said that he saw no temple in the holy city, because all the things we have mentioned will be unnecessary in that blessedness: “For God will wipe every tear from the eyes of the saints, and death will be no more, nor mourning, nor crying, nor pain will be any longer; for the former things have passed away” (Rev. 21:4). The Lord, however, will be its temple, and the Lamb, because the saints will be in him and dwell in him, as he himself said to his disciples: “As I am in the Father, and you in me, and I in you” (Jn. 14:20).

Revelation 21:23 And the city has no need of the sun or the moon to shine upon it, for the glory of God will illuminate it, and its lamp is the Lamb.

The New Testament is signified by the sun, the Old Testament by the moon. Without these two lights, the Church cannot walk the path of righteousness through the darkness of this age without frequent stumbling. But in that blessedness, where there will be no darkness, no stumbling, and nothing amiss that can be corrected, the light of the New and Old Testaments will not be needed; for the saints will enjoy forever the light of him who is the source of light, who enlightens every person coming into this world (Jn. 1:9).

Revelation 21:24 And the nations will walk by its light, and the kings of the earth will bring their glory and honor into it.

By the nations are meant all the elect. But how will they walk? They will walk in mind, not in body: not by changing their place, but by contemplating omnipotence, divinity, and majesty. They will walk by the light of the Church, because, to complete this journey, they will be enlightened by that light which almighty God will give to the whole Church. By the kings of the earth are meant the apostles and the other preachers. He calls those who believed through the preachers their glory and honor, as the apostle Paul says to his disciples: “For you are our glory in the Lord” (cf. 1 Thess. 2:20). They will therefore bring their glory and honor into it when they present to the Lord those who believed through them.

Revelation 21:25 And its gates shall not be shut by day, for there will be no night there.

Above, we said that the twelve apostles were represented by the twelve gates said to have been made of twelve pearls. But here, where the gates are mentioned without number, they have another meaning: they signify the hearts of the faithful. For the faithful, while living in this life, endure night, that is, demons and wicked people. They shut the gates at night when, against every temptation of demons, they lock the entrances to their minds and drive such temptations away. They also shut the gates at night when they fortify their hearts against the malice of wicked people, so that they do not listen to a slanderous tongue, repay evil for evil, or curse for curse; and, as the prophet says, “They shall shake their hands free of every bribe, stop their ears so they do not hear of bloodshed, and close their eyes so they do not see evil” (Isa. 33:15). But in that blessed state, where no demon or wicked one can exist to urge evil, those represented by night, the guarding of the gates, that is, of the minds, will not be necessary. For, literally, it will also be unending day there, with no night to follow.

Revelation 21:26 And they will bring the glory of the nations and their honor into it.

Above, he says that the kings of the earth, that is, the holy preachers, brought their glory into the holy city; by that glory, as we have said, the nations who believed through them were signified. If, then, the glory of the preachers is the nations, what is the nations’ glory? Perhaps we should understand this according to Solomon, who says: “The glory of the aged is their children’s children, and the glory of children is their fathers” (Prov. 17:6). For the glory of the aged, that is, the apostles, is their children’s children: the nations who believed through them and through their children, that is, their disciples. The fathers of those children will be their glory, because the nations will rejoice to have fathers of such value and excellence, since they have been deemed worthy to share in their glory. As for the words “they will bring,” we can refer them to the angels, who on the Day of Judgment will lead God’s saints to receive the promised rewards, as the Lord says in the Gospel: “The Son of Man will send his angels, and they will gather his elect from the four winds of heaven, from one end of heaven to the other” (Matt. 24:31).

Revelation 21:27 Nothing defiled shall enter it, nor anyone who practices abomination and falsehood, but only those who are written in the Lamb’s Book of Life.

He calls defiled those who have defiled themselves with various crimes; those who do evil, however, practice abomination. For God hates nothing except wickedness, as it is written in the Book of Wisdom: “You have mercy on all, O Lord, and hate none of the things you have made” (Wisdom 11:25). But one may ask why it is said that falsehood is practiced. What we do is no longer falsehood, because what has been done cannot remain undone so as to be falsehood. But he uses “done” in the sense of “spoken”; for we commit the sin of falsehood by speaking, not by acting. We may also understand every sin by falsehood, for we know that God is truth, whereas sin is the deception of souls. Whatever, then, is contrary to truth and refers to the deception of souls, even if it seems true, may rightly be called falsehood. Therefore, in that holy city nothing defiled shall enter, nor anyone who practices abomination and falsehood; for on the day of judgment the angels will go out, separate the wicked from among the righteous, and cast them into the blazing furnace (Matt. 13:49). For the door of the heavenly kingdom will also be shut to those who have not had the oil of love in their vessels, and the Lord will say to them: “Truly I tell you, I do not know you” (Matt. 25:12).

Chapter 22

Revelation 22:1-2a And he showed me the river of the water of life, bright as crystal, flowing from the throne of God and of the Lamb, through the middle of its street.

In his final vision, Ezekiel was shown a stream that issued from the temple and flowed into the sea. He speaks of it in these words: “The angel led me a thousand cubits, and brought me through the water up to my ankles. He led me a thousand cubits, and brought me through the water up to my knees. He led me a thousand cubits, and brought me through the water up to my loins. He led me a thousand cubits, and I could not cross the stream, because its waters had risen; the stream could not be crossed” (Ezek. 47:3–5). By the stream we should understand divine Scripture, which flows out of the temple, that is, the Church, from the mouths of preachers. The angel signifies the Lord, while the prophet represents those who wish to study the divine Scriptures so that they may themselves advance in them and instruct others through their knowledge. The number one thousand often signifies the perfection of something. The first crossing refers to understanding the historical sense, the second to the moral sense, and the third to the spiritual sense.

Then the angel leads the prophet a thousand cubits when the Lord grants one of his faithful the desire to understand the Scriptures. He leads him through the water up to his ankles when he grants him understanding of the historical sense. For whoever wishes to investigate the moral and spiritual understanding of the divine Scriptures must first possess an understanding of their historical sense. Second, the angel leads the prophet a thousand cubits when the Lord kindles in his faithful servant a desire to grasp the moral sense. He leads him through the water up to his knees when he reveals to him the meaning of moral knowledge. Third, the angel leads the prophet a thousand cubits when, after moral understanding, the Lord inspires his faithful servant with a desire to unfold the meanings of spiritual understanding. He leads him through the water up to his loins when he opens to him the meaning of spiritual knowledge. Fourth, the angel leads the prophet a thousand cubits when, after understanding the Scriptures, the Lord raises his faithful servant to contemplate divine and heavenly mysteries. But he could not cross the stream, because no one can fathom the depth of divine and heavenly mysteries as they truly are. “For we know in part, and we prophesy in part,” as the Apostle says; “but when that which is perfect is come, then that which is in part shall be done away” (1 Cor. 13:9–10). And again: “Now we see through a glass, darkly; but then face to face” (1 Cor. 13:12). Considering that he himself could not cross this stream, Paul said: “O the depth of the riches both of the wisdom and knowledge of God! How unsearchable are his judgments, and his ways past finding out! For who has known the mind of the Lord? or who has been his counsellor? Or who has first given to him, and it shall be recompensed unto him again? For of him, and through him, and to him, are all things: to whom be glory forever. Amen” (Rom. 11:33–36). And the prophet Isaiah says: “Eye has not seen, O God, beside you, what you have prepared for them that wait for you” (Isa. 64:4). Behold, the angel led the prophet to the appointed place, but did not lead him across, because almighty God brings his saints as far as contemplating the secret and hidden mysteries of his divinity, but does not lead them across; for human frailty cannot fully comprehend those unfathomable divine mysteries. And just as one who has smelled royal foods and tasted their sweetness beforehand desires them more than one who has neither seen nor smelled them nor learned by tasting what their sweetness is like, so the saints, who often taste through the grace of contemplation the sweetness of the heavenly homeland, burn each day with far greater love for it than those who, because of the sweetness of this world, do not perceive the sweetness of eternal life.

The same prophet says in what follows that he saw trees on both banks of the stream, whose fruit was for food and whose leaves were for healing. The two banks signify the Two Testaments. Between them flows the great stream of Scripture, set forth by the holy Fathers. The trees represent the teachers of the Church, said to be planted on both banks, that is, in the teaching of the Old and New Testaments. Drawing waters of true knowledge, they produce both fruit and leaves: the fruit signifies good works, and the leaves, words of preaching. The fruit serves as food and the leaves as medicine, because a teacher gives the ailing soul the healing medicine of words only after first nourishing it with examples of good works.

Ezekiel says that he saw a stream flowing out of the temple; (Ezek. 47:5) John, however, says that he was shown not a stream, but a river flowing from the throne of God and the Lamb. A stream is usually dried up by the sun’s intense heat, but a river is not dried up by any heat. So it is fitting that divine Scripture is represented by a stream, for it will dry up when the Sun comes, that is, when the Lord comes to judge, because after the resurrection divine Scripture will no longer be needed. The river, which is not dried up by the sun’s heat, signifies the everlasting glory of the saints. It is said to flow from the throne of God and the Lamb because the river of the saints’ glory will flow from the one from whom, while they were still in the flesh, flowed the stream of every good thing.

It is said to be a splendid river, like crystal. But since this stone is mentioned often in this book, we have decided to say a little about its nature and meaning. This stone is brighter than all other stones. It is born in the coldest places, where the cold never departs. Because it clings beneath to a somewhat softer rock, it sets its root in it a little, then rises as it grows, with six angles, tapering to a point at the top, while keeping the same arrangement of six angles all the way to the summit. It signifies every perfect man. The rock to which it clings represents Christ; the brightness of the crystal, the purity of the minds of the saints; and its six angles, the perfection of good works. It carries the arrangement of six angles up to the summit because the saints strive to bring the perfection of their good works to the end of life. At the top it tapers to a point because all the saints’ purpose is directed toward him who is one, and in whom they too become one.

Therefore, this river, which represents the glory of the saints, is said to have the brilliance of crystal, because it shines upon each of the faithful according to the quality of their deeds, as the Apostle says: “For star differs from star in brightness; so it is with the resurrection of the dead” (1 Cor. 15:41). It is said to flow from the throne of God and the Lamb, because all the glory of the saints will proceed from him, who is the author of all good things. It is also said to flow through the middle of the city’s street, because it will cheer the hearts of the saints with the continuous sweetness of delight.

Revelation 22:2b And on either side of the river was the tree of life.

Ezekiel was shown many trees on either bank of the stream, as we have discussed above; (Ezek. 47:7) but here he says that the tree of life occupied both sides of the river. There, the many trees represent the many preachers; here, the one tree of life signifies the one Christ, who is the life of all the faithful. By the two sides of the river, we can understand angels and human beings. Therefore the tree of life will occupy both sides of the river, because Christ will possess holy angels and human beings alike.

Revelation 22:2c Bearing twelve fruits.

The twelve fruits are the apostles and all who followed their teaching. The tree of life will bear twelve fruits because, in that blessedness, Christ will gather the whole multitude of the faithful.

Revelation 22:2d Yielding its fruit each month.

The months are the ages from the beginning of the world to its end, about which we have often spoken. So it will yield its fruit each month, because in that blessedness it will present to the Father all the elect who lived in each age and those who will live until the end.

Revelation 22:2e And the leaves of the tree are for the healing of the nations.

The leaves of the tree are the words of the Gospel, which have brought healing to many nations. The meaning is this: he will establish in heavenly blessedness all the nations whose souls were saved from their afflictions through the teaching of the Gospel.

Revelation 22:3a And there shall be no more curse.

By saying “no more curse,” he shows that there are many kinds of curses. The first man experienced the curse of death when he sinned, and from him it passed to all people (Gen. 3:17). And Paul says of Christ that he became a curse for us (Rom. 5:11), because for our salvation he accepted death, which is the curse of the first man (Gal. 3:13). Cain, too, was cursed by God for the guilt of murder (Gen. 4:11). And Moses says to those who despise the Law: “Cursed is whoever does not abide by the words of this book” (Deut. 27:26). Likewise, when we sin grievously, we ourselves condemn ourselves with the worst curse. Therefore, there will be no more curse, because there will be no sin, nor anything that might provide an occasion for sinning. The curse of the first man will also be erased at the resurrection, when immortality is granted to all the elect.

Revelation 22:3b And the throne of God and of the Lamb will be there.

If God’s holy ones are now thrones of God in this life, how much more will they be then, when this perishable nature has put on imperishability, and this mortal nature has put on immortality? (1 Cor. 15:53)

Revelation 22:3c And his servants will serve him.

What is this service by which God’s servants will serve God? Namely, loving him and praising him without end.

Revelation 22:4 And they will see his face, and his name will be on their foreheads.

The human mind cannot comprehend the greatness of the glory and delight that will be bestowed upon the saints, as the apostle Paul says: “What no eye has seen, nor ear heard, nor the human heart conceived, God has prepared for those who love him” (1 Cor. 2:9). But all sweetness and delight will be surpassed by this: they will see God face to face. The Son of God is the name of God; therefore, his name will be on the foreheads of the saints, because their very radiance and appearance will show that they are children of God.

Revelation 22:5a And night will be no more.

John says elsewhere: “God is light, and there is no darkness in him at all” (1 Jn. 1:5). What darkness, then, could those endure, when God will be seen continually? But if we choose to understand this saying as referring to demons and the wicked, there will be no night, because demons, together with all the wicked, will be imprisoned forever in the sewer of hell [inferni].

Revelation 22:5b And they will have no need of the light of a lamp, nor of the light of the sun.

We said above that the light of the moon and the light of the sun signify the Old and New Testaments; therefore, the lamp in this passage has the same meaning as the moon, as we said above. We have already said something above about the light of the sun and the moon, that is, the Old and New Testaments. The saints, then, will have no need of this light,

Revelation 22:5c because the Lord God will illumine them, and they will reign forever and ever.

Thus far the seventh vision. What follows concerns the admonition of John himself and of the other faithful ones.

Revelation 22:6a And he said to me: “These words are most faithful and true.”

He repeats this twice so that no one, considering the obscurities of the wording and things that seem irrational when taken literally, may become unbelieving. For there were some who, not understanding the divine mysteries contained in this book, wished to remove it from the canon of divine Scripture23.

Revelation 22:6b And the Lord God of the spirits of the prophets sent his angel to show his servants what must soon take place.

That is, the Lord God who gave the spirit of prophecy to all the holy prophets himself sent his angel, that is, the one speaking here, who also showed John all the mysteries contained in this book, to show his servants what must soon take place. Since this Scripture concerns not only the future but also the past, why does it say that it was sent by God solely to reveal what must soon take place? Because, of course, the past is easily recognized; but what is future and unknown to human beings, we can understand only through the revelation of the Holy Spirit or through the ministry of angels. Otherwise, whatever we do not know is future for us, because it is yet to come into our hearts: namely, what God predestined us to know. For knowledge of evil was not predestined for us; rather, it descends from original sin.

Revelation 22:7a And behold, I come quickly.

He speaks here in the person of Christ, signifying that he will soon come to judgment.

Revelation 22:7b Blessed is he who keeps the words of the prophecy of this book.

He keeps the words of the prophecy, that is, God’s commandments, who fulfills them by putting them into practice and perseveres in meditating on them, fulfilling the Psalmist’s saying: “But his delight is in the law of the Lord, and on his law he meditates day and night” (Ps. 1:2).

Revelation 22:8-9 And I, John, who heard and saw these things, after hearing and seeing them, fell down to worship at the feet of the angel who showed them to me. But he said to me, “See that you do not do this! I am your fellow servant, and the fellow servant of your brothers the prophets, and of those who keep the words of this book. Worship God.”

Blessed Gregory24 sufficiently explained why the angel did not wish to be worshiped by John. But we must ask why John repeated what he had previously been forbidden to do. If we carefully examine the text of this book, we will see that it did not happen without reason. For when the angel showed him Christ seated upon his throne, the four living creatures, and the twenty-four elders, (chapters 4 and 5) John is not said to have wished to worship the angel. Likewise, when the angel showed him how the Lamb opened the seven seals, (Rev. 6:1-17) and the things that took place at the sounding of the seven angels’ trumpets, (Rev. 8–11) as well as those said to have taken place when the seven angels poured out their bowls, (chapter 16) John is not said to have wished to worship the angel. But when the vision reached the passage in which the angel showed him how Christ’s bride, that is, the Church, was joined to Christ, namely, where it is written, “Let us rejoice and be glad, and give glory to him, for the marriage of the Lamb has come, and his wife has made herself ready; and it was granted to her to clothe herself in bright, pure linen.” (Rev. 19:7–8) For, it says, “the fine linen is the righteous deeds of the saints” (Rev. 19:8) then John immediately wished to worship the angel. Likewise, in what follows, when the angel showed him the downfall of the Antichrist, the binding of the Devil in the abyss, and the glory of the resurrection, (Rev. 19-20) John in no way wished to worship the angel. But when, at the end of this vision, he was shown how the Church, after the glory of the resurrection, would be joined to Christ and reign with him forever, he immediately wished to worship the angel. For the Church is joined to Christ every day through faith and the other good works that are represented by the fine linen. After the resurrection, however, she will be joined to him far more fully and gloriously: no longer in part, as now, but all together, to dwell with him forever and reign without end. Therefore John wished to worship the angel twice because the angel showed him the Church’s two unions with Christ.

Revelation 22:10 And he said to me: “Do not seal the words of the prophecy of this book, for the time is near.”

Scripture is sealed to those who do not understand it. John is therefore commanded not to seal the words of the prophecy of this book, that is, not to conceal its obscurities by keeping silent about them, but rather to make them clear by explaining them to all who wish to know, because the day of judgment, which is described in this book in various ways, is near, and we must see with our hearts the danger that threatens, lest it overtake and destroy us off guard, like a thief. But why did John not wish to make the book’s obscurities clear by explaining and describing them, in accordance with the angel’s command, especially since, because of those very obscurities, some fell into error and heresy, saying that after the resurrection Christ would reign bodily in the world for a thousand years with all the saints? (Rev. 20:4-6) Yet what we believe he did not do in writing, we believe he did in preaching.

Revelation 22:11 Let the one who does wrong continue to do wrong; let the one who is filthy continue to be filthy; and let the holy one continue to be made holy.

The one who says, “Let the one who does wrong continue to do wrong, and let the one who is filthy continue to be filthy,” said this not as a command but as a threat. People commonly use this kind of expression to frighten those under their authority, as when teachers say to lazy pupils, “Do not read, do not read”; but the pupils, understanding that this is a threat and not a ruling out, immediately begin to read. In the same way, masters commonly frighten servants who are sluggish with idleness so that they will complete the work assigned to them more quickly.

Revelation 22:12 Behold, I am coming soon, and my reward is with me, to repay each person according to their deeds.

People of this world keep the rewards they owe their servants in purses, storehouses, or certain places on earth; but Christ always has the rewards of his servants with him. For all the goodness of the saints now proceeds from him, and all their future glory will proceed from him as well.

Revelation 22:13-14 I am the Alpha and the Omega, the first and the last, the beginning and the end. Blessed are those who wash their robes, so that they may have authority over the tree of life and enter the city through its gates.

By robes in this passage we can understand our souls. Since we sin constantly, we must continually wash our robes, that is, our souls, through prayer, watchfulness, fasting, and giving alms. By the tree of life we can understand wisdom, as Solomon says: “She is a tree of life to those who take hold of her; blessed are those who hold fast to her” (Prov. 3:18). The saints have authority over the tree of life, that is, wisdom, because whoever possesses wisdom has power to uproot every vice, overcome the temptations of demons, and perform every good work, as Solomon says: “Better is one who is patient than a warrior, and one who rules his spirit than one who conquers a city” (Prov. 16:32). Such people, then, enter the city through the gates, namely through that gate which says in the Gospel: “I am the door. If anyone enters through me, he will be saved, and will come in and go out and find pasture” (Jn. 10:9). We can also refer this to the future: for, as we have already said many times, Christ is signified by the tree of life. Just as many branches grow from a single tree trunk, so too a multitude of saints comes from one Christ. Therefore, all who do not cease to cleanse their souls with spiritual washings, that is, with good works, will have authority over the tree of life, because, having become his members, they will be able to do great things through him. For just as all the strength of a tree’s branches comes from a single trunk, so all the power of the saints comes from one Christ. By the gate of the city, however, we can understand the mercy and compassion of God. This is the gate that will be opened to the righteous and shut to the unrighteous, because divine mercy will welcome the righteous into the halls of paradise, while the wicked will be excluded from that mercy. The foolish virgins will be turned away from this gate, because they will not have the oil of love in their vessels (Matt. 25:12). Therefore it continues:

Revelation 22:15 Outside are the dogs, and the sorcerers, and the sexually immoral, and the murderers, and those who serve idols, and everyone who loves and practices falsehood.

By “dogs” we may understand false preachers, of whom the apostle Paul says: “Beware of dogs, beware of evil workers, beware of the mutilation” (Philip. 3:2); and the prophet: “Mute dogs, unable to bark” (Isa. 56:10); and again: “The dogs are greedy and never have enough; they are shepherds who have no understanding” (Isa. 56:11). We have spoken above about the other vices contained here. Therefore, all who have been defiled by these vices will be excluded from that blessedness and condemned to eternal punishment.

Revelation 22:16 I, Jesus, sent my angel to testify these things to you in the churches. I am the root and offspring of David, the bright morning star.

David is the root of his entire lineage, because just as many branches spring from one root, so too a great line descended from David. Since, then, David was the root of Christ, in that Christ sprang from his lineage, how can Christ say that he is David’s root? Christ, therefore, is both the root and the son of David. He is David’s root because David himself was created by him, just like other human beings; he is David’s son because he came from David’s descendants according to the flesh. He also calls himself the morning star. The morning star, which we call the day star, is rightly said to be Christ: just as it rises at dawn and spreads its brightness throughout the world, so Christ, coming into the world, illuminated the whole face of the earth. He is rightly called the day star because, when he came into the world, he brought light with him, by which he extinguished the darkness of this world. Job speaks of this day star, saying: “Who brings forth the day star in its appointed time” (Job 38:32). God the Father brought forth the day star at its appointed time when he sent his Son, at the time he had ordained, to redeem humankind.

Revelation 22:17a Both the bridegroom and the bride say, “Come.”

The bridegroom and the bride, that is, Christ and the Church, daily invite all people through the Scriptures and through preachers to receive everlasting rewards:

Revelation 22:17b Let the one who hears say, “Come.”

Those who hear are not only the ones who understand the divine Scriptures, but also those who know the good things by which they can be saved. Many, however, hear, that is, have learned the divine commandments, but do not come, because they neglect to fulfill them in deed. Therefore, whoever hears must come; and whoever has already begun to come to God by way of good works must also draw others along by admonishing them.

Revelation 22:17c And let the one who thirsts come;

That is, let the one who thirsts for eternal life recognize that they must come to it by way of good works.

Revelation 22:17d Let whoever wishes take the water of life freely.

This was explained above (in chapter 12).

Revelation 22:18-19 A warning to everyone who hears the words of the prophecy of this book: If anyone adds anything to them, may God add to that person the plagues written in this book. And if anyone takes anything away from the words of the prophecy of this book, may God take away that person’s share in the book of life, and in the holy city, and in the things written in this book.

Since the angel earlier commands John, saying, “Do not seal up the words of the prophecy of this book,” (Rev. 22:10) why does John himself, in this passage, condemn with a dreadful curse those who add anything to the text of this book, especially since many things in it may seem, when taken literally, useless and empty unless spiritual understanding is applied to them, so that they may be understood? We must understand, however, that this was said not of interpreters but of heretics. For an interpreter neither adds nor takes away; rather, by expounding, he clarifies historical obscurities or demonstrates a moral or spiritual meaning. John therefore curses heretics who added falsehoods to the divine Scriptures to confirm their heresy, and removed things that seemed contrary to it.

Revelation 22:20 He who bears witness to these things says, “Yes, I am coming soon. Amen.” Come, Lord Jesus.

Christ says, “I am coming soon”; John pleads, “Come, Lord Jesus.” We too ought to ask for Christ’s coming in many ways. Indeed, we ask for his coming in the Lord’s Prayer, saying daily, “Your kingdom come” (Matt. 6:10), that is, the time that will follow the resurrection, when all the saints will reign with Christ forever. We lie in sin; we must beg his coming through repentance, weeping, prayer, abstinence, and generous almsgiving, so that he may return and show us his mercy, since he had departed in anger at our evils. We are set in the darkness of ignorance; we must beg the Lord to come and enlighten our hearts with the light of his wisdom. We are attacked by invisible enemies; we must ask the Lord to come and defend us from them by his invisible power. We suffer at the hands of visible enemies; we must beseech the Lord to come and grant us patience and endurance in adversity, so that we are not overcome by evil, but overcome evil with good. (Rom. 12:21) There are other enemies within us, far worse, against whom we are constantly at war: greed, gluttony, lust, envy, anger, hatred, pride, vainglory, arrogance, contempt for our neighbors, blasphemies, and many others too numerous to list. These are the worst enemies: they arise from within us, dwell with us, and continually fight against us. They also have countless arrows of evil thoughts with which they constantly wound us. If, therefore, our inner enemies were absent, our outward enemies could do us no harm. For these are the traitors within our city, and outward enemies can never capture the city of our hearts except through them. Let us therefore ask the Lord to come and utterly destroy these most brutal enemies within us, for unless they are destroyed, we cannot be saved.

Revelation 22:21 May the grace of our Lord Jesus Christ be with you all. Amen.

May the grace of the Lord Jesus Christ also be with us, and may he give us the weapons of the virtues, so that, armed with them and with him as our leader and standard-bearer, we may be defended against both inward and outward enemies, and may be deemed worthy to share in eternal life. He lives and reigns with the Father and the Holy Spirit for ever and ever. Amen.

The Author’s Admonition

Whoever wishes to know the author’s name should attend to the first letters of the explanations in the headings of the seven Visions. The number formed by the four missing vowels, if you supply the Greek ones, is eighty-one. Behold, I have written this book as briefly as I could. I beseech you, however, you to whom God has given knowledge of letters, and into whose hands this book has come to be read: do not reject the words of this book because of their foul and utterly rustic expression. Rather, imitate the man who found a jewel in a dung heap and washed it for a long time in clear water until it regained its former brightness. In the same way, wash away the stains of my ignorance with the water of your wisdom, and adorn the words of this book, which are necessary, with your own beautiful and well-ordered language, so that proud readers may no longer reject the book’s usefulness on account of its rusticity. For I know that many, puffed up with learning, will despise and mock this unpolished composition of mine. They will also be offended with me, asking why I, an unlearned man who knows nothing, presumed to forge a volume of exposition on a book of such great obscurity. To them I reply that just as the sprinkling of manure benefits barren soil more than any precious metal, so divine Scripture, expressed in ordinary language, benefits unlearned people more than if it were delivered in philosophical or poetic language. And let them know that our Lord Jesus Christ first entrusted the mysteries of his teaching not to philosophers, but to fishermen, so that through fishermen it might reach philosophers. Indeed, he who gave the donkey sensible words to correct the prophet’s folly (Num. 22:30) agreed to grant me, unworthy and the lowliest of all men, some understanding of this book. As I pondered Solomon’s saying, “Wisdom hidden and a treasure unseen, what profit is there in either?” (Sirach 20:32), and at the same time considered the grave danger threatening me if I hid in silence the very small treasure that almighty God had consented to bestow on me, I also considered that I was not fit to teach what I understood, since no one could be found who, like a disciple, would agree to hear the words of my foolishness. I therefore resolved to set down, in whatever style I could, the little knowledge that divine mercy had granted me, lest, by hiding my Lord’s talent, I be condemned along with that servant who went away, dug in the ground, and hid his master’s money (Matt. 25:18). And because I am not fit to multiply my Lord’s money, I entrust it to the money changers, so that when he comes, he may receive what is his, with interest. I call you, to whom I have begun to speak, a money changer, because just as a money changer beautifies unminted money with his own inscriptions, making it fit to yield profit, so you too can beautify with your wisdom and well-ordered words this money of the Lord that I entrust to you, making it fit to produce profits for our Lord Jesus Christ. Know that you will receive the best reward from him if, through your diligence and learning, this book, which, I believe, is necessary for the Church of God, loses the blemishes of its wording and, decorated with your fitting words, becomes more useful for the edification of those who read or hear it. I do not, however, permit the meaning of this book to be changed into other meanings, unless, God forbid, something contrary to the truth is found in it. I decree that such a thing must be wholly deleted, or at least changed for the better.

1 Ambrose Autpert and his defining 10-volume work survive in two complementary late 9th-century manuscripts preserved at the Abbey of St. Gall in Switzerland: Cod. Sang. 244: Transcribed at St. Gall from a lost 9th-century Reichenau manuscript, this contains Books 1 to 5. Cod. Sang. 245: A sister manuscript from the same period containing Books 6 to 10.
2 Manuscript MS. Bodl. 352, at Bodleian Libraries, University of Oxford.
3 Haimo of Auxerre, De varietate librorum 3.4, PL 118.935B
4 Haimo of Auxerre, Expositio in epistolas Pauli (on 1 Cor. 3:15), PL 117.526D–527A (printed under Haymo of Halberstadt).
5 Haimo of Auxerre, Expositio in Apocalypsin, PL 117.1104B (the names are at 1102C–1103B)
6 Haimo of Auxerre, Expositio in Apocalypsin, PL 117.1080D–1081A
7 The interpretations of the names come from Jerome.
8 "Judaei confitentes interpretantur" translates from Latin as "Jews are interpreted as 'confessing'". This phrase reflects a traditional etymological interpretation commonly found in medieval Christian biblical commentaries, which connects the name "Judah" (and by extension, "Jews") to its Hebrew root meaning "praise" or "confession".
9 In the ancient world, particularly among the Greeks and Romans, arithmetical calculations were frequently carried out using small stones called calculi (from which our modern word “calculation” comes). These stones were arranged on an abacus or a counting board.
10 Berengaudus follows the arrangement of Jerome from his commentary on Matthew and Jerome’s recension of the commentary on Revelation by Victorinus. Irenaeus has Matthew as the man, Mark as the Eagle, Luke as the calf, and John as the lion. Augustine has Matthew as the lion, Mark as the man, Luke as the calf, and John as the eagle.
11 This phrasing directly references Matthew 20:20–23, where the mother of James and John (the "Sons of Thunder") asked Jesus for positions of honor for them in His kingdom (cf. Mark 10:35–40, where the brothers ask themselves). Jesus responded by asking them: "Can you drink the cup I am going to drink?" They confidently answered, "We can." Jesus then prophesied: "You will indeed drink from my cup..."
12 Berengaudus explains that human development is traditionally categorized into five distinct stages: infancy, childhood, adolescence, youth, and old age. However, to align the lifespan of an individual with the grand timeline of cosmic history, Saint Gregory the Great split old age into two sub-stages to create a six-part system. Nineteenth Homily on the Gospels (Homilies on the Gospel, Homily 19)
13 The late Roman Christian poet Aurelius Prudentius Clemens (born 348 CE), and his most influential masterpiece, the Psychomachia (literally, "The Battle of the Soul").
14 In the Refutation of All Heresies (6.20; ch. 15 in the ANF translation) attributed to Hippolytus of Rome, as well as early apocryphal accounts, Simon found his followers declining and his reputation damaged by Peter. To regain his authority and "prove" his divine nature, Simon Magus promised his disciples that if they buried him alive in a grave, he would rise again on the third day.
15 Jerome, Commentary on Daniel, chapter 7
16 Revelation 17:15 with the Scripture verse and comments are actually out of the proper order of the Scripture text. Berengaudus, or a later editor placed it after Revelation 17:17.
17 The verse and explanation for verse 16 in the commentary is placed by Berengaudus or a later editor before verse 15. I put them into their correct order according to the order found in the Scripture text.
18 There appears to be either a misquotation of 15c or perhaps a variant reading. The part that says, “And they shall be broken like a potter’s vessel,” matches Revelation 2:27 (cf. Psalm 2:9).
19 Augustine of Hippo, City of God 20.7.
20 Augustine, City of God 20.18, discusses the destruction and renewal of the world by fire (2 Pet. 3); he does not, however, give the “purifying rain” reading of Psalm 49:3 (Vulgate; Heb. 50:3) that Berengaudus rejects here.
21 The Latin word for sorcerer is Venefici, the Greek word is φαρμάκοις, which is the etymological root for pharmacy and pharmacist.
22 Gregory the Great, Homilies on the Gospels 9.5
23 Several prominent Early Church Fathers and eastern councils hesitated or refused to include the Book of Revelation in the New Testament canon, primarily due to concerns over its obscure apocalyptic imagery, disputed authorship, and the risk of heretical or over-speculative abuse. Some of them were Eusebius of Caesarea, Cyril of Jerusalem, John Chrysostom, Theodore of Mopsuestia, Theodoret of Cyrus, etc.
24 Gregory the Great, Homilies on the Gospels 8.2.
